It took an hour of careful searching, routing each query through different network paths to avoid triggering monitoring systems. But she found it: payments from Meridian to a law firm called Benbridge & Associates. Legal consultation fees, according to the transaction descriptions.

She researched Benbridge & Associates. Small firm, three partners, specializing in commercial contracts and regulatory compliance. Their client list was private, but their scheduling system used standard encryption—secure enough for routine business, but vulnerable to someone with Nicole's expertise and tools.

She cracked the encryption in forty minutes, accessing appointment records and billing data. Benbridge & Associates had one client that accounted for forty percent of their billable hours: Directorate Office of Commercial Oversight.

Nicole felt her pulse quicken. The Directorate didn't hire mercenaries to sabotage civilian vessels. They commanded enforcement divisions, official channels for handling problems. If the Directorate wanted a ship stopped, they'd impound it through legal authority.

Unless they couldn't. Unless whatever was happening with the Avalon required unofficial action.

She thought about the ship's crew manifest she'd reviewed before accepting the job. Standard complement—captain, pilot, engineer, systems analyst, cargo handler. Nothing unusual except one passenger: Dr. Elise Chen, listed as a private consultant with no specific affiliation.

Nicole had researched Dr. Chen before accepting the contract—professional thoroughness, ensuring there were no complications. The public data had been sparse: academic credentials from a mid-tier university, published papers on quantum field theory, employment history showing work for various research institutions.

But there had been gaps. Three years where Dr. Chen's employment history went blank. No publications, no conference appearances, nothing beyond basic living expenses.

Three years was a long time to vanish from the professional world.

Nicole began a new search, this time focusing on Directorate research programs active during Dr. Chen's gap years. Most details were classified, but budget allocations were public record—the Directorate was required to report certain financial information to the central governance council, even if project specifics remained secret.

She filtered for programs involving quantum mechanics, cross-referenced with budget allocations showing unusual funding levels. One program kept surfacing: something called the Resonance Initiative. The details were heavily redacted, but the budget documents revealed massive resource allocation over a four-year period, followed by abrupt funding cessation eighteen months ago.

Project terminated, according to the official status. No explanation.

A terminated project and a researcher with gap years. A researcher now traveling to the outer sectors aboard a freight vessel, away from Directorate oversight. And someone had paid Nicole to ensure that vessel couldn't depart on schedule.

She was beginning to grasp the shape of what was happening, even if the details remained obscured.

Nicole's terminal chimed—a coded message from one of her monitoring systems. Someone was conducting a systematic sweep of Bastion's commercial sector, checking security footage and access logs with the thoroughness of professional hunters. The search pattern suggested they were working backward from the public records terminal she'd accessed earlier.

They'd find nothing useful there—she'd been careful about covering her tracks. But the fact that they were searching meant she'd triggered something significant enough to warrant active pursuit.

She needed to move, to establish distance between herself and anyone hunting her. But she also needed to decide what to do with what she'd learned. The professional choice was simple: delete everything, evacuate the station immediately, and let whatever was happening with the Avalon play out without her involvement.

But Nicole had spent her career maintaining certain principles. She didn't work for causes or ideals—she worked for payment and the satisfaction of a job executed flawlessly. But she also didn't enjoy being used as a pawn in games she didn't understand. And if the Directorate was involved, if someone had hired her as part of a larger operation without explaining the full context, that suggested she'd been positioned as a convenient scapegoat.

People who ended up as scapegoats tended to have short lifespans once their usefulness expired.

Nicole extracted her personal communication device—heavily encrypted, registered under a false identity she kept for emergencies. She composed a message, choosing her words carefully.

"To Marcus Cole, Engineering, Avalon. The malfunction you'll discover in the stabilizers was deliberate. External parties with significant resources are involved. Suggest complete security review. Trust no station authority. —A Friend"

She hesitated before sending it. Making contact would confirm her continued presence on the station, would give whoever was hunting her another data point to track. But she'd been aboard the Avalon for two weeks during her preparation, had worked alongside Marcus while identifying the optimal approach for the sabotage. He was thorough and observant—the kind of engineer who would notice anomalies and investigate them.

If the Directorate was involved, if whatever was really happening involved Dr. Chen and terminated research projects, Marcus and the crew were caught in something they didn't understand. And Nicole had positioned them there.

She sent the message through the routing system, rendering the source nearly impossible to trace. Then she wiped the terminal's memory, destroyed the data chips she'd been using, and prepared to disappear into the station's depths.

The job had seemed simple when she'd accepted it—sabotage and payment, a clean transaction with no complications. Now she'd stumbled into something that smelled like conspiracy, and the Directorate's shadow stretched across every piece of evidence she'd uncovered.

She remembered Marcus's face during those two weeks aboard the Avalon—the easy competence with which he handled the ship's systems, the way he'd noticed her watching him work and simply nodded, accepting her presence without suspicion. He'd trusted her because she'd presented herself as a fellow professional.

That trust was about to cost him everything if she didn't act.

Nicole moved through the service passages, her mind replaying another memory—older, from before she'd become what she was now. A conversation with a mentor who'd taught her the business of disappearing.

"The Directorate doesn't chase people," he'd said. "They erase them. If you ever see their fingerprints on a job, walk away. No payment is worth what they'll do to anyone caught in their operations."

She hadn't walked away. She'd taken the money and done the work. And now she was trapped in exactly the kind of situation he'd warned her about.

Her communicator vibrated—an incoming message through her emergency channel. She shouldn't check it. Every second she spent stationary was another second for the hunters to triangulate her position.

But the sender ID made her blood freeze.

It was the Handler. The anonymous contact who'd arranged the job in the first place.

"You've been investigating. That was unwise. But your thoroughness confirms you're the right person for what comes next. The Avalon sabotage was a test. You passed. Now there's a new assignment—one that will answer every question you've been asking. Report to Docking Bay 17 in three hours. Come alone. Bring everything you've learned about Dr. Chen."

The message self-deleted five seconds after she read it.

Nicole stood in the abandoned corridor, her pulse hammering against her ribs. A test. The entire job had been an audition, and she'd performed exactly as expected.

Which meant whoever was running this operation had been watching her far longer than three weeks. Had studied her methods, her principles, her tendency to investigate when things didn't add up. They'd designed the job specifically to trigger her curiosity, to see how deep she'd dig.

And now they wanted to recruit her for something bigger.

The smart move was still to run. To disappear into the Dead Sectors and never look back.

But Nicole had built her career on understanding the full picture before making decisions. And right now, the full picture was hidden behind a door the Handler was holding open.

Three hours.

She checked her weapons, verified her escape routes, and began moving toward Docking Bay 17.

Whatever was waiting for her there, at least she'd finally get some answers.

Chapter Twenty-Four

The Hunt Closes

The tactical display bathed Thane's face in shifting blues and whites as her task force assembled around Veridia's outer marker. Seventeen vessels—more than she'd expected, though still fewer than she'd hoped for. When she'd requisitioned twelve ships, Command had approved six. The remaining eleven had arrived anyway, volunteers drawn by the promise of finally cornering the fugitives who'd slipped through every net for the past month.

Captain Verin Thane stood at the center of her flagship's command deck, fingers interlaced behind her back, watching the formation coalesce. Each ship represented resources diverted from patrol routes, supply runs, merchant escorts. Her superiors had made their displeasure known through increasingly terse communications. The last message from Admiral Korthen had been a single line: Justify this or return to port.

She would justify it. With their capture. With their interrogation. With answers finally extracted from the woman who'd orchestrated Bastion's breach and the man who'd helped her escape.

"Captain, the Remnant's Oath is signaling ready status," Lieutenant Parl reported from communications. "That's the last of them."

Thane nodded without turning from the display. The binary stars of the Veridia system burned at the projection's center—one amber, one pale blue, locked in their eternal dance. Between them, in the narrow corridor of habitable space their competing gravities birthed, three planets clustered. Veridia Prime, the innermost, was where her intelligence suggested the fugitives had gone to ground.

"Update from surface assets?" she asked.

Parl's fingers danced across her console. "Confirmation of docking bay rental under one of their known aliases. Transaction occurred eighteen hours ago. The facility manager reported two individuals matching general descriptions, though visual confirmation was inconclusive."

"Eighteen hours." Thane's jaw tightened. Always one step behind, always arriving after they'd already established themselves, already begun whatever machinations would enable their next escape. "Deploy orbital surveillance. I want every ship that leaves that planet tracked and categorized. If they so much as charter a pleasure yacht, I want to know about it before they clear atmosphere."

"Captain." Commander Reis approached from the weapons station, his voice pitched low enough that only Thane could hear. "May I speak candidly?"

She turned to face him. Reis had served with her for three years, had earned the right to express concerns without invitation. His weathered face carried an expression she recognized—the same one he'd worn before suggesting tactical withdrawals that had saved their ship twice. Caution born of experience, not cowardice.

"Speak," she said.

"Seventeen vessels for two fugitives." His eyes held hers steadily. "Command is questioning the resource allocation. With respect, Captain, some of the task force officers are voicing similar concerns through channels."

Thane felt something cold settle in her chest. "The fugitives have demonstrated exceptional evasion capabilities across multiple systems. A robust response is warranted."

"Agreed, Captain. But there's robust response, and there's…" Reis paused, selecting his words carefully. "Permission to speak more directly?"

She studied his face. The request itself was significant—Reis asking permission to step outside normal protocols. "Granted."

"This pursuit has been unusually intensive, Captain. Eight systems, four weeks, personal intervention in operational planning that would typically be delegated." His voice remained respectful, but concern bled through. "Some officers are wondering if there are factors beyond the tactical situation driving the pursuit parameters."

"They breached Bastion Station," Thane said. The words emerged harder than she'd intended. "They compromised systems that were supposed to be impenetrable. They stole something valuable enough that someone hired an infiltration specialist to acquire it. And they've evaded every attempt at capture through means that suggest either exceptional luck or support from resources we haven't yet identified."

"Understood, Captain. The tactical briefing is clear on those points." Reis glanced around the command deck, ensuring their conversation remained private. "But that doesn't fully explain why you rejected Command's suggestion to hand operational authority to Regional Investigation. Or why you've personally coordinated this task force rather than delegating to sector command."

Thane turned back to the tactical display, watching the orbital positions shift as her ships maneuvered into formation. The silence stretched long enough that Reis shifted his weight slightly.

"You reviewed the Bastion casualty reports?" she asked quietly.

"Yes, Captain. Standard briefing materials."

"Then you saw the name Devon Thane. Junior engineer, third rotation. Engineering section, fourth deck." She kept her voice level, professional. As if discussing any casualty from any incident. "He was twenty-six. He was my brother."

The command deck's ambient sounds seemed to recede. Reis was quiet for a long moment. "I didn't know, Captain. That wasn't in your service record cross-references."

"Different surnames. He took our mother's family name when our parents divorced." Thane's fingers found the edge of the tactical display's frame. "The investigation concluded that structural failures during the breach operation caused multiple casualties. The fugitives weren't charged with those deaths—legally, they're wanted for unauthorized access, systems intrusion, and theft of classified materials. Not manslaughter."

"Captain—"

"But they were there, Reis. In the station while my brother was dying. While fires consumed three decks, while every alarm in the sector screamed." Her voice remained controlled, but something underneath it had edges. "They set events in motion. Whether the law holds them accountable for the consequences or not, those consequences exist."

Reis stood beside her, both of them staring at the display. When he finally spoke, his voice carried careful neutrality. "With respect, Captain, this is information that would typically be disclosed to Command when determining pursuit assignment. Personal connection to an investigation can be grounds for recusal."

"I'm aware of regulations regarding conflict of interest, Commander."

"Then you're also aware that pursuing suspects with personal connection to casualty lists, even if they're not charged with those casualties, creates appearance concerns." Reis paused. "Command would have assigned this to someone else if they'd known."

"Perhaps." Thane continued watching the tactical display. "Or perhaps they would have recognized that personal understanding of what was lost makes me more committed to ensuring these fugitives face justice."

"Or more committed to ensuring they face something else." Reis's tone remained respectful, but the implication landed clear. "Captain, I need to ask: are you pursuing them because they're dangerous fugitives who need to be apprehended, or because you need someone to hold accountable for your brother's death?"

The question landed like a physical blow. Thane turned to face him, something hot flaring in her chest before she controlled it. "I'm pursuing them because it's my duty. Because they've violated military security and evaded justice. Because if we let them slip away again, we're establishing that some people are simply beyond accountability."

"Understood, Captain." Reis maintained his measured tone. "However, I'm obligated to note for the record that several task force officers have expressed concerns about operational tempo and resource allocation. If those concerns reach Command through other channels, they may require formal response."

It was the closest Reis could come to warning her without crossing into insubordination. The officers were talking. Wondering. And if their concerns escalated beyond informal channels, Command would investigate. Would discover the personal connection she hadn't disclosed. Would see the pattern of decisions that looked increasingly like something other than professional duty.

"Your concerns are noted and logged, Commander," Thane said formally. "Is there anything else regarding operational readiness?"

Reis held her eyes for a moment longer, then shook his head. "No, Captain. All systems remain nominal."

"Then return to your station. Monitor the deployment and notify me when all vessels are in final position."

Reis nodded and departed without further comment. The door sealed behind him with a soft pneumatic hiss.

Alone at the tactical display, Thane studied the formation taking shape around Veridia Prime. Seventeen ships, hundreds of personnel, resources that could be protecting commercial routes or responding to actual emergencies. All committed to catching two fugitives because she'd made it personal.

Because Devon was dead, and someone needed to answer for it.

Her brother's face surfaced in her memory—not as it had been in the morgue, but from the last time they'd spoken. Three weeks before Bastion, a brief vidcom conversation where he'd complained about redundant safety drills and she'd teased him about soft station assignments. He'd been full of plans for advancing through the ranks until he could earn his own command.

Being angry doesn't make you stronger, he'd told her once, years ago after she'd gotten into a fight with another cadet. It just makes you alone.

The words had seemed naive then. Now, standing at the center of a task force that might end her career, they felt prophetic.

Thane pressed her palm against the tactical display, the interface cool beneath her fingers. On the projection, Veridia Prime rotated peacefully, unaware of the forces gathering around it. Somewhere on that rust-colored world, Nicole Varra and Silas Voss were moving through cities, believing themselves safe.

They were wrong.

"All ships reporting ready status," Lieutenant Parl announced. "Blockade formation is complete. No vessel can leave the gravity well without detection and interdiction."

Thane straightened, pulling herself back into command posture. Reis's words still echoed in her mind, but she pushed them aside. There would be time for self-examination later. After the fugitives were in custody. After she could look at Devon's name on the casualty list and know that someone had been held accountable for the chain of events that killed him.

"Status of surface teams?" she asked.

"Lieutenant Markov reports deployment complete. Four teams distributed across Veridia Prime's primary urban center. They've begun preliminary sweeps of identified locations."

"Time estimate for apprehension?"

Parl consulted her console. "Lieutenant Markov suggests three to four days for systematic coverage. Local authorities are cooperating, but civilian privacy laws are slowing search procedures."

Three to four days. Time enough for Admiral Korthen's patience to expire completely. Time enough for the fugitives to notice increased attention and implement whatever escape plan they'd prepared. Time enough for Command to discover what she hadn't disclosed and recall her in disgrace.

Time enough for everything to go wrong.

"Notify Markov that he has seventy-two hours," Thane said. "After that, Command will recall us regardless of operational status. If the fugitives are still free in three days, this entire pursuit becomes academic."

"Understood, Captain."

Thane returned her attention to the tactical display, to Veridia Prime spinning slowly beneath the light of its binary stars. Somewhere on that world, the hunt was closing. After weeks of pursuit, after systems crossed and resources exhausted, the end was approaching.

She thought of Devon again. Not dying in fire and catastrophic decompression, but alive. Standing at their family's viewport years ago, watching ships move through the orbital lanes like fireflies against the dark.

"I'm going to command one of those someday," he'd said, his voice carrying the certainty of youth. "And you're going to be the admiral who gives me orders."

She'd laughed then. Told him admirals didn't give orders to individual ships. He'd rolled his eyes with the particular exasperation only a younger sibling could muster.

"You know what I mean, Verin. We're going to matter. Both of us."

He'd been fourteen. She'd been nineteen, already accepted to the academy, already planning the career that would define her life. And she'd believed him—believed that they'd both rise through the ranks, that the family name would become synonymous with excellence and service.

Now Devon was ash scattered in the void, and she was hunting fugitives with resources she'd requisitioned through channels that would end her career if anyone looked too closely.

Being angry doesn't make you stronger. It just makes you alone.

The irony burned. She'd spent her entire career building alliances, earning loyalty, becoming the kind of officer that others followed willingly. And now she was throwing all of it away for a pursuit that had nothing to do with justice and everything to do with the hollow space where her brother used to be.

Reis was right. The officers were right. Command would be right, when they finally discovered the truth.

But she couldn't stop.

"Captain." Parl's voice cut through her reverie. "Priority transmission from surface team. Lieutenant Markov reports visual confirmation of target Nicole Varra at the eastern transit hub. She's moving toward the commercial district."

Thane's heart rate spiked. "Intercept status?"

"Teams are converging. Estimated contact in six minutes."

Six minutes. After weeks of pursuit, after systems and resources and the growing weight of questions she couldn't answer, the end was six minutes away.

"Patch me through to Markov directly," Thane ordered. "And prepare a shuttle for surface deployment. I'm going down personally."

"Captain, regulations suggest command personnel remain—"

"I gave an order, Lieutenant."

Parl's hesitation lasted only a fraction of a second. "Yes, Captain. Shuttle bay is standing by."

Thane moved toward the bridge exit, her boots striking the deck with measured precision. Behind her, the tactical display continued its patient rotation—seventeen ships holding position, hundreds of personnel executing her orders, an entire operation built on a foundation of grief she'd never acknowledged.

None of it mattered now. The hunt was ending.

And when she finally stood face to face with Nicole Varra, when she could look into the eyes of the woman who'd set the fire that killed her brother, Thane would have to decide what kind of officer she really was.

What kind of person.

The shuttle bay doors opened before her, and she walked through without looking back.

Chapter Twenty-Five

Why

The observation lounge viewport framed Veridia below, twin stars casting competing shadows across atmospheric bands—gold bleeding into crimson. Nicole stood with arms crossed, blocking the exit.

Marcus turned from the planet. She'd been silent for three minutes now, just watching him. The command deck's murmur filtered through the bulkhead, muffled voices coordinating the evacuation that Thane's blockade had forced into motion.

"You said you'd explain." Her voice carried the same flat tone from the archive room. "So explain."

He'd rehearsed this. A hundred times during long weeks hiding in transit stations and freight holds. None of those rehearsals had prepared him for the actual weight of saying it aloud.

"I found military-grade surveillance protocols on your personal system three days before Clearwater. Someone had been watching you for at least eight months."

Nicole's fingers tightened on her crossed arms. "Watching me why?"

"Because of me." Marcus pulled out a chair but didn't sit. "I'd been investigating Kastor Corporation's operations in Sector Seven for nearly a year. Requisitioning shipping manifests, cross-referencing supply chains. Small discrepancies at first. Then patterns that suggested something deeper than accounting errors."

"The binary Theron system," Nicole said.

"Among others. Veridia was the most obvious—an entire planet stripped while the population was told they were receiving investment and development. But there were six other colonies following the same pattern."

Nicole pulled out the chair he'd left untouched and sat, her movements precise. "That's not why they were watching me."

"No." Marcus finally sat. Through the viewport, one of Thane's cruisers drifted past. "I got careless. Started using my personal system to access secured databases. Someone in Kastor's security apparatus flagged my queries, traced them back, started building a profile."

"And found me," Nicole finished.

"Found us. They found our relationship. The fact that you worked in the same sector, that you'd visited some of the same installations I was investigating. They couldn't prove you were involved, but they didn't need to. The connection was enough."

"Enough for what?"

"To make you leverage. I found the surveillance two days after I intercepted an internal memo suggesting that 'alternative persuasion methods' might be necessary if I didn't abandon my inquiry. They never mentioned you by name. They didn't have to."

The observation lounge's environmental systems cycled. Nicole stood abruptly, the chair scraping against the deck. She paced to the viewport, pressed her palm against the transparent aluminum.

"You could have warned me," she said. "Could have told me to leave, to disappear—"

"Any contact would have confirmed what they suspected. That you knew about my investigation. As long as you remained ignorant, you were only potentially useful. The moment you acted on information I provided, you became a confirmed accomplice."

She didn't move, palm still flat against the glass. Marcus watched her reflection—eyes locked on the planet below, jaw tight.

"So you decided to die instead," she said.

"I decided to remove their leverage. If I was dead, there was nothing to gain from monitoring you. Your connection to me became irrelevant. A former relationship, nothing more."

"Except seventeen people actually died at Clearwater." Her voice had gone quiet. Dangerous. "I saw the reports. The explosion, the casualties. You just… what? Walked away?"

The question hit like a physical blow. Marcus stood but didn't approach her. "I was supposed to be on that inspection tour. Everyone knew my schedule. The explosion wasn't supposed to happen that day—a maintenance crew triggered a cascade failure in one of the old fuel storage chambers."

"How convenient for you."

"I was running late. Stopped to review final data about Kastor's supply shipments. By the time I arrived, the facility was already burning."

Nicole turned from the viewport. The mixed light from Theron's twin stars rendered her expression unreadable. "So you improvised."

"I saw an opportunity." The words tasted like ash. "My credentials would have placed me inside at the time of the explosion if I'd kept to schedule. The facility was completely destroyed—no possibility of recovering intact remains."

"And?" Her voice had dropped to barely above a whisper.

"The first responders were still arriving when I got there. Chaos. No one tracking who was present or absent. I accessed the external security terminal from my vehicle—the internal systems were slag, but the backup logs were intact. Changed my entry timestamp to show I'd arrived thirty minutes earlier. Then I left."

Nicole's hand fell away from the viewport. "You falsified records while people were dying."

"The emergency crews were focused on containment. No one was watching the parking perimeter, and I had administrator access to the facility network. It took maybe twenty minutes to alter the logs and purge the contradictory data from the backup servers." Marcus kept his voice level, factual. "Then I drove to the nearest transit station and disappeared."

"Did you know?" The question came sharp, sudden. "Did you know the explosion was going to happen?"

"No."

"But you didn't try to help afterward."

"No." The admission settled between them like a physical weight. "I didn't. I was too focused on exploiting the situation to cover my escape."

Nicole moved back to the table, braced her hands on its surface as if the confession had physically staggered her. For a long moment, she just stood there, head bowed. Then:

"And me? While you were busy being dead, what was I doing?"

Marcus had known this question was coming. Had prepared for it. The preparation meant nothing now. "I monitored your communications when I could. Made sure the surveillance on your systems went dormant like I'd predicted. You were… grieving."

"I was grieving." She looked up, and for the first time since entering the observation lounge, her composure cracked. "I attended your memorial service, Marcus. I spoke at it. I told stories about you to people who thought you were a hero. And you were what? Hiding in a transit station feeling proud of yourself?"

"I was trying to keep you safe—"

"You were trying to keep yourself safe!" The words came out sharp enough to cut. "Don't dress it up as protection. You found a convenient disaster and used it to solve your problems. The fact that it hurt me was just collateral damage you were willing to accept."

Marcus opened his mouth, closed it. Because she was right. He could dress it in justifications about surveillance and leverage and keeping her out of danger, but the fundamental truth was simpler: he'd chosen his investigation over her pain. Chosen the convenience of disappearance over the difficulty of warning her directly.

"You're right," he said.

Nicole blinked. Whatever response she'd been preparing for, agreement clearly wasn't it.

"You're right," Marcus repeated. "I told myself I was protecting you. But really, I was protecting my investigation. Making sure I could continue the work without the complication of you being a known associate. Your grief was collateral damage I accepted because the alternative—actually protecting you by stopping entirely—was something I wasn't willing to do."

She stared at him. "You're admitting that."

"What's the point of lying now?" Marcus gestured toward the viewport, toward Thane's blockade. "You're already involved. Already at risk. Already here in the middle of everything I tried to keep you away from. Might as well be honest about how we got here."

Nicole pushed away from the table, paced to the far wall, then back. Energy crackled through her movements now, anger finding physical expression. "You selfish, arrogant, single-minded—" She stopped herself, drew a breath. "You know what the worst part is? I probably would have helped you. If you'd just asked, if you'd trusted me with the truth, I would have helped you investigate Kastor. But instead, you decided I was too fragile or too compromised or too whatever to be trusted, and you made the choice for me."

"I know."

"Stop agreeing with me!" She whirled on him. "Stop being so calm and reasonable and accepting. Be defensive. Make excuses. Give me something to actually be angry at instead of just… this."

Marcus held her gaze. "What do you want me to say?"

"I want you to say it mattered!" The words burst out of her, raw and unfiltered. "That the month I spent grieving you actually meant something to you. That you didn't just calculate the acceptable cost and move on. That somewhere in your careful planning and strategic thinking, you actually felt guilty about what you were putting me through."

"I did." The confession came quieter than he'd intended. "Every day. Every time I accessed your communications and saw what it was doing to you. Every time I told myself it was necessary, there was a part of me that knew I was just justifying cruelty."

Nicole's anger seemed to deflate slightly. She moved back to the chair, dropped into it heavily. "And now I'm here anyway. On Veridia, investigating the same things you investigated, at risk from the same people. So it was all for nothing."

"Not nothing. You had a month of safety while I gathered more evidence. A month where they weren't actively planning to eliminate you."

"A month where I thought you were dead, Marcus. That's not safety. That's just a different kind of torture."

She wasn't wrong. Marcus pulled up the table's holographic display, called up the data he'd been compiling for the past month. Shipping manifests, population records, resource extraction timetables. "You want to know what I did while you were grieving? I documented this."

The holographic data streams coalesced into recognizable patterns. Nicole leaned forward despite herself, analytical instincts engaging.

"Seven thousand people on Veridia reported respiratory issues within three years of Kastor's arrival," Marcus said. "Five thousand of those cases resulted in chronic conditions requiring long-term pharmaceutical intervention."

"Pharmaceutical intervention Kastor provided," Nicole said, studying the data.

"Through a subsidiary. At reduced cost, marketed as corporate charity. But the medications didn't cure the conditions—they managed them. Created a permanent dependent population."

The holographic display rotated. Nicole stood, moved closer to examine the additional layers of information. Marcus watched her process it, saw the moment comprehension shifted to horror.

"This is systematic," she said. "Not just Veridia."

"All seven colonies. Same pattern, same timeline, same outcomes. And it's not accidental." He pulled up another data set. "I tracked several shipments from Veridia to an undisclosed military facility near the Theron binary's outer system. That's when I realized the health crises weren't side effects of industrialization. They were intentional. Testing population-level responses to environmental contaminants that could be weaponized."

Nicole straightened, face pale. "You're saying Kastor deliberately poisoned these populations as weapons testing."

"I'm saying the evidence strongly suggests that, yes."

She turned away from the holographic display, moved back to the viewport. Below them, Veridia's night side glittered with city lights. Thousands of people who had no idea their illnesses had been orchestrated.

"Why are you telling me this now?" she asked. "You stayed dead for a month. Let me grieve, let me think you were gone. Why surface now?"

Marcus deactivated the holographic display. "Because Thane's blockade changes everything. I thought I had time to build an airtight case, to leak the information through channels that couldn't be traced. But Thane's presence means Kastor's security apparatus has escalated. They're not just covering up evidence anymore—they're eliminating witnesses. And you're on their list now, whether I wanted it or not."

Nicole turned from the viewport. "So you came back to warn me."

"I came back because staying dead stopped being an option." Marcus moved to stand beside her, close enough to see the competing starlight reflected in her eyes. "Thane isn't just hunting us for the breach. She's working with Kastor—or at least for the same people. I intercepted communications three days ago. My 'death' didn't end the surveillance on you. It shifted it to active threat assessment."

"They were going to kill me anyway."

"Yes." The word felt like a blade in his chest. "Everything I did, all the pain I caused you—it bought time, but it didn't solve anything. You were always going to end up here, with them hunting you, because of me. Because of what I discovered."

Nicole was quiet for a long moment. The city lights below pulsed with the rhythm of ten thousand lives, none of them aware of the forces converging above their world.

"The data you showed me," she said finally. "The weapons testing, the population manipulation—is it enough? Enough to bring them down if we can get it to the right people?"

"Maybe. If we can reach independent media networks or the Senate oversight committees before Kastor's people intercept us." Marcus hesitated. "But getting off Veridia won't be easy. Thane has seventeen ships in blockade formation."

"Seventeen ships looking for two fugitives in a freighter." Nicole's voice had shifted—still angry, still hurt, but something else had entered it. Something sharper. "They're not looking for what we actually have."

Marcus frowned. "What do you mean?"

"I mean I didn't spend the last month just grieving, Marcus." She moved to the observation lounge's secondary terminal, calling up a schematic he didn't recognize. "I started investigating after the memorial service. Found some of the same threads you did. And I made contacts—people in positions to help if the evidence is solid enough."

"Contacts on Veridia?"

"Contacts everywhere." Nicole's fingers danced across the terminal. "Including one inside Thane's task force. A communications officer who's been feeding me blockade positions for the past six hours."

Marcus stared at her. "You have an asset inside the blockade."

"I have several assets in several places. Because unlike you, I don't try to do everything alone." She met his eyes, and despite everything, a ghost of her old smile flickered across her face. "You want to expose Kastor? You want to make your fake death actually mean something? Then stop treating me like someone who needs protection and start treating me like a partner."

The challenge hung between them. Marcus thought about all the justifications he'd constructed, all the careful logic that had led him to deceive her. And he thought about what she'd just revealed—that she'd been working the same problem from a different angle, building networks and gathering resources while he'd been hiding in transit stations feeling sorry for himself.

"Okay," he said. "Partners."

"Partners." Nicole nodded once, crisply. "Now here's what we're going to do. In four hours, there's going to be a shift change in the blockade's eastern quadrant. My contact has arranged a sensor gap—thirty seconds, maybe less. That's our window."

"Thirty seconds isn't enough time to clear the gravity well."

"It's enough time to launch a distress beacon with everything we have on Kastor. Even if they catch us, the data will be out there. Cascading through networks they can't control." Nicole's eyes held his. "This ends tonight, Marcus. One way or another."

Through the viewport, Thane's cruisers continued their patient patrol. Below, Veridia turned beneath the light of binary stars.

And somewhere in the space between hunter and hunted, the shape of everything was about to change.

Chapter Twenty-Six

Aftermath

Nicole stood at the viewport in the observation lounge, watching Veridia's terminator line creep across the visible hemisphere. Day surrendering to night on an ancient world. Transformations governed by the simple mechanics of orbital rotation.

Behind her, Marcus's voice had died several seconds ago. She couldn't recall his last words. Something about soldiers. Something about her father.

My father knew.

The thought arrived with crystalline clarity, sharp enough to draw blood. Admiral Trent Vaughn hadn't just known about Project Chimera—he'd authorized operations there. Dispatched soldiers into those facilities. The man who'd raised her on bedtime stories about honor and duty had walked past children in cages to retrieve data cores.

"Nicole." Marcus's voice, quieter now. Uncertain. "I'm sorry. I should have—"

"Don't." The word emerged harder than she'd intended. "Just… don't."

She felt him hesitate behind her, sensed the moment he decided to speak anyway. "Your father was following orders, same as I was. The Commonwealth leadership—"

"I need you to stop talking now." Nicole kept her gaze fixed on Veridia, tracking cloud patterns as they swirled across the darkening hemisphere. If she turned around, if she looked at his face, she would say things that couldn't be unsaid. "Please."

Silence stretched between them, filling the observation lounge with a pressure that made her ears ring. She heard Marcus draw breath, heard him abort whatever he'd been about to say. Then footsteps, moving toward the door.

The door panel chimed. Paused. Chimed again as he left.

Nicole remained at the viewport, alone with the truth that was rewriting her past with every passing second.

* * *

Her quarters felt smaller than usual. Nicole paced the narrow space between her bunk and desk, her hands trembling despite her efforts to still them. The tremor had started in the observation lounge and showed no signs of stopping.

He knew. He authorized it. He sent Marcus there.

She pressed her palms against her thighs, trying to anchor herself in physical sensation. The pressure didn't help. Nothing helped. The revelation sat in her chest like a shard of ice, melting slowly into her bloodstream.

The Edda thrummed around her, its constant vibration a reminder that the universe continued forward regardless of how thoroughly her understanding of it had collapsed. Somewhere in the ship, the crew went about their duties. Somewhere below, Veridia's ancient ruins waited with their secrets. Somewhere in the galaxy, Kael Drayton pursued his agenda with resources and conviction that terrified her.

And somewhere in her memory, her father's face smiled at her across the breakfast table, spinning stories about honor and sacrifice while the truth rotted behind his teeth.

Nicole stopped pacing, her gaze falling on the small desk bolted against the bulkhead. Her personal effects littered its surface—a data tablet, a few physical books, a holo-frame cycling through family images. She crossed to it slowly, her thumb finding the control that froze the display.

Her father stared back at her. Dress uniform, medals gleaming, hand resting on twelve-year-old Nicole's shoulder at her academy acceptance ceremony. She remembered that day with painful clarity—the weight of his hand, the scratch of her new cadet uniform, the way her chest had swelled with determination to make him proud.

"You lied to me," she whispered to the frozen image. "Every day. Every conversation. Every lesson about integrity."

Her hand closed around the frame. For a moment she just stood there, gripping it hard enough that the edges bit into her palm. Then she threw it.

Plastic shattered against the bulkhead. Components scattered across the deck plating. The display flickered once, then died.

The anger drained as quickly as it had come, leaving behind something worse—a hollow ache that filled her chest cavity and pressed against her ribs from the inside. Nicole sank to the floor, back against the bunk, and let her head drop into her hands.

This wasn't grief for her father. He'd been dead three years, mourned and buried and processed until the sharp edges had worn smooth. This felt more fundamental. She was mourning the person she'd been yesterday, the Nicole who'd understood her place in the galaxy's moral architecture.

A soft chime sounded. The door comm.

Nicole didn't move. Her throat felt too tight to speak, her body too heavy to stand. Whoever it was could wait. Could leave. Could assume she'd gone somewhere else.

The chime sounded again.

"Go away," she managed, her voice rough.

Silence. Then the door panel beeped its override warning—someone using command codes. Nicole looked up just as the door slid aside.

Daven stood in the corridor, hand still on the external panel. He took in the scene with a single glance—the broken holo-frame, the scattered components, Nicole sitting on the floor.

"I used my override," he said. "You can have me court-martialed later."

Nicole shook her head, unable to summon the energy to argue. Daven stepped inside and the door slid shut. He crossed to the desk chair, turned it to face her, and settled into it with deliberate casualness.

"I saw Marcus come out of the observation lounge," Daven said. "He looked like he'd just executed someone he loved."

Nicole's throat tightened. She forced the words out anyway. "My father knew about Chimera. Marcus told me—" She stopped, reorganizing her thoughts. "Commonwealth special operations came to those facilities. My father commanded that sector. He authorized it."

Daven nodded slowly. "What did they do there?"

"Retrieved data. Research." The words tasted like ash. "Marcus said they walked past children in cages to access computer cores."

"And Marcus was part of that unit."

"He was young. Following orders." Nicole looked up, meeting Daven's steady gaze. "He's carried that guilt for seventeen years. I could see it. But when I look at him now, all I can see is—"

"Your father's betrayal reflected in his face," Daven finished quietly.

"I know it's not fair to him. I know that." Nicole pushed herself up from the floor, crossing to the viewport. Veridia filled the view, cloud patterns swirling across the darkening hemisphere. "But knowing doesn't change what I feel."

"You're allowed to be angry," Daven said. "You don't have to process this immediately and present a perfect command face to the universe."

"We're in the middle of a crisis. Drayton is out there with enough technological advantage to destabilize the sector. We have ruins below that might contain the only leverage we have. I can't afford—"

"If you try to compartmentalize this, shove it down and deal with it later, it's going to poison everything you touch."

Nicole watched cloud patterns shift on Veridia's surface. The planet seemed peaceful from orbit, its ancient secrets hidden beneath layers of atmosphere and stone. But she knew what waited below—ruins that spoke of civilizations destroyed by forces they couldn't comprehend.

"I don't know how to reconcile it," she said quietly. "The man who raised me—the bedtime stories, the life lessons, the way he'd check my homework after sixteen-hour shifts. That man was real. But so was the man who authorized operations in facilities that tortured children." She turned to face Daven. "How do I hold both versions in my head at the same time?"

Daven was quiet for a moment. When he spoke, his voice carried a weight she rarely heard from him. "My uncle was a good man. Funny, kind, always had time for family. He also beat my aunt so badly she lost hearing in one ear." He met her eyes. "People contain contradictions. Your father could be both the man who loved you and the man who made terrible choices."

"That doesn't make it hurt less."

"No. But it might make it possible to eventually understand him. Not forgive—some things don't deserve forgiveness. But understand that people are capable of both great love and terrible cruelty."

Nicole felt tears finally break free, tracking hot lines down her cheeks. She didn't try to stop them. Daven stood and crossed to her, his hand finding her shoulder—a steady pressure that anchored her to the moment.

She let herself cry, silent tears that released some of the pressure building in her chest. Daven didn't offer platitudes or empty comfort, just his presence. The simple gift of not being alone.

Eventually the tears slowed. Nicole wiped her face with her sleeve, embarrassed but also somehow lighter.

"Thank you," she said. "For not letting me be alone with this."

"That's what first officers are for." He studied her face. "What about Marcus?"

Nicole turned back to the viewport. "I'm angry at him. But I also understand why he told me. He could have kept it hidden. Should have, probably. But he chose honesty instead." She drew a breath. "I need time before I can look at him without seeing my father's sins. But I also need him to know that I understand the difference between the boy who followed orders and the man who's trying to make amends."

"Do you want me to tell him that?"

"No. I will." The decision settled into her bones. "Just not tonight. Tonight I need to sit with this."

Daven nodded. "The crew's noticed something's wrong. Nothing specific, but the tension is obvious."

Nicole grimaced. "I'll call a briefing tomorrow. Not about this—this stays private. But we need to discuss the ruins anyway. Give everyone a clear objective."

"Good." Daven moved toward the door, then paused. "Marcus is tearing himself apart over this. Whatever you decide about him, he's going to assume the worst until you tell him otherwise."

"I know." Fresh guilt stabbed through her chest. "I'll talk to him soon. I just need a day."

After Daven left, Nicole retrieved a cleaning kit and spent several minutes gathering the broken holo-frame pieces. The display was ruined, but she kept the fragments anyway. She placed them in her bottom desk drawer, next to the physical letters her father had written during her first academy deployment.

Maybe someday she'd be able to read them without the bitter taste of betrayal. Maybe not.

* * *

Morning came too early. Nicole had managed perhaps three hours of fitful sleep, her dreams populated by shadowy laboratories and her father's face twisted into unfamiliar expressions. She woke feeling hollowed out, running on exhausted determination.

The briefing room felt crowded with the assembled department heads. Nicole stood at the head of the table, holographic display showing Veridia's primary ruin site. Daven sat to her right, datapad ready. Sera occupied the left, studying topographical data. Taryn claimed the far end, arms crossed. Marcus's seat remained empty.

"Where's our security chief?" Taryn asked.

"Following up on some personal research," Nicole said, keeping her voice neutral. "I've been briefed on his security recommendations."

It wasn't quite a lie. Marcus had sent her a detailed report at 0400 hours—clearly he wasn't sleeping either—outlining defensive postures for any ground expedition. The report was thorough, professional, completely devoid of personal content.

Daven gave her a sidelong glance but said nothing.

"Right." Taryn didn't look convinced. "So what's our play with these ruins?"

Nicole activated the next display layer. The ruin site expanded, revealing details their orbital scans had captured. "The structure extends at least three levels underground, possibly more. But this site shows signs of recent activity."

"Define recent," Sera said.

"Within the last standard year. We've detected trace residue from landing thrusters in this clearing." Nicole highlighted an area adjacent to the main complex. "Chemical signature matches Commonwealth military vessels, but the pattern is unusual. Someone made multiple visits."

"Drayton?" Daven suggested.

"Possibly. Or another faction." Nicole pulled up the next scan layer. "Here's where it gets interesting. Deep-penetration sonar shows structural modifications that don't match the original architecture. Someone has been building inside the ruins—adding rooms, reinforcing corridors, installing what looks like modern power infrastructure."

Sera leaned forward, studying the scan data. "That's significant resource investment. Whoever did this wasn't just visiting—they were establishing a base."

"Exactly." Nicole highlighted the power signatures. "And these energy readings suggest the installation is still active. Someone's down there right now, or was recently enough that the systems haven't gone dormant."

Taryn's expression shifted from skepticism to concern. "So we're not just exploring ancient ruins. We're potentially walking into an occupied facility."

"Which is why we're going in prepared." Nicole pulled up Marcus's security recommendations, grateful for the professional distance the document provided. "Four-person advance team, full tactical loadout. Reconnaissance only—no engagement unless absolutely necessary."

"Who's on the team?" Daven asked.

Nicole hesitated. The logical choice was obvious, but every part of her resisted saying it aloud. "Myself, Sera for technical analysis, Taryn for close-quarters capability, and—" She forced the words out. "And Marcus for security coordination."

The room went quiet. Everyone had noticed the tension between them; everyone was too professional to comment directly.

"Are you sure that's wise?" Daven asked carefully. "Given current... operational stress levels?"

"Marcus is the best tactical mind we have. Personal complications don't change that assessment." Nicole met Daven's eyes, willing him to understand. "We go in four hours. Use the time to prepare."

The department heads filed out, conversations resuming in low voices once they cleared the doorway. Nicole remained at the display, studying the ruin schematics without really seeing them.

Daven lingered. "You're pushing yourself."

"I'm doing my job."

"You're using your job to avoid dealing with what you're feeling." He moved closer, lowering his voice. "Going into a potentially hostile environment with someone you're struggling to trust—that's not professionalism. That's avoidance with tactical risks."

"Maybe." Nicole finally looked at him. "But it's also the fastest way to force myself through this. If I can work with Marcus under pressure, if I can trust him to watch my back when it matters—then maybe I can start trusting him with everything else."

"Or you could both get killed because your heads aren't in the mission."

"Then I guess we'd better make sure our heads are in the mission." Nicole deactivated the display. "I need to talk to him before we go down. Clear the air enough that it won't compromise us."

Daven nodded slowly. "And if you can't?"

Nicole thought about her father, about the contradictions Daven had described, about the way people could be both heroes and monsters depending on which angle you viewed them from.

"Then I learn to hold two truths at once," she said. "The man who hurt me, and the man I might still be able to trust. They're the same person. I just have to figure out how to see both."

She found Marcus in his quarters, staring at the same data reports she'd been avoiding. His face was haggard, marked by the same sleepless exhaustion she felt in her own bones.

"We go down in four hours," she said from the doorway. "I need to know if you can do this."

He looked up. The guilt in his expression nearly broke her resolve. "Nicole—"

"Not now. Later, we'll talk about everything. Right now, I need my security chief. Can you be that?"

A long pause. Then Marcus straightened, something shifting in his posture—the professional soldier emerging from beneath the weight of confession.

"Yes," he said. "I can be that."

"Good." Nicole turned to leave, then paused. "Marcus? The boy who followed orders seventeen years ago—I don't blame him. The man who told me the truth yesterday, even knowing how much it would hurt—I don't blame him either. But I need time to reconcile both of them with the person I thought I knew."

"I understand."

"I hope so." She stepped into the corridor, the door sliding shut between them.

Four hours. Then they'd descend into ruins that someone had already claimed, searching for answers that might destroy everything they thought they understood.

And somewhere beneath Veridia's ancient surface, something was waiting.

Chapter Twenty-Seven

Closing In

The interrogation chamber reeked of recycled fear and industrial solvent. Through the observation panel, Thane studied the trembling figure strapped to the chair—a man who'd survived three months in the Dead Sectors and bore every day of it in his flesh. His skin held the gray pallor of someone who'd breathed filtered air too long, eyes darting between her officers like a cornered animal hunting escape routes that didn't exist.

She'd been standing here nine hours. Her legs ached. Her eyes burned from the harsh overhead lighting. But rest meant closing her eyes, and closing her eyes meant seeing Marcus's face—not as it had been in those final moments, but as it should have been. Young. Idealistic. Alive.

Commander Voss worked the panel beside her, adjusting the audio feed until the prisoner's ragged breathing flooded the observation room. "Third contact we've pulled from this sector. Stories match the first two—scattered survivors, failed colonies, things in the dark that don't register on scanners."

"And the weapon?" The word tasted like ash in her mouth.

"He claims he's heard rumors. Nothing concrete." Voss glanced at her, his weathered face creased with concern. "Commander, the first two interrogators got nowhere. Maybe we're pushing too hard. Maybe he genuinely doesn't know anything useful."

Thane's jaw tightened. The first interrogator had wielded intimidation—standard pressure tactics that left the prisoner more terrified but no more forthcoming. The second had attempted rapport-building, spent three hours establishing trust only to strike the same walls. "Send in Lieutenant Marsh. Different approach—I want someone who can read between the lines. If he knows something but doesn't know he knows it, Marsh will excavate it."

Voss's silence spoke volumes, but he activated the comm panel and relayed the order. Through the observation window, the prisoner's head snapped up as the chamber door cycled open.

Thane had chosen Marsh specifically for this. The Lieutenant had spent two years in counterintelligence before transferring to her command—a patient interrogator who understood that information often hid in the spaces between words. Marsh entered with calm precision, back straight, movements economical, expression neutral. The prisoner's breathing accelerated.

Fear, Thane noted. But beneath it—he wants to talk. Needs to talk. He's been holding something for months and it's devouring him from within.

She'd witnessed it before. Survivors who carried knowledge like a weight, desperate to share the burden but terrified of the consequences. The key was making confession feel safer than silence.

Marsh settled into the chair across from him with deliberate ease. "State your designation. For the record."

The prisoner swallowed hard. "Designation?" His laugh held no humor. "We stopped using those after the sixth month. Names felt more… human. I'm Cade. Just Cade."

"Cade." Marsh leaned back slightly, a calculated gesture. "You were part of the Thornwood expedition?"

"Colony ship. Not expedition." Cade's hands clenched against the table. "We weren't exploring. We were supposed to be building something. A new start, away from the Core systems and their…" He trailed off, seemed to reconsider his words. "We wanted freedom."

Truth, Thane decided. Or at least, what he believes to be truth. How many idealists had she watched the Dead Sectors chew up? How many bright-eyed believers in new starts had learned the hard way that some spaces were dead for good reason?

"Tell me about the weapon," Marsh said.

Cade's expression shifted—fear giving way to something darker. Frustration, maybe. Or exhaustion with a question he'd already answered twice. "I told the others. It's just rumors. Stories people trade when the recyclers fail and you're sitting in the dark wondering if your oxygen will last another day."

"Stories come from somewhere." Marsh's tone remained neutral, but she'd shifted her posture—mirroring his lean, creating unconscious rapport. "What did you hear?"

"That something's waking up out here. Something old." Cade's voice dropped to barely above a whisper. "The sectors weren't always dead. Before the Coalition, before the expansion—there were others. We found their structures. Their… remnants."

Thane's pulse quickened. The briefing materials from Command had been sparse, carefully edited to excise classified details. But she'd read between the lines, recognized the patterns. Ancient installations. Power signatures that defied explanation. And Faren, somehow threaded through it all.

"What kind of remnants?" Marsh asked.

"Dead cities. Stations built into asteroids, technology we couldn't begin to understand." Cade's hands trembled now. "We thought we could salvage them. Use their materials, their power systems. But every team we sent came back wrong. Or didn't come back at all."

"Wrong how?"

The prisoner's eyes lost focus, staring at something beyond the chamber walls. "They'd forget things. Simple things at first—their quarters assignments, their shift schedules. Then bigger pieces. Names of crew members they'd worked with for years. Eventually, they forgot they were human at all."

Silence flooded the chamber. Thane felt ice crystallize along her spine. Memory loss. Progressive deterioration. Consistent with the psychological profiles, yes—but what if it's not psychological?

She thought of the scattered intelligence reports, the fragments Command had deemed too sensitive for standard distribution. Anomalous structures. Unexplained power signatures. Research teams that stopped reporting. All of it concentrated in these sectors, all of it brushed aside as equipment failure or stress-induced hallucination.

All of it potentially real.

Marsh had gone very still. Whatever she heard in Cade's testimony had triggered recognition, some connection to intelligence Thane hadn't shared with the full crew. Good. It meant her trust in Marsh's counterintelligence experience was justified.

"And the weapon?" Marsh pressed, but her voice had changed—acquired an edge that suggested she already suspected the answer.

Cade laughed, a sharp bark that dissolved into something close to sobbing. "You're not listening. The weapon isn't separate from the rest of it. It's all connected—the structures, the changes, the things in the dark. We thought we were exploring empty space. We were walking through a graveyard, and we woke something up."

Voss shifted beside Thane. "Commander, this is consistent with the psychological profiles we've developed for extended Dead Sector exposure. Paranoia, pattern-recognition failures, tendency toward mysticism—"

"Three separate survivors." Thane kept her voice level, but her mind raced through the implications. "Pulled from different locations. Describing the same phenomena. Either they've all been exposed to the same psychological trauma, developed the same delusions independently, or something in the Dead Sectors is generating consistent experiences."

"Or evidence of shared trauma responses. Mass hysteria isn't uncommon in isolated populations."

"Maybe." Thane studied Cade's face through the observation glass. The trembling wasn't performance—it ran too deep, settled in his bones like radiation exposure. "But I want to know what's generating the hysteria. Fear needs a source, even if it's misidentified."

She leaned toward the comm panel. "Lieutenant, ask him about recent activity. Specifically, any military vessels operating in this region."

Marsh nodded almost imperceptibly. "Have you seen any Coalition ships in this sector? Research vessels? Scientific expeditions?"

Cade's expression shifted to confusion. "Military? You're the first Coalition ship we've seen in eight months. Before that, maybe a patrol vessel every few weeks. Standard grid runs."

"No research vessels? Scientific expeditions?"

"Nothing official." Cade hesitated, and Thane saw the calculation in his eyes—weighing what he knew against what might keep him valuable, keep him alive. "But there were ships. Private ones, mostly. People who didn't want to be found, running from debts or warrants or failed marriages. The Dead Sectors attract those types."

"And recently?" Marsh asked.

Another pause, longer this time. Cade's fingers drummed against the table, a nervous pattern that accelerated as he made his decision. "There was a station. Three weeks ago, maybe four. Hard to track time out here. We picked up its beacon by accident, thought maybe it was another survivor group."

"Was it?"

"No." Cade's voice had gone flat. "It was operational. Full power, atmosphere, the works. But when we hailed them, they told us to stay away. Said they were conducting sensitive research and any approach would be considered hostile."

Thane's hands clenched at her sides. Her breath caught in her throat. A research station. Out here. Operational while survivors died in the dark. "Did they identify themselves?"

"Called themselves the Bastion Initiative."

The words hit like a physical blow. Thane's vision narrowed, her pulse thundering in her ears. Bastion. The word from Marcus's final transmission. The name buried in classified files she'd spent months accessing through unofficial channels. The connection between Faren's unauthorized research and whatever had gotten her brother killed.

Everything crystallized in that moment. The pieces she'd been gathering, the fragments of intelligence that Command had deemed too sensitive for standard distribution—they coalesced into something approaching pattern.

An ancient weapon. A hidden research facility. Faren, conducting unauthorized experiments in the Dead Sectors before Marcus discovered her activities.

Before she'd silenced him permanently.

"Get coordinates," Thane ordered, her voice cutting through the observation room like a blade. "Everything he knows about that station's location, capabilities, defensive systems. Everything."

Voss was already moving, but his voice carried carefully measured concern. "Commander, if this is a classified research facility, approaching without proper authorization could constitute a breach of operational protocols. We should relay this intelligence to Command and await—"

"Await what? More delays? More convenient classifications?" She turned on him, keeping her voice low enough that only he could hear. "We have credible intelligence regarding illegal operations in a restricted sector. We have a witness placing unusual activity in proximity to confirmed Dead Sector anomalies. That's well within our operational mandate."

"And if Command has reasons for keeping this classified? Reasons beyond protecting a murder suspect?"

"Then they can explain those reasons when we report our findings." Thane's expression hardened. "But I won't turn away from evidence because it might be inconvenient for someone's black budget project."

Voss held her gaze for a long moment before nodding. "Understood, Commander. But I'm logging my concerns for the record."

"I'd expect nothing less."

Marsh had pulled up a holographic display, and Cade was tracing coordinates with shaking hands. His voice filtered through the speakers, describing drift patterns and navigation hazards that had forced his crew off their intended course. Describing the station's silhouette against the stars, the way its power signature had blazed across their sensors like a beacon before they'd fled.

Describing everything Thane needed to find it.

The memory came unbidden—Marcus at eighteen, fresh from the academy, arguing with their father over dinner about the nature of military service. His eyes bright with conviction as he insisted that force should be the last resort, not the first. Their father's patient condescension as he reminded his son that the universe had sharp edges, and idealism dulled faster than blade steel.

Marcus had believed in justice. In accountability. In a system that worked when good people enforced it.

She would honor that.

Chapter Twenty-Eight

Reunion

Nicole stood outside the observation deck, her fingers trembling against the access panel. Through the narrow viewport, she could see Marcus silhouetted against the curve of Bastion's hull, his posture rigid as he stared into the void beyond. Three days had passed since his shuttle docked—since Commander Thane's terse shipwide announcement that Lieutenant Marcus Chen, declared KIA seven months prior, had returned from the Dead Sectors. Three days since Nicole had watched him disembark, hollow-eyed and twenty pounds lighter, and felt the universe tilt sideways.

The station hummed around her, its familiar vibrations doing nothing to settle her nerves. She'd faced down hostile boarding parties, navigated debris fields that would've shredded lesser pilots, survived the collapse of the Tertian Line during the war. None of that compared to the terror of pressing this button and stepping through that door.

Her hand moved before her courage failed completely. The panel chirped its acceptance, and the door whispered open on hydraulic breath.

Marcus didn't turn. His reflection in the reinforced glass showed the exhaustion that had carved itself into his features since his return—deeper lines bracketing his eyes, a hollowness in his cheeks that spoke of more than physical deprivation. The man who'd kissed her goodbye seven months ago had been solid, certain, full of the quiet confidence that had first drawn her to him. This version wore his body like borrowed clothes.

"Thought you might be Voss," he said quietly. "Coming to debrief me again."

"Just me." Nicole moved to stand beside him, keeping a careful distance. Close enough to talk. Far enough to retreat. "You've been avoiding the crew quarters."

"Been busy."

"You've been sleeping in the secondary maintenance bay. Chen mentioned it."

A muscle worked in his jaw. "Chen should mind his own business."

The silence stretched between them, filled only by the station's ambient noise—air circulators, the distant clang of cargo transfer, voices echoing through corridors designed to carry sound too far. Nicole studied Marcus's reflection, trying to reconcile the familiar lines of his face with the stranger inhabiting them.

"I don't know how to do this," she said finally. "I held your memorial service. I spoke about you in past tense while your mother wept in the front row. I watched them launch your empty coffin toward the solar wind because regulations required a ceremony even without remains. And now you're here, and I'm supposed to—what? Pretend those seven months didn't happen?"

Marcus's eyes closed. When he spoke, his voice carried a weight that made her chest ache. "I don't expect anything from you. I know what my disappearance did. What it cost."

"Do you?" The words came sharper than she'd intended. "Do you know I couldn't sleep in our quarters for two months? That I still wear your jacket when I run diagnostics because it's the only thing that makes the control room feel less empty? That I transferred to Bastion specifically because staying on the Meridian meant walking past your empty station every shift?"

He flinched as if she'd struck him. "Nicole—"

"You were dead." Her voice cracked on the word. "The scan data was conclusive. The debris pattern matched a catastrophic drive failure. There wasn't supposed to be any way you survived that, Marcus. And I made peace with it. I had to, because the alternative was falling apart when people needed me functional."

"I know."

"Then help me understand. Please." She forced herself to breathe, to gather the courage she'd been hoarding for three days. "Because right now you're standing three meters away and you might as well be on the other side of the Dead Sectors for all the distance between us."

Marcus turned finally, and the naked pain in his expression nearly undid her. He opened his mouth, closed it, his hands clenching and unclenching at his sides. For a long moment he just looked at her, and she watched thoughts move behind his eyes like ships navigating through fog.

"I don't know how to bridge that distance," he said at last. "Everything I went through out there—I can't explain it in a way that would make sense. And the things I had to do to survive, the compromises I made…" He trailed off, his voice catching. "I'm not sure I'm the same person you said goodbye to."

Nicole studied his face, this familiar stranger, and felt something shift in her chest. Fear, yes. Uncertainty. But underneath it, like bedrock beneath unstable ground, the same recognition that had first sparked between them in the Meridian's cramped navigation bay two years ago.

"Tell me about the last thing you remember," she said. "Before the accident. Before everything went wrong."

His eyebrows rose slightly. "Why?"

"Because I need to know if you remember it the way I do. I need to know if that's still real, even if everything after is complicated."

Marcus's expression softened. He moved closer, closing half the distance between them, and she could see the memory playing behind his eyes. "The night before the survey mission."

"Yes."

He was quiet for a moment, his gaze growing distant. "We were in crew quarters. You'd just finished a double shift routing supply manifests. You were exhausted, but you came anyway."

"You'd made dinner," Nicole said, the memory rising vivid and sharp. "Actual food, not ration packs. You'd traded your entire week's recreation credits for fresh vegetables from the hydroponics section."

* * *

The quarters smelled like sautéed peppers and garlic, an impossibly luxurious scent in the recycled air of a military vessel. Nicole stepped through the door and stopped, her exhaustion temporarily forgotten at the sight of Marcus bent over the small cooking unit they'd smuggled aboard in violation of at least three regulations.

"You actually did it," she said, laughing. "After all your talk about getting real food. I thought you were joking."

"Does this look like I'm joking?" Marcus glanced up, his face flushed from the heat of the improvised stove, and grinned. "Besides, tomorrow I'm heading out for a three-week survey run. I wanted one good meal with you before I spend twenty-one days eating rehydrated protein paste."

She crossed to him, wound her arms around his waist from behind, pressed her face against his shoulder. He was warm and solid and real, and the knot of tension she'd been carrying through fourteen hours of logistics work began to unwind.

"You're trying to bribe me with food," she murmured against his shirt.

"Is it working?"

"Completely."

They ate sitting on his bunk, plates balanced on their knees, talking about nothing important. Nicole told him about the requisition officer who'd tried to argue that fuel rods were discretionary supplies. Marcus described the navigation charts for the survey sector, pointed out the peculiar gravity eddies that made standard plotting algorithms unreliable. Easy conversation, comfortable in its familiarity.

Afterward, when the plates were set aside and the illegal cooking unit dismantled and hidden, Marcus pulled her close. The quarters were barely large enough for two people, the walls close and gray, but in that moment it felt perfect.

"Come back safe," Nicole said against his chest. "Three weeks isn't that long, but come back anyway."

"Always." He tilted her chin up, his eyes serious. "I've got too much here worth coming back to."

She kissed him then, tasting garlic and promises, feeling the steady beat of his heart beneath her palm. They made love slowly, carefully, as if they had all the time in the universe instead of just one night. His hands traced familiar paths along her skin, and she memorized the sound of his breathing, the way he whispered her name in the darkness.

Later, as she drifted toward sleep wrapped in his arms, he spoke so quietly she almost missed it.

"Whatever happens out there," he said, "remember this. Remember us like this."

At the time, she'd thought it was just sentiment. Just the kind of thing people said before temporary separations. She'd kissed his shoulder and fallen asleep without realizing it was goodbye.

* * *

"I thought you were being romantic," Nicole said, the observation deck snapping back into focus around them. Her eyes stung with unshed tears. "That last thing you said. I didn't know you had a premonition."

"I didn't." Marcus's voice was rough. "Or maybe I did. There'd been equipment failures all week. Small things. Inconsistencies in the survey vessel's diagnostic reports. I logged them, but the mission timeline was tight and Command didn't want delays. So I went anyway, and I spent that whole night trying not to think about what might go wrong."

"You should have told me."

"And said what? That I had a bad feeling? That I was scared?" He shook his head. "You would have worried, and it wouldn't have changed anything. The mission was mandatory. The only choice I had was whether to let fear poison our last night together."

Nicole felt anger flash through her, hot and clarifying. "Don't do that. Don't decide what I can handle. I would have worried anyway—I always did when you went out. But at least I would have understood. At least that last night wouldn't feel like a beautiful lie now."

The words landed harder than she'd intended. Marcus went still, something breaking behind his eyes.

"You're right," he said quietly. "You're right, and I'm sorry. I was trying to protect you, and all I did was make it worse."

"Yes. You did." She let the truth sit between them, sharp-edged and necessary. "So no more protection. No more deciding what I can bear. If we're going to have any chance of rebuilding this, I need to know you'll trust me with the hard things."

"Even if they hurt?"

"Especially then."

Marcus looked at her for a long moment, something shifting in his expression. "I'm sorry," he said again, and this time it carried more weight. "For leaving you. For surviving in a way that made the loss worse when you found out. For coming back different. For every decision I made that put that grief in your eyes."

"Stop apologizing and tell me what I need to hear." Nicole moved closer, drawn by the rawness in his voice despite her fear. "Tell me if there's anything left of what we had. If that night I keep replaying was real or just something I'm holding onto because grief makes us stupid."

"It was real." His hand reached out, hesitant, fingers trembling in the space between them. "Everything we had was real. Everything I felt was real. That hasn't changed, Nicole. That's the one thing that didn't break out there in the dark."

She caught his hand before she could second-guess herself. The touch sent electricity through her nerves—seven months of absence, three days of careful distance, and now this. His skin against hers, warm and alive and impossible. She'd dreamed this contact so many times in the weeks after his death, phantom sensations that left her aching when she woke.

"I don't know how to trust that," she whispered. "I don't know how to believe in something that was taken away so completely."

"Then don't trust it yet." Marcus moved closer, closing the last of the distance between them, until she could feel the heat radiating from his body. "Just let me show you I'm here. Let me prove it's real, one day at a time, for as long as it takes."

Nicole looked up at him, this man who was Marcus and not-Marcus, familiar and foreign. Fear warred with longing in her chest. The safe choice would be to step back, to maintain distance until she could process everything. The safe choice would be to protect herself from the possibility of losing him again.

She'd never been good at safe choices.

Her free hand rose to his chest, pressed flat against his sternum. His heart hammered beneath her palm, as rapid and uncertain as her own. "If I do this," she said, "if I let you back in—I need you to promise me something."

"Anything."
