Nicole's scanner showed the layout—a central hub with six arms radiating outward. They were in the dock arm. The others led to processing, storage, life support, operations, and research.

"Life support first," Marcus said. "If we can get atmosphere and heat, we can take off the suits."

The life support arm showed more damage. A breach had been sealed at some point—emergency foam hardened across a section of wall. But the systems beyond remained intact. Marcus found the manual controls and began working through the restart sequence.

"This will take a while," he said. "These old stations had safeties built in layers."

Nicole explored while he worked. Storage yielded to her crowbar, revealing racks of vacuum-sealed supplies. Food. Water. Medical supplies. Enough for a crew of maybe twenty for several months. She grabbed what they'd need immediately—rations, water, a medical kit better than their ship's.

The station had been evacuated, she realized. Not destroyed suddenly, but deliberately abandoned. Someone had sealed the breaches, shut down non-essential systems, and left. Waiting, perhaps, for a rescue that never came.

The station shuddered. Nicole froze, listening. Just debris glancing off the hull, the impact transmitted through the structure as a low harmonic moan. The Ashfall never stopped moving, never stopped grinding itself into smaller pieces.

When she returned to life support, Marcus had shed his helmet. He glanced up, and something in his expression made her stop.

"Atmosphere's stable in this section," he said. "Scrubbers are running. Temperature will take longer, but we can breathe."

Nicole removed her own helmet. The air tasted stale but clean, filtered through machinery that had been sleeping for years. She set down the supplies and met his eyes.

"Thank you," she said.

He shook his head. "We both needed this."

The words stuck in her throat—the acknowledgment of the tether that had formed between them somewhere in the days since Bastion's auction house. Since she'd watched him move through a crowd with careful grace, someone who'd learned to make himself small, learned to survive.

"There's crew quarters in the habitat arm," Marcus said. "We could—" He stopped, suddenly aware of the weight of what he was suggesting.

Nicole felt her pulse quicken. "Show me."

The habitat corridor held a dozen berths. Marcus chose one at random and palmed the door control. Inside, signs of hasty departure—an open locker with empty hangers, personal items on magnetic strips. A framed image of strangers frozen in laughter. A small plant reduced to scattered leaves and brittle stems.

Marcus began working on the climate controls. "I'll get the heat up. These old berths had individual systems."

Nicole watched his hands move with certainty across panels and switches. "Where did you learn all this?"

He glanced over his shoulder. "Had to. Growing up on the rim, stations like this were everywhere. Most abandoned, some still running on backup. If you knew how to wake them safely, you could have a place to sleep where no one would find you."

"You were running."

"Most of the time. My father's debts followed me. His enemies followed me." He made a final adjustment and the heat flow increased. "It was easier to keep moving, to find places the official maps had forgotten."

Nicole sat on the bunk. The mattress was thin but intact—probably vacuum-sealed, designed for exactly this kind of long-term dormancy. Marcus turned to face her, exhaustion carved into his features. The gray dust of the Ashfall had settled into every crease. He looked older than his years.

"We should rest," he said. "Take actual shifts sleeping. Six hours each."

Nicole stood. She crossed the small space until she could feel the heat radiating from his suit, could see the pulse beating at the base of his throat where the seal met skin.

"Nicole," he said quietly. "You're—"

"Tired of being alone." She reached for his helmet seal. "Tired of pretending I don't feel this."

His hand rose slowly, fingers brushing against her jaw. The touch was tentative, asking permission. She leaned into it, feeling the calluses on his palm.

"I don't want to take advantage," Marcus said. "You've been through so much—"

She kissed him. Cut off the words with her mouth against his, with her hands finding the seal of his suit and opening it with practiced efficiency. His breath caught. His hands moved to her shoulders, steadying them both.

When she pulled back, his eyes searched hers.

"I'm sure," Nicole said. "I've been sure for days."

His smile was small, almost shy. "So have I."

They helped each other out of the suits, peeling away layers until they stood in thin underlayers. Nicole felt exposed in a way she hadn't since before Bastion fell—not threatened, but vulnerable. Marcus's hands found her waist, drawing her closer, his forehead resting against hers.

"Tell me what you need," he said.

Nicole's throat tightened. When had anyone asked her that?

"Just be here," she whispered. "Just be with me."

They moved to the bunk together. Marcus pulled the blanket free from its seal and spread it over the mattress while she stripped away her underlayer. The cold air raised goosebumps. His hands moved to warm her—palms sliding up her arms, across her shoulders, down to the small of her back.

"You're beautiful," he said.

The way he looked at her made her believe it.

They lay down together, fitting into the narrow bunk. Marcus's body was warm against hers, solid and real in a way that made everything else recede. He kissed her slowly—jaw, throat, collarbone. Each touch was a question, and Nicole answered with the arch of her back, the press of her hips.

"Tell me," Marcus said again. His hand moved lower. "Tell me what you want."

"Everything," she whispered. "I want everything."

His touch became more certain, mapping her body, finding the places that made her gasp. When he moved above her, she opened to him—not just her body but something deeper, something she'd thought long dead.

The joining was slow. Marcus moved with care, watching her face, adjusting to her responses. They found their rhythm together. The bunk creaked. The station hummed around them, life support and gravity combining into a mechanical lullaby.

Nicole lost track of time. There was only rising heat, friction of skin, the sound of Marcus's breathing growing ragged.

"Look at me," Marcus said.

She opened her eyes. Met his gaze. Saw herself reflected there—not the soldier, not the commander. Just Nicole.

The orgasm broke over her like a wave. She cried out, clutching his shoulders as pleasure rolled through her. She felt him follow, felt him shudder and gasp her name.

They collapsed together. Marcus rolled to his side, taking her with him, keeping them connected. His hand found her hair.

"Nicole," he whispered. "God, Nicole."

She pressed her face against his chest. Let herself hear his heartbeat. Let herself be held.

"Thank you," she said.

Marcus kissed her forehead. "For what?"

"For reminding me I'm still alive."

They stayed like that as minutes passed into hours. Marcus fell asleep first, his breathing evening out. Nicole remained awake longer, listening to the station's mechanical breathing, feeling the gentle rotation.

She thought about the people who'd lived here once. The crew who'd evacuated in good order, expecting to return. What had happened to them? The image on the wall caught her eye—those frozen faces, that moment of joy captured and abandoned.

Nicole closed her eyes. Let herself drift toward sleep while Marcus's warmth surrounded her. Tomorrow they'd continue the hunt. Tomorrow she'd put the armor back on.

But tonight, she was just Nicole. Just a woman sleeping beside someone who'd chosen to follow her into hell.

Someone who made her remember what it felt like to hope.

* * *

When she woke, Marcus was watching her. The lighting had shifted—some automated cycle mimicking day and night. His expression was soft, unguarded.

"How long did I sleep?" Nicole asked.

"Four hours. I was going to wake you soon."

"You should have woken me sooner."

Marcus shook his head. "I wanted to watch you sleep. You looked peaceful."

She pulled him closer. They lay together in the dim light, skin against skin, breathing synchronized.

"Tell me something true," she said. "Something you've never told anyone else."

Marcus was quiet for a long moment. "I'm afraid all the time. Every decision, every move—I'm terrified I'll make the wrong choice and people will die because of it. That I'll turn out like my father."

Nicole understood that fear intimately. "You're nothing like him. You couldn't be if you tried."

"How do you know?"

"Because you're here. Because when I needed help, you gave it without asking for anything in return."

His fingers traced patterns on her shoulder blade. "What about you? What's your true thing?"

Nicole took a breath. "I'm afraid I'm exactly like Thane. That the only difference between us is opportunity."

"That's not true."

"I want her dead, Marcus. I want to put a bullet between her eyes and watch the light go out. What does that make me?"

"Human," Marcus said. "Revenge isn't the same as cruelty. Justice isn't the same as tyranny."

"The line between them is thinner than you think."

"Maybe. But you see the line. That's what matters. Thane doesn't even acknowledge it exists."

Nicole closed her eyes. Let herself believe him. Marcus's hand moved to her face, tilting her chin up. He kissed her again, gentle and thorough. The heat began to build between them once more.

Chapter Thirty-Five

The Handler's Game

The common area of the waypoint station bore a desolate quality that mirrored Sera's mood. She sat cross-legged on the deck plating, portable terminal balanced on her knees, staring at the communication array access she'd been circling for the last twenty minutes.

They'd been aboard for three hours now. Long enough to confirm the station's abandonment, long enough to verify basic life support still functioned, long enough for the others to begin sorting through the equipment cache they'd discovered in the cargo bay. Long enough for Sera's paranoia about all of it to crystallize into certainty that something was wrong.

The Handler's directions had delivered them here too easily. The station had powered up too smoothly. And now the communication array sat accessible from a standard terminal, practically inviting her to peer inside.

Marcus sat nearby, cleaning weapons with methodical precision. He glanced up as Sera finally made her decision and began typing.

"Checking something," she said before he could ask.

"Want company?"

"Might as well. This is either going to be enlightening or get us killed."

She routed her access through three separate protocol layers, establishing monitoring routines to watch for traceback attempts. It would slow her down, but it might also keep them from getting caught with their hands in the honeypot. The Handler's protocols resisted for exactly the right amount of time—long enough to feel like a victory, not long enough to constitute genuine security.

That wrong-feeling rhythm made her add another layer of protection, spoofing her access point to appear to originate from a different terminal entirely. Only then did she push deeper into the system.

The message fragments surfaced almost immediately, encoded in a commercial cipher that any basic academy student could crack with enough time. Another detail that scratched at the back of her mind. Why use commercial encryption for anything actually sensitive?

Unless you wanted someone specific to read it.

Sera worked through the decryption while her monitoring programs ran in parallel, watching for any sign that the system was phoning home about her intrusion. So far, nothing. Either her precautions were working or whoever had constructed this trap wanted her to find it badly enough to stay quiet.

Neither option offered comfort.

"Sera?" Marcus had abandoned his weapons maintenance, moving closer to read over her shoulder. His breath caught as the first decoded message materialized.

Subject responsive to crisis scenarios. Recommend escalation.

Extraction sequence initiated. Asset Kray will provide believable threat.

Subject bonding with team parameters as predicted. Proceed to secondary location.

Sera's hands froze on the keyboard. She sat motionless, staring at words that rewrote the past three months of her life.

"This can't be right," Marcus said, but his voice carried no conviction.

She forced herself to continue, pulling up more messages. Directorate routing codes embedded in the headers. Directorate encryption keys nested inside the commercial cipher. A conversation that rendered everything they'd endured choreographed.

Her monitoring programs chimed—not with an alert about traceback, but with detection of physical devices nearby. Sera grabbed her scanner, already standing.

"What is it?" Marcus asked.

"Physical surveillance. And when something's too easy, you sweep for tripwires." She gestured back at the terminal. "Those files are damning enough that no competent intelligence service would leave them accessible unless they wanted someone to find them."

They swept the common areas first, Sera's scanner humming as it detected electronic signatures. She found three monitoring devices hidden in equipment they'd brought aboard—passive sensors designed to masquerade as standard environmental monitors.

"Clever," she muttered, isolating the nodes without disabling them yet. "Give us everything we need, make it too useful to refuse, and let us bug ourselves."

"Can you disable them?"

"In a minute. First I want to know what they're transmitting and where it's going."

She connected her portable terminal to one of the nodes, intercepting its data stream. The device was reporting basic telemetry—location, power status, air quality readings. Nothing sensitive yet, but it was configured to accept remote commands that could expand its surveillance capabilities.

The destination address was routed through a Directorate military network.

"They're not even trying to hide it," she said.

Marcus leaned closer, frowning at her screen. "Why be sloppy if they've been so careful everywhere else?"

"Because they want us to know." The realization crystallized as she spoke. "They want us to find this, to understand they have leverage on everyone. It's psychological warfare—make us feel powerless before they make their next move."

She disabled the monitoring devices then, watching her scanner confirm each one going dark. Somewhere in the Directorate's intelligence apparatus, someone would notice the sudden loss of signal. Let them notice. This was about control, about making them feel surrounded.

"We need to tell the others," Marcus said quietly. His usual steady demeanor had fractured slightly—she could see it in the tension around his eyes, the way his hand kept curling into a fist and releasing. "All of it."

Sera nodded, already heading back to the communication terminal. But she paused before sitting down, looking at her teammate—her friend, really, though she'd been slow to admit it.

"Marcus. Before we do this. Are you—" She stopped, recalibrated. "How are you handling this? Finding out you've been manipulated?"

He was quiet for a long moment. "I keep thinking about every choice I made. Joining your crew. Taking those jobs. Coming into the Dead Sectors." He met her eyes. "Trying to figure out which decisions were actually mine and which ones they engineered. And I can't tell anymore. That's what's really getting to me—not knowing if any of it was real."

"It was real," Sera said. "The manipulation was real. But so was everything we did in response to it. They can set up the scenario, but they can't reach inside our heads and pull the strings directly. We still made choices."

"Did we though? If they predicted exactly how we'd respond—"

"Prediction isn't control. I know it feels like the same thing right now, but it's not." She turned back to the terminal. "And we're about to prove it."

Instead of copying files, Sera began a careful analysis of what was accessible and what wasn't. The communications were laid out like breadcrumbs—obvious, damning, designed to be found. But there were other files in the system, protected by heavier encryption. Files that weren't meant to be part of the revelation.

Those were the ones she wanted.

"What are you doing?" Marcus asked.

"They want us to see certain things. So I'm looking at what they're trying to hide instead." Her fingers moved across the keyboard, probing the system architecture. "If this is really a trap, the bait is those communications. The real intelligence will be buried deeper."

She found it in a partitioned section of the array's memory, protected by Directorate military-grade encryption. Not impossible to crack, but time-consuming enough that most people would settle for the easy discoveries and move on.

Sera wasn't most people.

She established the decryption protocols and sat back, watching the progress bar. "This is going to take a while. Might as well brief the others on what we've found so far."

Marcus nodded, then hesitated. "Sera, if they have leverage files on everyone… if they have information about my sister…"

"We'll deal with it," she said, though the promise felt hollow. "Together. That's the one thing I don't think they accounted for—that we'd actually become a real team instead of just a collection of assets."

"You think that matters?"

"I think it's the only thing that does."

* * *

They found the others in what had been the station's common area—a space that might have been welcoming once, before decades of abandonment had stripped it down to bare metal and failing life support. Yuri was working on one of the environmental processors, his mechanical expertise slowly coaxing the ancient system back to life. Chen sat nearby, cataloging medical supplies they'd salvaged from the ship. Kade stood at one of the viewports, staring out at the gray that pressed against the station like fog against glass.

All of them looked up as Sera and Marcus entered, and she saw the questions forming before anyone spoke.

"We found something," Sera said. "About the Handler. About why we're really here. And it's bad."

She connected her portable terminal to a larger display, pulling up the communications she'd decoded. No point in easing into it. They deserved the truth straight.

"They've been watching me for almost a year," she said as the messages scrolled past. "Building a profile, predicting how I'd respond to recruitment. The Handler wasn't offering me freedom. They were offering me exactly what I wanted to hear so I'd do what they needed."

She watched their faces as they absorbed the staged pursuit, the careful choreography that had driven them into the Dead Sectors. Shock giving way to anger, anger mixing with the dawning realization of how thoroughly they'd been played.

Then she pulled up the leverage files.

"And they have information on all of you. Pressure points they're ready to use if we don't cooperate."

The silence stretched long enough that Sera could hear the station's struggling ventilation system. Yuri's hands had gone still on his workbench. Chen's medical supplies lay forgotten. Even Kade had turned from the viewport, his combat-trained focus entirely on the display.

"My son," Yuri said finally. "What exactly do they have on him?"

Sera pulled up his file. "Custody arrangements. Financial support records. Notes that his mother's current housing is subsidized by a Directorate-linked corporation." She paused. "If you're declared a criminal, they can make the custody agreement fall apart. Or make things difficult enough that she loses him anyway."

Yuri's hands curled into fists, but his voice stayed level. "What else?"

She went through the rest—Chen's mother's experimental medical treatment funded by a Directorate research program, Kade's brother in the military with debts that Kade had been quietly helping with, Marcus's fifteen-year-old sister in academy prep.

Marcus went very still when she showed him that file. His sister's photo smiled up from the display—a kid who should be worried about exams and social dynamics, not serving as a pawn in intelligence operations.

"They documented everything," Marcus said quietly. "Every connection, every vulnerability. Like we were just… inventory."

"That's exactly what we are to them," Sera said. "Inventory. Assets. Tools to be used and discarded."

"So what do we do?" Chen's question was practical, cutting through the emotional weight. "We're here. They know where we are. They have pressure points on most of us. What are our options?"

Sera had been thinking about that question since she'd first cracked the Handler's communications. The answers weren't good.

"We can cooperate. Take the equipment, do whatever operation they have planned, become what they're trying to make us." She met their eyes. "I'm not recommending it. Just laying out options."

"What else?" Yuri asked.

"We can run. Deeper into the Dead Sectors, beyond where even the Directorate can easily reach. But that means abandoning everyone back in Directorate space. Leaving them vulnerable to retaliation."

The weight of that settled over the group. Sera saw it in their faces—the calculation of whether they could live with sacrificing the people they cared about for their own freedom.

"Third option," Marcus said. "We fight back."

"Against the Directorate?" Chen's skepticism was clear. "We're five people with a damaged ship and limited supplies. They have entire fleets."

"We're not fighting their fleets," Marcus said. "We're fighting their information advantage. Right now, they think they have us cornered because they control what we know. But Sera found files they didn't want us to see." He looked at her. "What's in them?"

Sera checked her terminal. The decryption had finished while they'd been talking. She pulled up the results, scanning through rapidly—and felt her breath catch.

"Names," she said slowly. "Operational codenames. And a project designation: CONVERGENCE." She looked up, meeting their eyes. "It's not just about us. The Handler is running multiple operations across the Dead Sectors. Dozens of teams, all being manipulated the same way we were. All being positioned for something."

"Positioned for what?" Kade asked.

Sera scrolled deeper into the files. "There's a target. Something called the Nexus Array. According to this, it's a pre-Collapse communications hub buried somewhere in the deep sectors. The Directorate's been searching for it for decades. And now they're using expendable assets—us—to find it for them."

"Expendable," Yuri repeated flatly.

"That's the word in the file. Expendable reconnaissance assets. We find it, we report it, and then..." She trailed off, but they all understood. Assets that knew too much didn't survive long after their usefulness ended.

The silence that followed was different from before. Not shock anymore—something harder. Something that looked like resolve.

"Then we find it first," Marcus said. "We find this Nexus Array before they expect us to, and we use it as leverage. Information for information. Our families' safety for whatever secrets that thing contains."

"That's insane," Chen said. "We'd be gambling everything on—"

"We're already gambling everything," Sera cut in. "The only question is whether we keep playing their game or start playing our own." She stood, gathering her equipment. "I need to dig deeper into these files. Find out where the Nexus Array actually is. In the meantime, everyone think about what you're willing to risk."

She was almost to the door when Kade's voice stopped her.

"Sera. The Handler—do we have a real name? A face?"

She turned back, pulling up the final file she'd cracked. The image that materialized on the display made Marcus inhale sharply.

A woman in Directorate uniform. Severe features, silver-streaked hair, the kind of eyes that had seen terrible things and made peace with them. And beneath the image, a name.

Director Elise Vaughn. Directorate Special Operations. Codename: HANDLER.

"Vaughn," Chen whispered. "That's—"

"Nicole's mother's maiden name," Sera confirmed. "Either it's a coincidence, or Nicole's connection to this goes deeper than any of us realized."

The implications hung in the air like smoke. Nicole, who had been manipulated into rescuing Marcus. Nicole, whose every choice might have been engineered by someone who shared her blood.

"We need to tell her," Marcus said quietly. "She deserves to know."

"She deserves a lot of things," Sera agreed. "Starting with the truth." She saved the files to an encrypted drive. "I'll handle it. But not here. Not like this."

She left them staring at the image of Director Vaughn—a woman who had turned her own family into pawns, who had orchestrated everything from the shadows, who was about to discover that her expendable assets had stopped following the script.

The game was about to change.

And Sera intended to make sure they were the ones rewriting the rules.

Chapter Thirty-Six

Convergence

The Ashfall debris field stretched before them like the scattered bones of a thousand dead ships, each fragment tumbling through the void in its own lonely orbit. Thane studied the tactical display as the Resolute knifed through the outer edge of the field, sensor arrays limning the wreckage in shades of amber and red.

"Contact," Lieutenant Marsh called from tactical. "Three ships just dropped out of jump. Bearing two-seven-mark-three."

Thane's jaw tightened. The timing was too precise to be coincidence. Someone had fed them coordinates—probably the same source that had been dripping intelligence to Command for the past week. He'd suspected a leak in Nicole's operation since Vardus Station, but confirmation still tasted like copper on his tongue.

"Configuration?" he asked.

"Light cruiser. Looks like the Meridian's Hand. Two escort frigates running interference pattern." Marsh's fingers danced across her console. "They're vectoring straight for the debris pocket where we lost them."

Where we lost her, Thane thought, but didn't say. The Ashfall had claimed forty-seven vessels in the past decade alone. Most captains gave it a wide berth, treated it like the graveyard it was.

Perfect place to hide. Perfect place to die, too.

"Take us in," Thane ordered. "Standard pursuit formation. Tell the Hammer and Vigilance to bracket our approach—I don't want them slipping past us again."

The deck shifted beneath his feet as the helm adjusted course. Through the forward viewport, the debris field swelled from scattered points of light into a maze of twisted metal and frozen atmosphere crystals. The largest pieces loomed building-sized—former warships from the Consolidation Wars now gutted to hollow shells. Smaller fragments choked the spaces between: panels, hull sections, cargo containers that had ruptured and spilled their contents across the void.

"Sensors are compromised," Marsh reported. "Too much metal. Getting ghost readings everywhere."

"Switch to thermal tracking," Thane said. "They'll have to vent heat eventually. Nothing runs cold forever, not in active evasion."

He moved closer to the tactical display, studying the three-dimensional map of the debris field. The Ashfall earned its name from the volcanic-orange hue of its larger fragments, metal scorched by weapons fire and atmospheric burn during whatever ancient battle had birthed this monument to destruction. Somewhere in that chaos, Nicole Kess was plotting her next move.

Ezra Kellan was with her—that much intelligence had confirmed—and possibly the quantum encryption keys from the Bastion facility. Together, those two elements represented enough threat to justify mobilizing a full pursuit squadron.

But Thane had volunteered for this hunt. Had pushed Command to give him lead. The job needed doing properly, with precision rather than overwhelming force. A commander who understood both the tactical requirements and the political implications. Command had agreed, though whether they understood his real motivations was another question.

"Sir, the Meridian's Hand is requesting coordination protocols," the communications officer said.

"Denied. Tell Captain Voss we'll maintain independent operation. His job is to seal escape vectors, nothing more."

The officer hesitated. "He's senior rank, sir."

"And I have Command authority for this operation." Thane kept his voice level, aware of the weight of what he was doing. Voss would file a complaint. Command would expect results to justify the command conflict. "Send it. Then append a personal note—tactical independence is required given debris field density and communication lag. Multiple command voices will compromise pursuit effectiveness."

It wouldn't satisfy Voss, but it would give Command cover if they needed it. And Thane needed the freedom to operate without coordination delays, without someone else making the call when Nicole finally showed herself.

The Resolute shuddered as it pushed deeper into the debris field. Through the viewport, a massive hull fragment drifted past—close enough that Thane could read the half-melted designation on its flank. The Sovereign Dawn, lost twelve years ago with all hands. Now just another obstacle in the void.

"Heat signature," Marsh said. "Bearing one-one-five-mark-eight. Could be them."

"Could be?"

"Signature's weak. Might be residual heat from an old reactor core. Might be deliberate misdirection." She glanced up from her console. "If I were trying to lose a tail in the Ashfall, I'd seed the field with thermal decoys."

If you were Nicole Kess, Thane thought. "Investigate it anyway. Use the Vigilance—they have better point defense for close debris work."

The hunt continued. The Resolute threaded through the wreckage like a predator through tall grass, sensors straining against the interference. Twice more they found heat signatures that vanished upon approach. Once they detected a drive plume that turned out to be outgassing from a ruptured fuel cell.

Each false contact tightened the knot in Thane's chest. She was out there, somewhere in this graveyard, buying time or setting a trap. The woman had always been too far forward, too eager to be first into danger. It was what had made her such an effective operative. What had made Thane notice her.

Time compressed into a series of contacts and disappointments. Sensor sweeps. Course corrections. The methodical, grinding work of pursuit through hostile terrain.

Then Marsh's voice cut through the bridge tension, sharper than before: "Solid contact. Three ships, close formation, bearing three-three-zero-mark-two. Power signatures match the Wayfarer and her escort vessels. They're moving fast, heading for the far edge of the field."

There. Finally.

"All ships, pursue," Thane ordered. "Weapons free. Target drives with focused energy bursts—I want them disabled, not vaporized."

The Resolute surged forward, compensators straining to keep the acceleration manageable. On the tactical display, the three ships blazed as bright points of light threading through the debris maze. Nicole's ship in the lead, naturally.

"Range in forty seconds," Marsh reported. "They're not taking evasive action. Just running straight."

Something cold touched Thane's spine. "Scan for weapons signatures. Full spectrum."

"Negative on weapons. They're clean."

Too easy. Nicole Kess didn't run straight. She turned and twisted, found angles others missed, made chaos work for her instead of against her.

"Sir, we're entering maximum debris density," the helm officer said. "Fragments are getting larger. Navigation recommends reduced speed."

"Maintain pursuit speed," Thane said. "What's our closure rate?"

"Thirty seconds to firing range."

On the viewport, the chase had become visible—three ships weaving between massive hull fragments, thrusters firing in controlled bursts to navigate the deadly maze. The Resolute followed, larger and more powerful but also less maneuverable. Behind them, the Hammer and Vigilance spread wide, covering possible break-off angles.

Twenty seconds.

The lead ship—had to be the Wayfarer—cut hard to port, diving beneath a tumbling destroyer section. The two escort ships split, one high, one low.

"Evasive pattern," Marsh said. "Now they're moving."

Fifteen seconds.

Thane leaned forward, hands gripping the rail at the tactical station. Close enough now to see details on the fleeing ships—hull scarring, painted insignias, the blue-white glow of overcharged engines.

Ten seconds.

"Weapons lock," Marsh said. "Ready to fire on your mark."

"Fire."

The Resolute's energy batteries released focused beams, electromagnetic pulses designed to overload drive systems without breaching hulls. In the void between ships, the shots appeared as lances of blue-white light, closing the distance in heartbeats.

And missing.

The Wayfarer rolled, presenting minimal profile. One escort ship cut thrust entirely, dropping like a stone while the beams passed overhead. The other fired maneuvering thrusters in a sequence too complex to be human-piloted—computer-assisted evasion, reading the incoming fire and calculating counter-moves faster than conscious thought.

"Targets evading," Marsh said, unnecessary but protocol. "Recalculating firing solution."

"They're leading us," Thane said. The realization clicked into place with terrible clarity. "This whole run—they want us here."

"Sir?"

Before he could answer, the tactical display erupted with new contacts. Dozens of them, scattered through the debris field around their position.

"Mines," Marsh said, voice tight. "Proximity-triggered. We're in a kill box."

The first detonation hammered the Resolute's shields, a burst of shaped explosives designed to channel force into a narrow cone. Not enough to breach primary armor, but enough to scatter their formation, force them to react defensively.

More explosions followed, a cascade of fire that transformed the debris field into a weapons platform. Nicole had seeded the Ashfall with enough ordnance to make it a deathtrap, probably days ago, and then waited for them to chase her straight into it.

"Evasive action!" Thane ordered. "Get us out of the kill zone."

The helm threw the Resolute into a hard turn, but this deep in the debris field, there was nowhere to turn to. Fragments hemmed them in on all sides, and the mines detonated with perfect timing, always where they were going rather than where they'd been.

Someone had programmed this. Not just set charges and hoped—actually mapped their pursuit trajectory and placed explosives accordingly.

Nicole. Had to be Nicole. She'd always been three moves ahead.

"Shield strength at seventy percent," Marsh reported. "The Hammer took a direct hit—they're venting atmosphere from sections four and five."

On the tactical display, the three fleeing ships had reversed course. Now they came back, weapons hot, using the confusion of the minefield to close the distance.

"Incoming fire!" Marsh shouted.

The viewport blazed as mass driver rounds hammered their forward shields. Not military-grade weapons—Nicole's ships couldn't match that firepower—but enough to matter when shields were already compromised. The Resolute shook like a living thing, damage reports flooding in from multiple sections.

"Return fire," Thane ordered. "Target the Wayfarer. Disable only—I want that ship intact."

But Nicole's ship was already diving beneath them, using the Resolute's bulk as cover while her escort vessels maintained suppressing fire. Thane watched her thread between debris fragments that should have torn her hull open, executing turns that exceeded recommended tolerance by half again.

She'd always been the best pilot he'd known. Now she was proving it.

"The Vigilance is breaking off," communications reported. "Captain Ross says his hull integrity is compromised. He's requesting permission to exit the debris field."

"Denied," Thane said. "All ships maintain engagement."

"Sir, if the Vigilance loses structural integrity—"

"I said denied!"

Another explosion, this one close enough to throw Thane against the tactical rail. Alarms shrieked across the bridge. Through the viewport, he saw one of Nicole's escort ships execute a perfect barrel roll, using the maneuver to bring its weapons to bear on the Hammer.

The Meridian's Hand was trying to flank, Voss finally deciding to ignore orders and join the fight properly. But Nicole had anticipated that too—more mines detonated in the Hand's path, forcing the cruiser to abort its approach.

Thane's jaw clenched so hard his teeth ached. She was beating them. Three civilian ships with improvised weapons and a tactical genius at the helm, and she was actually beating a fully armed pursuit squadron.

"New contact," Marsh said. "Single ship, emerging from behind the destroyer hulk at bearing zero-nine-zero. It's the Wayfarer—she must have swapped transponder codes with one of the escorts."

Thane's blood ran cold. The ship they'd been chasing wasn't the Wayfarer at all. Nicole had switched identities mid-pursuit, let them chase a decoy while she repositioned for—

"She's making a run for the far edge of the field," Marsh reported, voice tight with grudging admiration. "Clear trajectory. If she clears the debris in the next ninety seconds, she can jump."

"Intercept course!"

But the Resolute was already committed to the wrong vector, shields depleted, navigation systems struggling with the debris density. By the time they could turn and pursue, Nicole would be gone.

Thane watched the real Wayfarer streak through the gaps between dead ships, her hull catching the reflected light of distant stars. She flew like she always had—reckless and precise, pushing her vessel to the edge of destruction and somehow finding the line that kept her alive.

"The Vigilance might have an angle," Marsh said. "If Ross ignores your earlier order—"

"He won't." Thane knew Ross. The man followed orders even when they were wrong. Especially when they were wrong. "She's going to make it."

The words tasted like ash.

On the tactical display, the Wayfarer cleared the last major debris cluster and entered open space. Her drives flared as she built velocity for the jump, the escort ships falling in behind her in tight formation.

"Jump signature forming," Marsh said quietly. "She's gone in ten... nine..."

Thane watched, helpless, as the woman he'd been hunting for months slipped through his fingers one more time. The mines had cost him two ships. The decoy had cost him his positioning. And Nicole's brilliant, infuriating tactical mind had cost him everything else.

"...three... two..."

The Wayfarer vanished in a flash of displaced light, her escorts following a heartbeat later. The debris field settled into stillness, mines expended, the trap sprung and successful.

"Contact lost," Marsh said. "They're gone."

The bridge fell silent. Around them, the Ashfall drifted in its eternal dance of destruction—a graveyard that had nearly claimed three more ships, that had witnessed another chapter in a pursuit that seemed endless.

"Damage report," Thane said finally, his voice flat.

"Hammer is dead in space—life support holding but drives are gone. Vigilance has multiple hull breaches. We've lost shield generators two and four, and the forward sensor array is compromised." Marsh looked up from her console. "We're not pursuing anyone until we get repairs."

Thane nodded slowly. His hands had left white-knuckled impressions on the tactical rail. He forced himself to release it.

"Signal the Meridian's Hand. Tell Captain Voss he has operational command while we conduct repairs." The words burned coming out. "And get me a secure channel to Command. They'll want to know we lost her again."

"Sir?" Marsh's voice was carefully neutral. "What do I tell them about how she escaped?"

Thane stared at the empty space where the Wayfarer had been. Nicole Kess had outmaneuvered three warships with improvised weapons and superior tactics. Had turned his own pursuit into a trap. Had proven, once again, that she was exactly as dangerous as he'd always known she was.

"Tell them the truth," he said. "She was better than us. And unless we change our approach, she always will be."

He turned from the tactical display and walked toward his ready room. Behind him, the crew began the grim work of counting their losses.

But even as he moved, Thane's mind was already racing ahead. Nicole had won this battle. She'd escaped the Ashfall and whatever came next was beyond his immediate reach.

But she'd also shown him something. The way she thought. The patterns she used. The brilliant, ruthless logic that made her so effective.

Next time, he'd be ready.

Next time, he'd think like she did.

And next time, Nicole Kess wouldn't see him coming.

Chapter Thirty-Seven

Sacrifice

The neural interface port behind Marcus's left ear burned like someone had pressed a plasma torch to his skull. Blood trickled warm down his neck as his fingers danced across the haptic controls, vision fracturing between the console display and raw data hemorrhaging directly into his optical cortex.

The enemy vessel hung in the viewport like a predator coiled to strike. Its hull folded in on itself in geometries that made his eyes water when he tried to trace the curves. Covenant architecture—though the name felt inadequate for something so fundamentally other.

Marcus expanded the neural link, consciousness splintering. One layer revealed auxiliary control and Ensign Kellor's worried face at the doorway. Another painted the Resolute's network in cascading light—every sensor node suddenly as intimate as his own nervous system, every weapons system and shield generator responding to his touch like extensions of his will. He monitored them all simultaneously, keeping the ship's defenses coordinated while his attention pushed outward, threading through the quantum static toward the enemy vessel.

This was why Thane had dragged him from engineering. Because Marcus could do things with a neural interface that the manual said were impossible.

"Marcus, whatever you're doing, do it faster." Cara's voice crackled through the bridge comms. "They're charging weapons again."

The Covenant vessel's security protocols slammed into him like a wall of knives. Pain lanced through his synapses as alien code tried to shred his intrusion. Marcus gritted his teeth and pushed harder, letting his neural architecture flow around the defenses like water finding cracks in stone. Don't fight the system—become part of it.

His vision tripled as he breached the outer defenses. The enemy ship's interior unfolded with crystalline clarity. Energy conduits pulsed with impossible frequencies. The crew phased in and out of reality itself. And at the vessel's heart, buried deep: the reactor.

A sphere of contained nothing. Space folded so tightly that matter and energy became one, feeding the ship with power drawn from elsewhere. Marcus felt his consciousness sliding toward it, mesmerized by the elegant horror.

"Marcus!" Cara's shout snapped him back. "Fifteen seconds to weapons range!"

He sent his consciousness racing along mapped pathways, finding nodes where the reactor's control systems interfaced with the rest of the ship. The code was alien, but code followed rules. He began inserting commands—tiny fragments that would look like natural fluctuations.

Through the Resolute's systems, he felt their shields fluctuate under enemy scanning protocols. He redirected power flow, reinforcing the forward arrays while maintaining the appearance of normal distribution. The ship herself seemed to hum under his attention, responsive to his distributed consciousness in ways that should have been impossible.

The reactor's containment fields began to oscillate. Marcus fed the fluctuations carefully, nurturing cascading instability—each tiny error amplifying until the whole system screamed toward collapse.

The neural load struck him like a physical blow. Marcus gasped, blood trickling from his nose. The bridge between his mind and the enemy ship widened, data flowing both directions, and some of what flowed back wasn't meant for human consciousness.

He glimpsed fragments of memory that weren't his own. Places where light bent upward, where sky existed underneath and gravity was suggestion. Beings in more dimensions than he could perceive, their true forms bleeding through as geometric impossibilities.

"Contacts breaking off!" Marsh's voice filtered through chaos. "The Covenant ship—something's wrong with it."

Marcus felt the enemy reactor tearing itself apart through the neural link. The containment fields collapsed into recursive failure. Energy bled through from the other dimension, carrying impossible properties.

He should disconnect. Every survival instinct screamed at him to sever the bridge before the enemy ship's death throes dragged him down. But if he let go now, they might stabilize. Might recover. Might kill everyone aboard the Resolute and the civilian transports they protected.

Three hundred and forty-seven souls on those transports. Marcus had seen the manifests. Families. Children. People whose names he'd never know, whose faces he'd never see.

His father's voice echoed from memory: An engineer's duty isn't to the machine. It's to the lives depending on that machine.

Marcus held the connection. His legs had gone numb. The numbness was spreading.

Through the Resolute's systems, he felt rather than saw the crew at their stations. Cara coordinating defensive fire. Marsh managing sensors. Thane in the command chair, trusting Marcus to do the impossible one more time. All of them depending on him to keep the ship's systems running while he murdered the enemy vessel.

He poured more of himself into the alien network.

The reactor went critical.

Pain exploded through every nerve as feedback surged back. His brain tried to expand beyond his skull, consciousness stretching across vacuum. For one impossible instant, Marcus existed in three places—auxiliary control, the dying alien vessel's heart, and distributed through the Resolute's network, feeling the ship shudder when the explosion's shockwave hit.

Then the reactor detonated.

Space folded wrong where the Covenant vessel had been, reality puckering before it snapped back with light in colors Marcus couldn't name. The shockwave struck three seconds later.

He tried to disconnect.

The neural interface wouldn't release him.

Marcus clawed at the port behind his ear—when had he started bleeding from his ears?—but the connection held fast. The enemy ship's data streams were gone, replaced by something else. Information flooded in, chaotic and vast, pouring through the hole the explosion had torn in dimensional barriers.

He saw a city built from crystal and folded time, where buildings existed in past and future simultaneously. Fleets that moved by thinking through space rather than burning thrust. Equations describing reality in terms his training recognized but couldn't grasp.

His vision was definitely red now. Blood in his eyes. Marcus forced his hand back to the console, movements clumsy, and began manual shutdown. The interface fought him, confused why it was being ordered to disconnect mid-stream.

"Please," Marcus whispered. His vision was tunneling, darkness creeping inward. "Please, just let me go."

The neural link severed.

Marcus collapsed forward, forehead cracking against metal. The tide of information cut off, leaving his mind echoing with sudden silence. He tried to push himself upright. His arms wouldn't respond. Most of his body wasn't responding. Everything felt distant, like he was piloting himself through a failing connection.

"Ensign!" Kellor rushed over. "Ensign Rhelan, can you hear me?"

Marcus managed to turn his head. The room spun. Kellor's face was pale, eyes wide at whatever she was seeing.

"Did it work?" His voice sounded wrong—thin, distant. "The ship. Is it gone?"

"It's gone." She eased him down to the deck with surprising gentleness. "You destroyed it. Jesus, Marcus, what did you do?"

Good question. The memories were already fragmenting like reflections on disturbed water. He remembered reaching into the enemy ship. The reactor. Pain and light and existing in multiple places at once.

The rest was blur and blood and copper taste.

"Need to tell Thane." The words came out slurred. "The gateway. It's not just a portal. It's a weak point. Dimensional barriers are thinner here. That's why their ships are so strong in this sector. They're closer to home."

"You can tell him yourself." Kellor worked on his neural port, fingers quick and professional. Combat medic training. "Once we get you to medical bay. Can you move your legs?"

Marcus tried. His legs remained stubbornly motionless.

"Kellor to bridge. I need a medical team to auxiliary control immediately. Ensign Rhelan is down. Neural trauma, possible seizure activity, unresponsive lower extremities."

"How bad?" Thane's voice, tight with concern and discipline.

"Bad enough." She met Marcus's eyes. "He's conscious but showing severe neural feedback. The interface port is still hot—temperature readings are off the scale. I think he might have burned out some implanted circuits."

Marcus wanted to argue, but when he tried to access his neural interface, he hit a wall of static and pain. The connection that had been as natural as breathing was gone, replaced by a screaming void where his expanded consciousness used to be.

Like losing a limb he'd never known he had until it was missing.

The medical team arrived—Marcus's time sense had become unreliable. They loaded him onto a stretcher, asking questions he tried to answer through growing fog.

"Pupil response is sluggish. Neural activity is all over the place. Get him to medical now."

They carried him through corridors at a pace that made overhead lights streak into continuous lines. Marcus watched them blur, head lolling, trying to catalog the damage. Neural circuits possibly burned out—fixable with surgery. The headache splitting his skull—probably neural fatigue, nothing permanent. The fact that he couldn't feel his legs—

He didn't want to think about his legs.

Medical bay swallowed him in harsh white light and antiseptic smell. Dr. Voss appeared, dark face creased with concern, already snapping orders. Marcus tried to focus but her words kept sliding past comprehension.

"—massive neural trauma—"

"—inflammatory response—"

"—stabilize before we can assess—"

Something cold pressed his neck. An injector hissed. Sudden warmth spread through his veins, thick and syrupy. Pain receded, along with most other sensations. He felt himself floating, drifting from his body.

"Stay with me, Ensign." Dr. Voss leaned close, hand on his shoulder. The weight was the only thing tethering him to reality. "Marcus, I need you to stay conscious. Can you do that?"

"Tired." His tongue felt thick, clumsy. "So tired."

"I know. But you need to stay awake. That's an order."

Orders. Marcus understood orders. His father's orders, the academy's orders, Thane's orders. Orders were simple when the universe increasingly wasn't.

He forced his eyes open—when had he closed them?—and focused on Dr. Voss's face. She looked worried. Dr. Voss never looked worried. She'd reattached Marsh's arm under enemy fire without losing composure.

"The damage," Marcus said. "How bad?"

"I don't know yet." She glanced beyond his field of vision. "Your neural implants show severe degradation. The biological-synthetic interface is inflamed and partially rejected. Thermal damage to surrounding brain tissue. Abnormal spinal cord activity patterns. I won't lie to you, Marcus. This is serious."

"Will I walk again?"

The question came out naked and afraid. Dr. Voss's expression softened slightly.

"I don't know. We need to stabilize you first. Then we'll assess. One thing at a time, Ensign."

One thing at a time. Marcus clung to that like a drowning man clutching driftwood. First, stay conscious. Then stay alive. After that, they could worry about everything else.

He felt the medical team working through pharmaceutical haze. Time became meaningless again, stretching and compressing in ways that reminded him uncomfortably of impossible geometries in the Covenant data streams.

At some point, Thane arrived. Marcus heard his voice first.

"How is he?"

"Alive. Beyond that, I'm still evaluating."

"Can he hear me?"

"Probably. His cognitive function is impaired but not absent."

Footsteps approached. Thane's face swam into view. The captain looked older, stress lines carved deep around his eyes. Or maybe Marcus was just seeing him clearly for the first time, without the buffer of rank and professional distance.

"You did good, Marcus. The Covenant vessel is completely destroyed. The transports are safe. Three hundred and forty-seven people are alive because of what you did."

The numbers meant something. Should have meant something. Marcus tried to feel pride or relief, but the pharmaceutical fog made everything too distant.

"The data," he managed. "Before the connection broke. I saw things. Their technology. Their weaknesses. The dimensional barriers—"

"Later." Thane's hand found his shoulder, the weight grounding him. "Right now, you focus on getting better. The debriefing can wait."

"No." Marcus forced the word out, fighting against the sedatives pulling him under. "Can't wait. The information is already fading. I need to... need to record it before..."

"Marcus—"

"Please." He found Thane's eyes, held them with what little focus remained. "I saw how they move through space. Where they're vulnerable. If I don't get it out now, it'll be gone. And people will die because I was too weak to stay conscious."

Thane studied him for a long moment. Something shifted in the captain's expression—calculation giving way to reluctant understanding.

"Dr. Voss. Is there anything you can give him? Something to keep him lucid long enough to record a statement?"

"I don't recommend—"

"I'm not asking for a recommendation. I'm asking if it's possible."

Silence. Marcus felt the tension between them, the push and pull of competing priorities. Then Dr. Voss sighed.

"Twenty minutes. That's all I can give you without risking further damage. And someone needs to be recording everything he says."

"I'll do it myself." Thane pulled a chair close, produced a data tablet. "Marcus, talk. Tell me everything you remember."

The words came in fragments at first, disjointed images and half-formed concepts that Marcus struggled to articulate. The city of folded time. The equations describing dimensional travel. The weakness he'd found in their reactor design—a flaw that existed because their ships were optimized for operation near the dimensional rifts, not in normal space.

"They're stronger near the gateway because the barriers are thinner there. But that dependency works both ways. Away from the rifts, their technology becomes unstable. If we could find a way to seal the rifts, even temporarily..."

"Their ships would lose power," Thane finished. "Their weapons would fail."

"More than that. They might not be able to exist in our space at all. The dimensional bleed is what keeps them coherent. Without it, they'd... I don't know. Fragment. Dissolve. Return to wherever they came from."

Marcus felt the information slipping away even as he spoke, memories dissolving like morning frost. He pushed harder, trying to capture everything before it vanished.

"There's something else. The data I downloaded—some of it survived the neural link severance. It's in my head, buried deep. I can feel it, but I can't access it consciously. Like a file I know exists but can't open."

"We'll find a way to retrieve it," Thane said. "Neural mapping, memory extraction—whatever it takes."

"It might kill me." The words came out flat, matter-of-fact. "The damage to my neural interface... if you try to access that data through conventional means, the feedback could finish what the Covenant ship started."

Thane's jaw tightened. "Then we'll find an unconventional way."

The fog was closing in now, darkness creeping at the edges of Marcus's vision. He felt Dr. Voss's hand on his arm, heard her voice saying something about time being up.

"One more thing," Marcus said, the words barely a whisper. "Nicole. Before I... before this. We were supposed to talk. About us. About the future."

"She's on her way," Thane said. "She'll be here when you wake up."

"If I wake up."

"When." Thane's voice carried a command's weight—an order that reality itself was expected to obey. "You're too valuable to lose, Ensign. And too stubborn to die."

Marcus tried to smile. He wasn't sure if his face actually moved.

The darkness took him gently, pulling him down into dreamless depths. His last conscious thought was of Nicole—her face, her voice, the future they might still have if he could just hold on long enough to reach it.

Then even that faded, and there was only silence.

Somewhere in the depths of the Resolute, medical monitors beeped their steady rhythm. Dr. Voss worked through the night, fighting to save what could be saved, to preserve what remained.

And in the data core of Marcus's damaged neural implants, alien information waited—secrets that could win a war or destroy a civilization, locked behind barriers that might prove impossible to breach.

The price of victory, not yet fully paid.

Chapter Thirty-Eight

Recovery

The Ashfall's outer hull groaned one final time as Nicole fired the aft thrusters at maximum burn. Metal shrieked against metal—their ship tearing free from the docking clamp Marcus had just unlocked, the clamp that had held them captive while Syndicate forces converged. The ship lurched forward, and then they were free—tumbling into open space with sparks trailing behind them like a comet's tail.

"We're clear!" Cara's voice crackled through the comms, breathless with relief.

Nicole didn't respond. Her hands flew across the navigation console, steadying their trajectory, bleeding off the spin before it could turn lethal. The viewport showed nothing but stars wheeling past, faster and faster, and she anchored herself to that—to the mathematics of motion, the vectors and velocities that would keep them alive for another few minutes.

Behind her, she could hear Dex moving through the bridge, securing loose equipment, checking systems. The adrenaline of their escape was beginning to fade, replaced by the exhaustion that always shadowed close combat. They'd made it out. Somehow, impossibly, they'd—

"Nicole." Dex's voice cut through her thoughts, quieter than before, edged with alarm. "Something's wrong with Marcus."

Her hands stilled on the controls. "What?"

"He's—I think he's seizing."

The words struck like a physical blow. Nicole forced herself to finish the sequence—lock the trajectory, engage the stabilizers—even as every instinct screamed at her to turn around. Only when the ship was stable did she spin her chair.

Marcus lay on the deck plates near the emergency medical station, his body rigid, jaw clenched so tight she could see the muscles standing out in his neck. Dex knelt beside him, hands hovering uselessly above Marcus's chest like he wanted to help but didn't know how.

She was moving before conscious thought caught up, crossing the bridge in three strides and dropping to her knees beside them. Marcus's eyes had rolled back, showing only whites, and a thin line of blood trickled from his left nostril.

"How long?" Her voice came out steady despite the ice crystallizing in her chest.

"Maybe twenty seconds. Started right after you broke us free." Dex's face was pale. "The neural port—I think the interface overloaded when he severed the connection. Too much feedback."

Nicole pressed her palm against Marcus's forehead, feeling the heat radiating from his skin. The port behind his ear looked worse up close—inflamed tissue surrounding the metal socket, fluid weeping from the edges. She'd seen interface burns before, back when she'd run cargo through the Outer Colonies. Seen people die from them too, when the feedback cooked their synapses from the inside out.

"Get the medical kit." She kept her voice level, clinical. Panic wouldn't help him. "The real one, not the emergency supplies."

Dex moved immediately, and Nicole shifted her attention back to Marcus. His seizure was beginning to subside—the rigid muscles loosening incrementally—but his breathing remained shallow and rapid. She checked his pulse, found it racing and irregular. Not good. The neural feedback could trigger cardiac arrest if—

No. She couldn't think like that.

"Cara, status report." She forced herself to assess the tactical situation even as her hands moved to Marcus's neck, checking for airway obstruction.

"Nothing on sensors yet." Cara's voice came from the tactical station, tight with concentration. "But that firefight's going to draw attention. We need to jump soon."

Nicole's jaw tightened. Cara was right—staying here was suicide. But jumping with Marcus in this condition, without stabilizing him first…

"Give me five minutes," she said.

"Nicole—"

"Five minutes." She met Cara's eyes across the bridge, saw her own fear reflected there. "If we jump now and he's seizing, the stress could kill him. If Syndicate ships arrive and we're still here, we're all dead. Either way, we need him stable first."

Cara held her gaze for a long moment, then nodded. "Five minutes. Then we jump whether he's ready or not."

It was the right call. The only call. Nicole turned back to Marcus as Dex returned with the medical kit, a battered metal case that had seen better decades. She popped it open, hands moving with practiced efficiency through the contents. Neural suppressants, anti-inflammatory patches, a diagnostic scanner that might still work if they were lucky.

She pressed the scanner against the port, and Marcus's head jerked at the contact. The movement sent a spike of hope through her—reflexive response meant his nervous system was still functioning. The small display flickered to life, showing readings that made her stomach clench.

Severe inflammation. Thermal damage to the surrounding tissue. Abnormal electrical activity in the temporal lobe.

"Dex." She kept her voice calm. "I need you to monitor his breathing. If it becomes irregular or stops, tell me immediately."

"Got it." Dex shifted position, leaning close to watch Marcus's chest.

Nicole loaded a suppressant cartridge into the injector, her hands steady despite the fear coiling in her gut. The dosage was critical—too little and the seizures would continue, potentially causing permanent brain damage. Too much and she could suppress his respiratory system, stop his heart.

She checked the dosage once. Twice. A third time.

"Nicole." Cara's voice held a warning. "Four minutes."

"I know." Nicole pressed the injector against Marcus's carotid artery. "Come on. Stay with me."

The soft hiss of the injection seemed too quiet for something that might mean the difference between life and death.

Nothing happened.

Nicole counted seconds, her eyes locked on Marcus's face, watching for any sign of change. Five seconds. Ten. Fifteen—

His breathing began to slow, the rapid shallow gasps deepening into something more regular. The tension drained from his body like water from a breached hull. His eyes closed, and the line between his brows smoothed out.

"There." Nicole sat back on her heels, suddenly aware of how hard her heart was hammering. Her hands were shaking now that the immediate crisis had passed. She made fists to hide it, pressing them against her thighs.

"Is he—will he be okay?" Dex asked.

"I don't know." The honest answer felt like failure. "The suppressant should stop the seizures, but the damage to the port—that could be permanent. Could be worse than that. He needs a real medical facility."

She reached out and carefully placed an anti-inflammatory patch over the port site, then another on Marcus's temple. His color was already improving, the deathly pallor fading. That was good. That meant—

"Three minutes," Cara said quietly.

Nicole looked down at Marcus, at his unconscious face, and felt something twist in her chest. They'd escaped the Ashfall. They'd survived the Syndicate ambush. They'd made it this far.

She couldn't lose him now.

"Cara, plot a jump to the nearest safe harbor. Somewhere with medical facilities that won't ask too many questions."

"That's going to be tricky. We're in Syndicate space, and after what just happened—"

"I know what just happened. Do it anyway."

A pause. Then: "Plotting now. Threshold Station. It's independent, three parsecs out. They'll have medical facilities."

"What's the catch?"

"It's expensive. And they sell information to anyone willing to pay."

"Then we don't give them anything worth selling." Nicole gently adjusted Marcus's head, checking his airway one more time. "Dex, help me secure him. We can't have him sliding around when we jump."

Together they moved Marcus to the medical bay—a generous term for what amounted to a closet with a fold-down bunk and some basic equipment—and secured him with safety webbing. His breathing remained steady, and Nicole allowed herself a moment of relief before returning to the bridge.

Cara had their jump coordinates ready. Dex had confirmed their hull integrity. Everything was as prepared as it could be.

Nicole settled into the captain's chair and pulled up the jump initiator. "Everyone strapped in?"

"Ready," Dex confirmed.

"Ready," Cara echoed.

Nicole's hand hovered over the controls. Once they made this leap, there would be no going back—no chance to reconsider, no alternative paths. They'd be committed to Threshold Station, to whatever waited for them there.

She thought about Marcus, lying unconscious in the medical bay. About what he'd done to get them out of the Ashfall alive—pushing the neural interface past its limits, knowing what it might cost him. Buying them the time they'd needed to escape.

She pressed the initiator.

Reality shuddered. The stars outside the viewport stretched into lines of light, then collapsed into a single point. Nicole's stomach dropped as the jump drive caught hold of space-time and twisted. The sensation lasted only seconds, but it felt like falling through an infinite void.

Then the stars snapped back into focus, rearranging themselves into an unfamiliar configuration.

They'd made it. Three parsecs in an instant, putting distance between them and the Syndicate forces that had tried to kill them.

Through the viewport, Threshold Station hung in space like a barnacle clinging to reality's hull. The structure had clearly grown over decades, newer sections grafted onto older ones in a chaotic sprawl that suggested organic growth more than deliberate design. Docking spars jutted out at irregular angles, some lit with guidance beacons, others dark and presumably abandoned. The whole thing rotated slowly, creating artificial gravity through spin rather than the expensive grav-plates that larger stations used.

"We're here," Cara said, and Nicole heard the exhaustion threading through her voice. "Approach vectors incoming."

Nicole guided their ship toward one of the active docking spars, following the automated instructions. Beside her, Cara kept watch on sensors, scanning for threats that might have followed them through the jump. Behind them, the medical bay remained silent.

"Docking fees are posted," Dex said, reading from his console. "They're not kidding about the expensive part."

"Pay them." Nicole made a minor course correction, bringing them into alignment with the docking clamps. "We need medical attention more than we need credits."

The ship shuddered as the clamps engaged, magnetic locks pulling them snug against the station's hull. Nicole ran through the shutdown sequence, then stood immediately and headed for the medical bay.

Marcus lay exactly as they'd left him, secured to the bunk by safety webbing. His color looked better—less of that deathly pallor—but the port behind his ear remained swollen and angry-looking despite the anti-inflammatory patches.

She placed her fingers against his throat, checking his pulse. Strong and steady. The relief that washed through her felt disproportionate to the clinical information, and she recognized it for what it was—not just professional concern for a crew member, but something deeper.

Something she wasn't ready to examine too closely.

"I'll go ahead," Cara said from the doorway. "Find us a medical contact. Dex, you're with me. Nicole should stay with him in case he wakes up."

Nicole started to protest—she was the captain, she should be making contact—then stopped. Cara was right. Someone needed to watch over Marcus, and she knew his current condition better than anyone. "Be careful. If this place deals in information—"

"I know how to keep my mouth shut." Cara offered a tight smile. "We'll be back within the hour."

They left, and Nicole found herself alone with Marcus in the small medical bay. The space felt even more cramped with just the two of them, the walls pressing close. She could hear the ambient sounds of the station through the hull—the hum of circulation systems, the distant clang of metal on metal, voices echoing through corridors.

She pulled over the medical bay's single stool and sat, positioning herself where she could monitor his breathing.

Chapter Thirty-Nine

The Plan

The bridge held its breath.

Nicole stood at the viewport, watching Velis Prime rotate beneath them—a mottled sphere of browns and grays wrapped in atmospheric haze. Behind her, seven pairs of eyes burned into her spine, waiting for her to say something. Anything.

They'd clawed their way to orbit. Barely. The Ashfall had shrieked the entire ascent, and somewhere in the lower decks, systems were dying that she lacked the crew or materials to resurrect.

She killed the main drive and let the ship drift.

The silence that followed felt artificial, like the pause between lightning and thunder when you knew the crack was coming but couldn't predict its shape. Nicole turned and found them all watching her. Cara hunched at communications, fingers still hovering over dead switches. Garrett stood by the weapons console, arms crossed, his face carved from stone. Mira occupied the engineering station, surrounded by damage reports that scrolled endlessly across her screens. The others—four more crew members who'd pressed onto the bridge despite there being no stations for them—had been drawn by the same gravity that pulled them all toward this moment.

"We need to talk," Nicole said.

"About what?" Garrett's voice carried the edge it always did when he was trying not to shout. "About how we're dead the moment they find us? About how we just burned half our fuel getting off that rock?"

"About what happens next."

"What happens next is we run." He moved closer, his bulk imposing even in the cramped space. "We plot a jump, any jump, and we get the hell away from here before Colonel Draven and his fleet reduces us to atoms."

Nicole held his gaze. "And then what? Jump again? Keep jumping until we run out of fuel in some nowhere system and die slowly instead of quickly?"

"At least we'd be alive long enough to die slowly."

"That's not living. That's just prolonging the inevitable."

Cara's voice sliced through the tension, softer than Garrett's but no less firm. "Nicole, he has a point. We can't fight the Dominion Navy. We're one ship with a skeleton crew and half our systems gutted. They have—" She gestured helplessly at her console. "They have everything."

"I know what they have."

"Do you?" Mira spoke up from engineering, not bothering to hide the exhaustion in her voice. "Because from where I'm sitting, it looks like suicide. Hull integrity is down to sixty-three percent. We've got fires in sections seven and twelve that the suppression systems can't reach. The port stabilizer is gone—just gone, Nicole. We're held together by hope and welding scars."

Nicole nodded slowly. "I know. I know all of it. But running doesn't change any of that. Every jump we make, every system we push, we're just buying time with a currency we don't have."

"So what are you suggesting?" Ko asked. He leaned against the bulkhead, his face pale from the burns he'd sustained in the escape. The youngest of the crew who'd followed her into mutiny, barely past his certification exams when this had all started. "That we turn around and fight? We'd last maybe thirty seconds against a Dominion cruiser."

"Maybe thirty seconds is enough."

The words hung in the air like smoke. Nicole watched the idea move through them, saw it register on their faces—confusion first, then comprehension, then a strange marriage of horror and curiosity.

"You're serious," Garrett said. Not a question.

"I'm serious."

"Explain." Ko's voice cracked slightly. "How does thirty seconds of fighting accomplish anything except getting us killed?"

Nicole stood, her body protesting the movement. Everything hurt—her ribs, her shoulder, the cut on her forehead that Mira had sealed but not healed. She moved to the center of the bridge, where they could all see her.

"We're not going to win a straight fight," she began. "Garrett's right about that. But we don't need to win. We need to survive long enough to matter."

"Matter to who?" Cara asked.

"To everyone watching."

Nicole summoned a holo-display, her fingers dancing across the interface until it showed a star map of the surrounding sectors. "Right now, we're a footnote. A ship that mutinied, a crew that disobeyed orders. The Dominion will hunt us down, kill us, and file a report that says we were traitors who got what we deserved. And the official story will be the only story because we're not around to contradict it."

She zoomed in on their current position, then expanded the view to show the trade routes and comm relays that webbed through this part of space. "But what if we don't run? What if we force a confrontation here, where we can at least control some of the variables? We broadcast everything—every order, every transmission, every piece of evidence we pulled from the Ashfall's secure database before we left. We scatter it across every open channel—civilian, military, commercial. We make it impossible to bury."

"The Dominion will jam us," one of the other crew members pointed out. "Military-grade ECM. They'll flood the channels with interference."

"They'll try. But jamming is indiscriminate. It takes time to set up properly, and while they're doing it, we're transmitting. Some of it will get through—maybe just fragments, but fragments are enough. People can piece together the truth from fragments."

Garrett shook his head. "Even if we broadcast our reasons, even if some of it survives the jamming, it won't change the outcome. Draven will still blow us apart."

"Maybe. But at least the story won't end with us. At least other crews, other ships, will know what we stood against. And maybe—maybe—some of them will start asking questions."

"Questions won't save us," Garrett said flatly.

"No. But they might save the next crew who sees what we saw. The next captain who has to choose between following an illegal order and facing a firing squad."

The bridge fell silent. Nicole could feel the weight of their doubt, heavy as gravity. She couldn't blame them. What she was proposing wasn't strategic brilliance—it was desperation dressed up in purpose. But desperation was all they had left.

Ko spoke again, his voice steadier now. "What did we see, Captain? I mean—what's the actual thing we're fighting for? I need to hear it said plain."

Nicole met his eyes. "We saw Command order the bombardment of civilian settlements. We saw them target refugee convoys. We saw Admiral Kessik sign off on tactics that violate every treaty the Dominion has signed, and we saw Colonel Draven execute crew members who objected. We saw a command structure that's rotted from the inside, that's decided winning the war is more important than remembering what we're supposed to be protecting."

She paused, letting the words settle. "We're fighting because someone has to. Because if we don't draw a line somewhere, there won't be anything left worth defending by the time this war is over."

"Pretty speech," Garrett said, but his tone had shifted—less confrontational, more contemplative. "But it doesn't answer the practical question. How do we survive long enough to make it matter?"

"We use what advantages we have. We're already in orbit—stable orbit, which means we're not burning fuel. Draven's fleet will come in hot, aggressive, looking to end this quickly. They'll expect us to run or surrender. Instead, we maintain position and we keep transmitting. Every minute they spend setting up their approach, repositioning to cut off escape vectors, coordinating their jamming—that's time our message is getting out."

"And when they get in range?" Cara asked.

"We fight with everything we have. We won't win, but we can make it costly. Make them work for it. The longer we last, the more time the transmission has to spread."

Mira frowned. "They won't need to get close. They can bombard us from distance. We're stationary—we're the easiest target they'll ever have."

"Which means we need to make ourselves more complicated than we look." Nicole turned to her. "What's our status on the port stabilizer?"

"Completely torn off in the escape."

"What about the starboard?"

Mira consulted her screens, scrolling through damage reports. "Functional, but stressed. I wouldn't trust it in any kind of aggressive maneuvering."

"I don't need aggressive. I just need it to work long enough to matter." Nicole pulled up another display, this one showing the Ashfall's deck plans. "The stabilizers have independent power supplies, right? Capacitors that charge from the main reactor?"

"Yeah, but they're designed for station-keeping and minor corrections. You can't—" Mira stopped, realization dawning. "Oh. Oh no. You want to overload it."

"Could it work?"

"Technically? Maybe. You'd have to disable the safety limiters, route all available power to the capacitors, and then trigger a cascade failure at exactly the right moment. The explosion would be—" She paused, calculating. "Significant. Comparable to a torpedo, maybe more if you time it during a weapons discharge."

"But we'd only get one shot," Garrett said. "And we'd have to let them get close enough for it to matter. Which means letting them into weapons range. Which means they get to shoot at us first."

"Yes."

He stared at her. "You're talking about using us as bait."

"I'm talking about using everything we have. Our position, our orbit, our damaged systems—all of it. We make them think we're helpless, lure them in close where they think they can intimidate us into surrender, and then we hit them with everything at once. If we can damage even one of their ships, it changes the optics. It shows we're not just rolling over. That we're willing to stand and fight even when we shouldn't be able to."

"Or it shows we're willing to die for nothing," Garrett countered.

"Is it nothing?" Nicole asked. "Really? Because from where I'm standing, it's the only thing that matters. We've already lost our ship, our careers, our futures. Running won't get any of that back. But this—this at least means something. This at least says we chose our fate instead of letting it choose us."
