"Which is currently linked to ours," Marcus agreed. "So maybe we use that. Maybe we stop treating this as something that happened to us and start treating it as something we can work with."

Nicole studied him, and Marcus could see her processing—running simulations, testing variables, searching for the flaw in his reasoning. It was the expression she wore during tactical planning, when she was trying to see all angles of a problem simultaneously.

"Show me," she said.

"Show you what?"

"The influence. What it feels like. How you can tell when it's happening." She moved closer, dropping her arms from their defensive position. "If we're going to do this—if we're going to treat this as partnership instead of infection—then I need to understand what you're experiencing. What the integration actually means."

Marcus nodded. "It's subtle. That's the first thing. You're looking for dramatic changes—sudden impulses, foreign thoughts—but it doesn't work like that. It's more like... a gentle pressure. The way a current in water might nudge you in one direction without you realizing you're drifting."

He reached out, not quite touching her, letting his hand hover between them. "Right now, I can feel it encouraging this conversation. Not controlling what I say, but making the act of communication feel more rewarding. Reinforcing the neural pathways associated with connection and trust."

Nicole's eyes flickered to his hand, then back to his face. "So it wants us to reconcile."

"It wants stability. Conflict creates stress hormones that interfere with optimal integration. But the desire to reconcile—that's mine." He met her gaze. "That's always been mine."

She was quiet for a moment, processing. Then, slowly, she reached out and took his hand. Her fingers were warm against his, grounding him in a way the fungal network couldn't replicate.

"Okay," she said. "Let's say I accept the framework. Let's say I agree that compromised autonomy is the human condition and we're just aware of one more variable now. That still leaves the question of what we do with it."

"We use it." Marcus felt the certainty crystallize as he spoke. "The network contains information we need. Coordinates. Patterns. Understanding of the Remnant technology that the Directorate would kill to possess. Instead of treating this as a violation we're recovering from, we treat it as a resource we control."

"Can we control it?"

"I think so. The influence works both ways. The network responds to my neurochemistry just as it shapes it. When I focus, when I direct my attention toward specific queries, I can feel information organizing itself in response." He squeezed her hand. "It's like learning to use a tool that's also using you. Mutual adaptation."

Nicole considered this, her tactical mind clearly running scenarios. "If the Directorate finds out what you can access—"

"They'll want me more than ever. I know." Marcus released her hand, stepping back to lean against the wall. "But they already wanted us dead or captured. This doesn't change the threat level. It just changes our capabilities."

"Does it change your capabilities enough?" Nicole crossed her arms again, but the gesture felt different now—thoughtful rather than defensive. "The Directorate has fleets. Resources. A surveillance network that spans half the explored sectors. We have one damaged ship and a crew that's barely holding together."

"We have something they don't." Marcus tapped his temple. "Two hundred million years of accumulated pattern recognition. Chemical signatures of every hostile environment the network ever encountered. And coordinates for places even the Remnant considered significant."

Nicole's expression sharpened. "You found something in there. Something specific."

"I found references. Partial coordinates. A location the network associates with... I don't know how to describe it. Origin, maybe. Source. The place where things began." Marcus felt the fungal tendrils pulse with something that might have been recognition. "If I can complete the pattern, if I can access the full coordinates—"

"We'd have leverage." Nicole's voice was low, calculating. "Something valuable enough to negotiate with. Or valuable enough to use ourselves."

"That's the idea."

She studied him for a long moment, her expression unreadable. Then something shifted in her posture—a relaxation, a release of tension she'd been holding since she walked through the airlock.

"You know this is insane," she said. "Trusting alien biochemistry to guide us toward something valuable instead of something catastrophic."

"Completely insane." Marcus allowed himself a small smile. "But we were already in insane territory the moment we set foot on that installation. At least now we're insane with a plan."

Nicole laughed—a short, surprised sound that seemed to catch even her off guard. "A plan that involves letting fungal networks influence our decision-making while we search for ancient alien coordinates based on chemical memories we can't independently verify."

"When you put it that way, it sounds perfectly reasonable."

She shook her head, but the smile stayed. "Alright. We do this together. You access the network, try to complete those coordinates. I keep the ship running and the crew from panicking. And we both watch for signs that the influence is pushing us somewhere we don't want to go."

"Deal." Marcus extended his hand again, this time formally. "Partners."

Nicole took it, her grip firm. "Partners."

They stood there for a moment, hands clasped, the weight of everything they'd discussed settling into something that felt almost like certainty. Then Nicole released him and moved toward the airlock.

"I'm lifting the quarantine," she said. "You're no more dangerous in the common areas than you are sealed in here. And we need you on the bridge."

"Why?"

Nicole paused at the door, looking back over her shoulder. "Because while you were processing two hundred million years of chemical memory, our sensors picked up something interesting. Three ships, Directorate signatures, holding position at the edge of the system."

Marcus felt his pulse quicken. "They found us."

"Looks like it. But they're not moving. Not attacking, not hailing, just... waiting." Her expression hardened into command mode. "Whatever they're planning, I want you with me when we find out."

The airlock cycled open, and Nicole stepped through. Marcus followed, leaving the quarantine cell behind.

Whatever came next, they'd face it together.

And somewhere in the depths of his networked consciousness, ancient coordinates slowly resolved into clarity.

Chapter Forty-Five

Allies

The communications relay on Deck Nineteen had been decommissioned for six months, which made it perfect. Nicole settled cross-legged on the dust-filmed floor, her portable terminal balanced on her knees, watching encryption protocols cascade across the screen. The relay might be officially offline, but its hardline connections to the station's backbone remained intact—veins and arteries still pulsing with data. Someone with the right access codes could thread a signal through seventeen different nodes before it ever brushed against Bastion's main grid.

Someone like her.

The first response arrived faster than she'd anticipated. Text only, routed through a dead drop in the Kessler Belt. RECEIVED YOUR PACKAGE. INTERESTED. SPECIFY LOCATION.

Viktor Crane. She'd worked with him twice during the expansion surveys, back when Halstrom-Reed still pretended to care about ethical resource extraction. He'd been a freight captain then, hauling independent cargo between the settled zones and the outer territories. Smart enough to know when to ask questions. Smart enough to know when not to.

She typed quickly. STATION BASTION. COORDINATES ATTACHED. SITUATION CRITICAL. NEED DISCRETE TRANSPORT CAPACITY AND PERSONNEL.

The cursor blinked. Thirty seconds. A minute. She forced herself to breathe steadily, counting the intervals between each exhale. The relay hummed around her, power conduits still thrumming in the walls despite the official shutdown. Somewhere above, the station rotated through its perpetual cycle, artificial gravity holding everything in its precise, calculated orbit.

THREE WEEKS MINIMUM. MAYBE TWO IF I BURN HOT. WHAT'S THE CARGO?

Nicole's fingers hesitated over the keys. How did you compress something like this? How did you distill the weight of what she'd learned, what Marcus had discovered, what Venn was becoming, into a message short enough to encrypt?

EVIDENCE. PEOPLE. BOTH VOLATILE.

Another pause, longer this time. She pictured Viktor in whatever ship he commanded now, calculating risk against reward, wondering if this justified the fuel expenditure alone. The Dead Sectors had their own economy, their own logic. Trust was a commodity measured in prior transactions.

DOUBLE MY USUAL RATE. HALF UP FRONT.

She'd expected that. AGREED. FIRST TRANSFER IN TWELVE HOURS. CREDENTIALS TO FOLLOW.

The connection terminated. Nicole sat in the darkness, letting the tension drain from her shoulders. One contact secured. She needed at least three more before this could work. Before she could extract Marcus from this station without Halstrom-Reed tracking every microsecond of his departure.

Before whatever was happening to Venn metastasized beyond containment.

The second message went out through a different routing entirely. This one bounced through civilian traffic, riding the wake of entertainment feeds and personal correspondence, camouflaged among ten thousand other signals hemorrhaging from Bastion every hour. The Council maintained no official presence in corporate space, but they had representatives everywhere. You just had to know which frequencies they monitored.

She'd learned those frequencies from Marcus, back when he'd still been trying to convince her that someone in the Dead Sectors gave a damn about what happened in places like this.

TO WHOM IT CONCERNS. HALSTROM-REED CONDUCTING UNAUTHORIZED RESEARCH. XENO-BIOLOGICAL MATERIALS. HUMAN SUBJECTS. ORIGIN POINT: ARCHAEOLOGICAL SITE PLANET DESIGNATION HR-7743. REQUEST FORMAL INQUIRY.

It was almost laughably polite, phrased in the careful bureaucratic language the Council pretended to operate under. But the substance was there. Human subjects. That phrase alone would trigger mandatory review protocols under the Outer Territories Charter, assuming anyone in the Council hierarchy actually cared about enforcement this far from the core systems.

Nicole didn't harbor much faith in institutional oversight. But she needed every pressure point she could manufacture.

She sent three more variations of the same message, each one routed differently, each one carefully worded to trigger different alarm systems. Redundancy was everything when you were trying to light fires in a vacuum.

* * *

The commissary on Deck Eight served food until twenty-three hundred hours, mostly to accommodate the rotating shift schedules that kept Bastion operational around the clock. Nicole bought a meal pack—something with protein and vegetables that had never existed in any recognizable form—and found a table in the corner where she could watch the entrance.

Torin arrived exactly on time, which was so characteristic it almost made her smile. He wore civilian clothes instead of his usual technician's uniform, but the cautious way he moved through the space marked him clearly. He'd never learned to fake casualness.

"You're going to get us both reassigned to waste recycling," he said, sliding into the seat across from her. He didn't look at her directly, instead focusing on the meal pack he'd brought.

"I'm already on selective duty suspension," Nicole said. "What are they going to do, suspend me harder?"

"Fair point." He opened his pack, prodding at something that might have been engineered grain. "I got your message. The one that wasn't really about requisition forms."

"And?"

"And I ran the thermal analysis you requested." He pulled out a data chip, turning it between his fingers. "Unofficially. Using processing time allocated to structural integrity monitoring, so no one's going to notice the cycle drain for at least a week."

Nicole felt something tight in her chest loosen. "What did you find?"

"Heat signatures. Deck Forty-Seven, subsections J through M. Concentrated around what the official schematics list as equipment storage. Except equipment storage doesn't generate heat patterns like this." He slid the chip across the table. "Whatever they're running down there, it's active. And it's pulling enough power to register on the secondary grid."

She pocketed the chip without looking at it. "Can you get me access?"

"To Forty-Seven?" Torin finally met her eyes, his expression somewhere between incredulity and concern. "Nicole, that section is locked down. Command-level authorization only. I can fake a lot of things, but walking through those doors isn't one of them."

"I'm not asking you to come with me. I'm asking if you can open them."

He was quiet for a long moment, mechanically eating his meal. Around them, other station personnel cycled through their routines—tired engineers, bored security staff, researchers too absorbed in their terminals to register human presence. Background noise. The constant static of institutional life.

"There's a maintenance window every forty-eight hours," Torin said finally. "Four minutes when the biometric sensors reset. It's not much. But if you were already inside the access corridor when the window opened, and if someone had hypothetically created a temporary credential ghost in the system…" He trailed off. "Why am I helping you do something this catastrophically stupid?"

"Because you've seen the numbers too," Nicole said quietly. "You've noticed the resource allocation anomalies. The energy consumption patterns that don't match any registered research protocols. You're too good at your job not to have questions."

Torin set down his utensils carefully, precisely aligned beside his meal pack. "I have a family. Back in the core systems. A husband. Two kids. This job pays for their housing, their education, everything. I can't afford to be heroic."

"I'm not asking for heroism. I'm asking for four minutes and a ghost credential. After that, this all falls on me."

He closed his eyes briefly. "When?"

"Tomorrow. Twenty-two hundred hours."

"You're insane."

"Probably." Nicole stood, gathering her trash. "But I need to know what they're doing down there. I need to see it with my own eyes before I can move forward with the rest."

"The rest of what?"

She didn't answer, just walked away, leaving Torin alone with his questions and his complicity.

* * *

Her quarters felt smaller every time she returned to them. Nicole sat on the edge of her bunk, the dim emergency lighting casting everything in shades of amber and shadow. She pulled out her personal terminal—the real one, not the sanitized corporate device they'd issued her—and accessed the archived files she'd been forbidden to touch.

Marcus's research logs. The ones he'd compiled before Venn, before the ruins, before everything went to hell.

His face appeared on the screen, younger than she remembered, less worn. This recording was from nearly a year ago, back when the expedition was still in the planning phases. Back when it was all theoretical risk and academic excitement.

"—don't think people really understand what we're looking at," Marcus was saying, his hands gesturing as he spoke. "Not just ruins. Not just archaeological remnants. We're talking about a civilization that might have spanned this entire sector. The scale alone would rewrite everything we think we know about pre-human expansion."

The recording jumped. Different session, different day. Marcus looked more haggard here, shadows pooled under his eyes.

"—asked me what I thought the future looked like. Honestly? I think it looks like contact. Real contact, not just picking through dead cities and wondering what happened. There's something out there, Nicole. I can feel it in the data, in the patterns. And when we find it…" He smiled, that slightly crooked expression she'd always found both endearing and frustrating. "When we find it, everything changes. Everything we've built, every assumption about humanity's place in the universe. It all shifts."

Nicole paused the playback. That smile. God, she missed that smile. The optimism behind it, the genuine belief that discovery meant progress, that knowledge was inherently valuable.

He'd been so wrong about what discovery would bring.

She let the recording continue. This segment was darker, filmed in what looked like temporary quarters somewhere on the planet's surface. Marcus was closer to the camera, his voice lower.

"—know you disagree with me about corporate oversight. And you're probably right. Halstrom-Reed doesn't care about the archaeological significance. They care about what they can extract, what they can monetize. But here's the thing, Nicole—someone's going to do this work. Someone's going to explore these ruins, catalog what's there. I'd rather it be us, with at least some ethical framework, than a pure profit operation that treats xeno artifacts like ore deposits."

He rubbed his face, exhaustion evident in every line.

"I guess what I'm trying to say is… I believe in the future. I have to. Otherwise, what's the point? Why spend your life digging through the past if you don't think it means something for what comes next? We're building something out here. Maybe it's messy. Maybe it's compromised. But it's still building. Still moving forward."

The recording ended. Nicole sat in the silence, feeling the weight of those words like physical pressure. Marcus had believed in the future right up until the present destroyed him. Until whatever was in those ruins took root in Venn and turned her into something that shouldn't exist.

She wondered if he still believed. If whatever remained of Marcus Voss in that quarantine cell still held onto those convictions, or if everything had burned away in the transformation.

* * *

The response from the Council came eighteen hours later, routed through the same civilian channels she'd used to send the initial message. Nicole almost missed it, buried among routine status updates and automated reports.

YOUR INQUIRY HAS BEEN LOGGED. INVESTIGATIVE TEAM DISPATCHED TO SECTOR. ESTIMATED ARRIVAL FORTY DAYS. MAINTAIN DOCUMENTATION. PROTECT WITNESSES.

Forty days. A lifetime in the current situation. But it was something. A commitment, however distant, that someone outside this station's gravity well was paying attention.

She composed responses to two more contacts—a former security officer who'd left Halstrom-Reed under suspicious circumstances, and a data broker in the outer territories who owed her a favor from the Kessler surveys. Neither was guaranteed, but redundancy remained the strategy. Cast enough lines and something would hold.

The maintenance window was in six hours. Six hours to prepare, to plan, to accept that what she was about to do would cross every line she'd spent her career carefully maintaining.

She pulled up Marcus's archived logs one more time, scrolling to a recording she'd watched so many times she could recite it from memory. This one was different from the others—personal, not professional. He'd made it for her, though she hadn't known it at the time.

His face filled the screen, closer than the other recordings. More intimate.

"Nicole." His voice was soft, almost shy. "I know you'll probably never see this. Or if you do, it'll be because something went wrong and you're going through my files trying to understand what happened. So I guess this is my chance to say what I've been too careful to say in person."

He paused, running a hand through his hair—that nervous gesture she knew so well.

"I believe in the future. I've said that before, probably too many times. But what I haven't said is that when I picture that future, you're in it. Not just as a colleague or a friend. As something more. As the person I want to build things with."

Nicole felt her throat tighten, even though she'd heard these words a dozen times before.

"I don't know where this expedition is going to take us. I don't know what we're going to find or what it's going to cost. But I know that whatever happens, I want you there. I want you to see it with me. The discoveries, the challenges, all of it."

He smiled—that crooked, hopeful smile that she'd been trying to forget.

"So this is me, officially on the record, saying: when this is over, when we've figured out what's in those ruins and what it means for humanity, I want to talk about what comes next. For us. For whatever we could be."

The recording ended. Nicole closed the file and sat in the darkness, feeling the weight of promises made before anyone understood what the ruins would demand.

Marcus had wanted a future with her. Had believed they could build something together, something that mattered.

Now he was locked in a quarantine cell, fighting to remember who he'd been before the infection. And she was preparing to break into a restricted sector to gather evidence against the corporation that employed them both.

Not the future either of them had imagined.

But maybe—just maybe—the only future worth fighting for.

Her terminal chimed. A new message, routing through channels she didn't recognize, encryption protocols she'd never seen before. She almost deleted it as suspected malware.

Then she saw the header: FROM THE ARCHIVE PRIME. FOR NICOLE BASTION ONLY.

Her blood ran cold. Archive Prime. The location Marcus had mentioned, the coordinates buried in his fragmented memories. The place the Remnant considered sacred.

She opened the message.

WE HAVE BEEN WATCHING. WE HAVE BEEN WAITING. THE TIME FOR OBSERVATION HAS ENDED. COME TO US. BRING THE ONE WHO CARRIES OUR MEMORIES. THE PATH WILL OPEN WHEN YOU ARE READY.

YOU HAVE BEEN CHOSEN. DO NOT DELAY.

Nicole stared at the screen, her heart hammering against her ribs.

The Remnant weren't just ancient history. They weren't just extinct memories carried in Marcus's altered consciousness.

They were reaching out.

And they wanted her to come.

Chapter Forty-Six

The Enemy's Face

The conference room Frost had chosen was small—the kind where tactical discussions unfolded off the record. Thane stationed herself near the door while Frost arranged datachips across the table's surface in deliberate rows.

He'd changed since their last conversation. The casual confidence replaced by something harder. Honed to an edge.

"You're going to want to sit," Frost said.

"I'm fine standing."

He touched the table's control surface. The room's lighting dimmed, and a projection field hummed to life. A three-dimensional schematic materialized above the table.

Thane's breath caught.

The structure was massive. Geometric. Wrong. It hung in the projection like a crystallized thought, all angles and recursive patterns that seemed to shift when she wasn't looking directly at them.

"This is ARCHON," Frost said.

Thane drifted closer despite herself. The structure's core pulsed with a light that registered as white in the projection but suggested colors her eyes couldn't quite process. "What am I looking at?"

"The next phase of human civilization. Or the end of it." Frost rotated the image with a flick of his fingers. "ARCHON is a weapon, yes. But calling it that is like calling a star a campfire."

The schematic expanded, subsystems branching into subsystems, complexity breeding complexity until Thane couldn't track the individual components. She recognized some base technology. Resonance field generators. Neural interface substrates. Quantum-state processors operating at scales that should have collapsed under their own impossibility.

Her tactical training kicked in, cataloging specifications even as her gut rejected what she was seeing. "Where did this come from?"

Frost summoned personnel files. Research logs. Budget allocations buried beneath seventeen layers of compartmentalization. "Bastion's been building it for eight years. Started as a defensive project after the Threshold Incursion. The admiralty wanted something that could neutralize hostile forces before they reached inhabited space."

"And it evolved."

"It metastasized." His voice carried an edge she hadn't heard before. "The original design was meant to collapse enemy vessels from the inside out. Molecular-level disintegration keyed to specific resonance signatures."

The projection shifted. Thane watched a simulation—a fleet of ships whose hulls simply ceased to exist as waves of distortion passed through them. No explosions. No debris. Just absence where vessels had been.

Her stomach tightened. She'd fought in enough battles to understand destructive power. This wasn't power. This was erasure.

"The original design had limitations," Frost continued. "Range. Power requirements. Targeting precision. It needed line of sight and conventional military deployment."

"Past tense."

"They fundamentally altered it." The projection changed. Now the weapon's core revealed something organic. Neural pathways. Synaptic bridges. Connection points suggesting an interface between human consciousness and the weapon's targeting systems.

Thane felt cold seep through her chest. "Someone has to pilot it."

"Not pilot. Merge with." Frost's expression remained carefully neutral. "The current iteration requires a human consciousness as its processing core. The targeting algorithms are too complex for conventional AI. They need intuition. Pattern recognition. Strategic decision-making in real-time."

Thane stared at the neural interface components, trying to imagine what it would be like to merge with something that could reach across space and unmake matter by thought alone. Her training had prepared her for tactical decisions under fire. For weighing lives against strategic objectives. Not for this. Not for becoming the weapon itself.

"How many people know about this?" she asked.

"Inside Bastion? Maybe two hundred with full clearance. Another thousand working on component systems without glimpsing the whole picture." He gestured at the projection. "Outside Bastion? That's the question."

He summoned intelligence reports. Communication intercepts. Pattern analysis suggesting information leaks across multiple channels. "Someone's been feeding data to external parties for at least eighteen months. We don't know who. We don't know what they've shared."

Thane studied the intercept patterns. Careful tradecraft. Dead drops and cutouts designed to make tracking impossible. "The separatist cells."

"Or someone who understands that ARCHON represents an existential threat to any group Bastion decides is problematic." Frost's jaw tightened. "Which right now includes a lot of people who just want out from under military occupation."

The projection rotated slowly. Thane's eyes kept returning to the neural interface, that web of connections where human thought would fuse with annihilation. "The pilot. Who is it?"

"They haven't selected one yet. The neural compatibility requirements are extreme. They've been testing candidates for three years. No successes." Frost pulled up communication logs, heavily redacted. "But they're accelerating the timeline. My source suggests they're planning to activate within the month. Which means either they've found their pilot or they're willing to risk a suboptimal neural match."

Thane turned away from the projection. Walked to the room's small viewport and stared out at Bastion's bulk. Somewhere in those corridors, they'd built something that could reach across the stars and kill with a thought. And they'd convinced themselves this was necessary.

Her mind was already working through implications. If ARCHON deployed, every tactical principle she'd learned became obsolete. No maneuver could counter it. No defense could stop it. War would become simple extermination, decided by whoever controlled the weapon.

"Why show me this?" she asked.

"Because I need you to understand what we're trying to prevent. The separatist cells. The attack on Bastion. All of it traces back to ARCHON. People aren't just afraid of military occupation. They're afraid of being erased from existence if they resist too loudly."

"So the attack is self-defense."

"The attack is desperation." Frost deactivated the projection. The room's normal lighting returned, harsh after the display's glow. "But it's going to get a lot of people killed. And it's not going to stop ARCHON. If anything, it'll accelerate deployment. Give the admiralty the excuse they need to use it."

Thane turned back to face him. Her brother's face flashed through her mind—the certainty in his eyes the last time they'd spoken. Before his final mission. Before whatever had killed him. "What do you want from me?"

"Help me find another way. You have access I don't. Connections. Your military record gives you credibility I lost when I started asking uncomfortable questions."

"You want me to commit treason."

"I want you to prevent a catastrophe." The intensity in Frost's voice cut through his careful control. "ARCHON isn't a solution. It's an extinction-level mistake that our grandchildren won't forgive us for making."

Thane's training screamed at her to report this. Follow protocol. Trust the chain of command. But she thought about the battles she'd fought. The enemies who'd become allies later. The conflicts resolved through diplomacy after both sides had bled enough to realize peace was preferable.

None of that would be possible in a universe where ARCHON existed.

"I need to verify this," she said.

"You think I haven't verified it?" Frost pulled out a datachip. "I've spent eighteen months confirming every detail. This isn't speculation. It's documentation."

He held out the chip. Thane stared at it without moving.

"Taking that makes me complicit," she said.

"Standing by makes you responsible."

The chip sat in his palm. Small. Innocuous. The kind of thing that passed hands a thousand times a day on Bastion.

Thane reached out and took it.

* * *

Thane walked the perimeter of Officer's Block Seven, the datachip burning in her pocket like it might physically ignite. The corridors were quiet this shift. She moved through pools of light and shadow, trying to impose order on her thoughts.

She'd built her career on trust in the chain of command. Her brother had believed that too. Had died defending that principle on a mission she still couldn't access full details about.

Now Frost was telling her the hierarchy had created something that violated every principle they claimed to defend.

She found herself in an observation gallery overlooking one of Bastion's main hangar bays. Below, crews moved around corvettes and frigates undergoing maintenance. The careful choreography of military readiness. Everything precisely coordinated toward the mission of defense.

Except now defense had evolved into something else.

She pulled out her comm. Stared at it like it might contain answers.

She could report Frost. Turn over the datachip and let proper channels handle the investigation. That was protocol. That was what her training demanded.

But if Frost was telling the truth, the people she'd report to were the ones building ARCHON. They weren't going to investigate themselves.

She thought about her brother. The last conversation before his final deployment. He'd seemed troubled. Had started to say something about his mission parameters, then stopped. Told her some questions didn't have good answers, only necessary ones.

What if he'd known something? What if he'd seen something that made him question the whole structure they served?

What if that's why he was dead?

When she opened her eyes, she was already moving. Not toward Raines's office. Not toward security. Toward the restricted data archives.

If her brother had known something, there might be traces in his final mission reports.

* * *

The archives occupied a reinforced section of Bastion's core. The reading room was empty when she arrived. Just rows of terminals and the faint smell of recycled air.

She accessed her brother's service record. Missions scrolled past. Deployments to contested sectors. Operations that had earned him commendations and his eventual assignment to special operations.

His final mission was listed with minimal detail. Convoy protection through the Margin Belt. Three vessels lost to "hostile action by unknown forces." Her brother's vessel among them. No survivors. Wreckage showed signs of catastrophic structural failure consistent with weaponry that exceeded expected separatist capabilities.

Thane had memorized every word searching for closure. But now she hunted for different patterns.

She dug deeper. Found requisition orders for the convoy's cargo. The paperwork was deliberately vague, but she recognized the coding structure. Specialized research equipment. Neural interface components. Quantum processing arrays that matched specifications from Frost's briefing.

Her brother had been escorting ARCHON components.

The realization hit like decompression.

Her hands trembled on the terminal's interface. She pulled up after-action reports. Found inconsistencies she'd missed before. Response times suggesting foreknowledge of the attack. Wreckage patterns that didn't match the reported weapons signatures.

Someone inside Bastion had set up that convoy. Had marked her brother and his crew for elimination.

And she'd spent the last year hunting the people who might have been trying to stop it.

* * *

Thane returned to her quarters, locked the door, and sat in darkness. The datachip lay on the desk beside her brother's service medal. She'd carried that medal through every deployment since his death.

Now it felt like mockery.

She thought about Frost's offer. It sounded rational. Strategic. The kind of solution her training prepared her to execute.

But it meant betraying everything the medal represented. Every oath she'd sworn. Every principle her brother had died defending—or thought he was defending.

Except he'd been protecting a lie. Had died for people who viewed him as expendable.

She picked up the medal. Felt its weight. Her brother's face looked out from the small photo embedded in its backing. Serious. Certain. The expression of someone who believed absolutely in what he was fighting for.

"What would you do?" she whispered to the photograph. "If you'd known what they were building? If you'd known they'd sacrifice you to protect it?"

The photograph offered no answers. It never did.

Thane set the medal down beside the datachip. Two objects. Two competing loyalties. Her brother's memory and the truth about what he'd died protecting.

She'd spent her entire career believing that service meant sacrifice. That the chain of command existed because individual soldiers couldn't see the full picture. That trust in leadership was the foundation that held everything together.

Now that foundation had cracked, and she could see what lay beneath.

ARCHON. A weapon that could unmake matter with a thought. A weapon that required a human mind as its processing core. A weapon that her brother had died transporting without ever knowing what it would become.

The rage that had fueled her pursuit of the fugitives suddenly felt hollow. She'd been hunting people who might have been trying to prevent exactly this. Might have been trying to stop Bastion from building something that violated every principle the military claimed to defend.

Her comm chimed. A message from Command—routine status request, nothing urgent. She stared at it without responding.

If she reported Frost, ARCHON would continue. The separatists would be crushed. Anyone who questioned the project would be silenced. And eventually, inevitably, someone would give the order to activate. Would merge their consciousness with annihilation and reach across the stars to erase whoever the admiralty decided was a threat.

If she helped Frost, she became a traitor. Everything her brother had died for—everything she'd believed about honor and duty and service—would mean nothing.

Unless it had always meant nothing. Unless the principles were just words used to justify whatever the powerful wanted to do.

Thane picked up the datachip and turned it in her fingers. Frost's evidence. Proof of what ARCHON really was and what it would cost.

She thought about Nicole Varra, the woman she'd been hunting. The fugitive who'd broken into Bastion and stolen secrets worth dying for. At the time, Thane had seen her as a criminal. A threat to order and stability.

Now she wondered if Nicole had seen something that Thane was only beginning to understand.

She inserted the datachip into her personal terminal and began to read.

The files were extensive. Technical specifications she couldn't fully comprehend. Communication logs between project directors. Budget allocations hidden beneath layers of bureaucratic obfuscation. And at the center of it all, the truth about what ARCHON would require to function.

Not just a pilot. A sacrifice. The neural merger was one-way. Whoever activated the weapon would never return to their own consciousness. They would become ARCHON, their humanity dissolved into the targeting algorithms, their identity erased in service to the machine.

They weren't building a weapon. They were building a tomb.

Thane closed the files and sat in darkness, the medal cold in her palm.

Her brother had believed in duty. Had died believing his sacrifice meant something.

Maybe it was time to make his sacrifice actually mean something.

She opened a secure channel—one of the emergency frequencies she'd memorized during her tactical training, designed for situations where normal command structures couldn't be trusted.

Her fingers hesitated over the interface.

Then she began to type.

TO FROST. I'VE READ THE FILES. I'M IN. TELL ME WHAT YOU NEED.

The message vanished into the network. Thane sat in the darkness, waiting for a response, knowing that whatever came next would change everything.

Her brother's medal gleamed in the faint light from her terminal. She picked it up one more time, pressing it against her chest.

"I'm sorry," she whispered. "But I think this is what you would have wanted."

The comm chimed with Frost's reply.

And Thane began to plan.

Chapter Forty-Seven

Preparation

Nicole stood in the armory bay while maintenance crews cycled through their final checks on the Valiant's weapon systems. The smell of lubricant and ozone saturated the air, familiar and oddly comforting. Through the observation window, she watched the artifact chamber three decks below, the alien construct pulsing with that steady rhythm that had grown almost hypnotic over the past weeks.

They'd finally cracked it. After months of fragments and guesswork, the artifact had yielded its secrets like a flower responding to the right combination of light and shadow. The weapon systems it described surpassed anything human engineering had achieved, but the principles were translatable. Barely.

Marcus materialized at her elbow, datapad clutched in one hand, what looked like burn marks streaking his sleeve. "The fabrication teams are finishing the interface nodes. Kove says they'll be ready for installation in six hours."

"That's cutting it close." Nicole turned from the window, her tactical instincts already calculating risk factors. "If even one integration point fails during the engagement—"

"Then we adapt. Like we always do." He pulled up a schematic on the pad. "But look at this. The power distribution network mirrors the artifact's internal structure almost perfectly. It's as if whoever built that thing knew someone would eventually need to replicate its function."

Nicole studied the branching paths of energy conduits, the way they split and rejoined in patterns that seemed almost organic. The design triggered something in her tactical training—asymmetric warfare principles, distributed networks, systems engineered to survive partial failure. "Or they built dozens of these things, and this was always meant to be a blueprint. A weapon you could reproduce even if you'd lost everything else."

"Either way, we're not just understanding it anymore. We're making it work." Marcus swept through several screens, each displaying different aspects of their modifications to the ship's systems. "Thane's people have been running simulations constantly. The theoretical output is staggering."

"Show me the constraints," she said, because theoretical output meant nothing without understanding the breaking points. In her experience, the difference between victory and catastrophic failure usually lived in the margins no one wanted to discuss.

Marcus pulled up a series of graphs. "Life support stays at full capacity. Navigation gets reduced to minimal automated functions during firing sequences. We'd lose recreational gravity in non-essential sections, but combat areas maintain full functionality." He zoomed in on a cluster of red warning indicators. "The real limitation is thermal. We can fire in short bursts, but sustained output would cook us from the inside out."

Nicole took the pad from his hands, scanning the temperature curves. The numbers told a clear story: devastating power, but only in carefully measured doses. "How long between bursts?"

"Ninety seconds minimum for the cooling systems to cycle. Longer if we push close to maximum output."

"So we get one good shot per engagement window. Maybe two if we're lucky." She handed back the datapad. "Then we make sure we don't waste them. Every shot has to count."

"That's where your tactical planning comes in. I can give you the weapon." Marcus met her eyes, and she saw the same mix of determination and unease she felt. "You tell me when to fire it."

Nicole turned back to the observation window, watching the artifact's steady pulse. Down in that chamber lay the key to their survival—or the catalyst for their destruction. She'd spent weeks studying its patterns, cross-referencing its data with known Dead Sectors encounters, building a mental model of how these pieces fit together. But models weren't reality, and reality had a way of exposing assumptions you didn't know you'd made.

"Walk me through the integration one more time," she said. "I need to understand exactly what happens when we activate the weapon systems. Every stage, every potential failure point."

Marcus moved to stand beside her at the window. "The artifact's core generates a focused energy pulse. We've built interface nodes that can capture and channel that pulse through the ship's existing weapon grid. Think of it like… like we're turning the Valiant into a lens. The artifact provides the light, we focus it, and the target gets burned."

"And the cooling issue?"

"The artifact doesn't generate heat—it generates this energy that our systems convert to useful work, and that conversion process produces massive thermal output. The more efficiently we can convert, the less heat we generate." He pulled up another schematic. "Kove's been optimizing the conversion efficiency, but there's a hard limit based on our ship's thermal capacity."

Nicole processed this, integrating the technical constraints with tactical possibilities. "What about partial activation? Could we use lower power output for sustained fire instead of maximum power bursts?"

"Theoretically, yes. Though at lower power, I'm not sure it would be any more effective than our conventional weapons." Marcus tilted his head, considering. "Why?"

"Because maximum output makes us predictable. The Dead Sectors have proven they can adapt to threats—if they see us charging up for a devastating shot, they'll know to scatter or take defensive positions during our cooldown period." She traced patterns on the window's surface, her mind working through scenarios. "But if we can vary our output, keep them uncertain about our capabilities…"

"We maintain tactical flexibility." Marcus nodded slowly. "I'd need to run tests, but the principle is sound. The artifact responds to input intensity—feed it less, get less output, but also less thermal stress."

"How quickly can you vary output mid-engagement?"

"The interface nodes respond almost instantly. The ship's systems take a few seconds to adjust, but nothing that would leave us vulnerable." He was warming to the idea now, she could see it in his expression. The engineer's natural inclination toward elegant solutions meeting practical tactical requirements. "We could even pulse our fire, keep constant pressure on a target instead of these long gaps where we're sitting ducks."

Nicole felt something click into place. This was why she'd insisted on understanding the weapon's mechanics rather than accepting a simple "point and shoot" briefing. The technical details revealed tactical options that wouldn't be obvious from a surface-level explanation. "Get me firing profiles for variable output by the time we reach the command deck. Include thermal curves and recovery times for each power level."

"That's twenty minutes of analysis minimum—"

"Then we'll spend twenty minutes in analysis before we plan the engagement." She turned from the window, her decision made. "I'm not going into combat with a weapon I don't fully understand. Not when hundreds of lives depend on me using it correctly."

Marcus studied her for a moment, then nodded. "Engineering lab three is empty. We can use their systems to run the modeling."

They left the armory bay together, moving through corridors that hummed with preparation. Crew members hurried past on various errands, the ship's tempo elevated to pre-combat readiness. Nicole noted the tension etched in people's faces, the way conversations died as she approached. The tactical officer, coming to save them or lead them to destruction, depending on whether her plans worked out.

Engineering lab three was indeed empty, its workstations waiting in standby mode. Marcus activated the main terminal while Nicole sealed the door behind them. The privacy wasn't strictly necessary, but she worked better without an audience watching over her shoulder.

"Starting the simulation runs now," Marcus said, his fingers dancing across the controls. "I'm modeling five different output levels from twenty percent to maximum. Each one gets evaluated for damage potential, thermal load, and recovery time."

Nicole moved to stand beside him, watching data populate across multiple screens. Numbers became curves, curves became projections. The artifact's alien power filtered through human engineering, transformed into something they could use. Something they could understand, at least partially.

"There." She pointed to a graph showing sustained output at forty percent capacity. "That's our harassment fire. Enough punch to force evasive action but low enough thermal load that we can maintain it indefinitely."

Marcus highlighted the curve, pulling up associated metrics. "Sustained fire for up to four minutes before we'd need to throttle back for thirty seconds. Against typical Dead Sectors ships, it would take multiple hits to achieve a kill, but it would keep them defensive."

"Which means controlling their positioning. Making them react to us instead of executing their own tactics." Nicole's mind raced ahead, seeing the engagement space as a three-dimensional chess board. "What about this spike here? The seventy percent output level?"

"That's the sweet spot for single-target elimination. Enough power to punch through their defenses with one or two shots, but thermal recovery in under a minute." He zoomed in on the relevant section. "If you're looking to remove priority targets quickly, this is your tool."

"And maximum output?"

"Ship-killer. At full power, we could theoretically crack a capital-class vessel in one shot." Marcus pulled up the thermal curve, and Nicole saw the problem immediately—a vicious spike that pushed their cooling systems to the absolute limit. "But we pay for it with a three-minute recovery period where we're thermally maxed and vulnerable."

"So maximum output is a last resort or an alpha strike against a critical target when we know we'll have cover during recovery." Nicole committed the profiles to memory, already imagining how they'd integrate with her tactical plans. "This is good. This gives me options."

She became aware that they'd been standing close together, both leaning over the same terminal, for several minutes. Close enough that she could smell the faint scent of the industrial soap they all used, mixed with something uniquely him—probably whatever compound had scorched his sleeve earlier. The observation was clinical, tactical even. You learned to read the people you worked with, to anticipate their reactions, to know when they were stressed or confident or uncertain.

Marcus had become easier to read over the past weeks, or perhaps she'd simply gotten better at interpreting his particular signals. The way he tilted his head when considering a problem. The slight smile when a solution clicked into place. The intensity of focus when something genuinely interested him, like now, as he worked through the firing profiles she'd requested.

"We should get this to the command deck," she said, stepping back to create professional distance. "Thane needs to see these options before we finalize the engagement plan."

"Agreed." Marcus transferred the analysis to his datapad, then paused. "For what it's worth, I think you're approaching this the right way. Too many tactical officers would just want to know which button makes the biggest explosion. You're thinking about the whole engagement."

"That's the job."

"No, that's you being good at the job. There's a difference." He headed for the door, then glanced back. "I'm glad you're the one planning this operation, Nicole. Whatever happens out there, I know you'll give us the best possible chance."

The words settled into her chest, unexpected and warming. Professional respect from a colleague whose judgment she trusted. That shouldn't have mattered as much as it did, but exhaustion and stress had a way of making simple kindness feel profound.

"Try to keep us from melting when I call for maximum output," she said, aiming for lightness. "I'd hate to win the battle and lose the ship to thermal failure."

"I'll do my best to keep you in one piece." Marcus smiled—that slight, genuine expression she'd learned to recognize. "Both of you. Ship and officer."

They walked to the command deck together, the corridors quieter now as the crew settled into their final preparations. Nicole felt the familiar pre-combat calm descending over her thoughts, sharpening her focus, pushing everything else to the periphery.

The command deck was alive with activity when they arrived. Thane stood at the central tactical display, running through engagement scenarios with his senior officers. Kove had set up a portable engineering station nearby, monitoring the artifact integration in real-time. Sera occupied the communications array, coordinating with their allies across the system.

Everyone doing their part. Everyone synchronized toward a single purpose.

"Commander." Thane looked up as Nicole approached. "Marcus briefed me on your variable output proposal. It's elegant."

"It's practical." Nicole moved to the tactical display, pulling up the firing profiles Marcus had generated. "We've been thinking about this weapon like artillery—big shots, long reloads. But it doesn't have to work that way. We can use it as a precision tool, a suppression system, or a ship-killer depending on what the situation demands."

Thane studied the profiles, his tactical mind clearly processing the implications. "This changes our engagement planning significantly. We'll need to redistribute our escort positioning to account for the new flexibility."

"I've already started working through scenarios." Nicole called up her preliminary tactical layouts, the engagement space rendered in glowing lines and markers. "The key insight is that we don't have to commit to a single approach. We can adapt mid-battle, switch between harassment and elimination based on how the enemy responds."

"Show me."

For the next hour, they worked through engagement scenarios together—Nicole's tactical planning integrating with Thane's strategic vision, Marcus contributing engineering constraints, Kove flagging potential system failures. It was the kind of collaborative planning that only worked when everyone trusted each other's expertise.

And somehow, despite everything they'd been through, that trust had formed.

Nicole stepped back from the display as the final scenario resolved, watching her team—her people now, in every way that mattered—move through their preparations with practiced efficiency. They'd come together from different backgrounds, different loyalties, different reasons for being here. But they'd become something more. A crew that could fight as a single unit.

"We're ready," she said, and meant it.

Thane met her eyes across the tactical display. "Then let's finish this."

The ship-wide comm crackled to life, Sera's voice carrying the calm authority that belied the magnitude of what they were about to attempt.

"All hands, this is your ten-minute warning. Final preparations complete. Battle stations in ten minutes."

Nicole watched the crew respond—the quick movements, the last checks, the quiet words exchanged between people who might not see each other again after today. This was the moment she'd been trained for. The moment everything came down to.

Marcus appeared at her side, datapad tucked away, his expression focused. "Artifact integration is stable. Engineering confirms all systems ready for combat operations."

"Good." She turned to face the main viewport, where the stars waited with their ancient indifference. Somewhere out there, their enemies were preparing too. Making their own plans. Believing they knew what they were facing.

They were wrong.

"Whatever happens out there," Marcus said quietly, "we've done everything we could to prepare. The rest is up to the universe."

"The universe doesn't decide battles," Nicole replied. "People do. And we're ready."

The ten-minute warning ended. Battle stations sounded across the ship—that familiar klaxon that transformed civilians into warriors, passengers into crew. Nicole moved to her tactical station, her hands finding the controls with the ease of long practice.

The artifact pulsed in its chamber three decks below, ancient power waiting to be unleashed.

And somewhere in the darkness ahead, their enemies were about to learn what the Dead Sectors had taught them.

How to survive. How to fight. How to win.

Chapter Forty-Eight

Contact

Marcus stood in the artifact chamber on the Valiant's lowest deck, watching Nicole work through the translation sequence that had consumed her for the past eight hours. The object—a meter-tall cylinder of black material covered in symbols that seemed to shift when viewed directly—pulsed with a faint blue luminescence.

"How much longer?" he asked.

Nicole didn't look up from her workstation, where streams of data cascaded across three holographic displays. "Another ten minutes if the pattern holds. But Marcus, what I'm seeing here—these aren't just coordinates. There's a warning embedded in the structure."

"A warning about what?"

"Still working on that part."

Marcus studied the artifact, feeling the familiar unease it had triggered since they'd recovered it from the derelict station in the Ashford Belt two days ago. The cylinder had been found in what appeared to be a command center, surrounded by the desiccated remains of whatever crew had operated the station before it went dark. The station itself had been one of hundreds scattered through the belt—or what they'd thought was a belt. Nicole's preliminary scans suggested the asteroids weren't natural formations at all, but structures so ancient that ice and stone had accumulated over their surfaces across millennia.

The Dead Sectors were revealing themselves to be far more than empty space where civilizations had vanished. They were graveyards with the bodies still present, just buried under the accumulated dust of ages.

"I'm heading back to the bridge," Marcus said. "Call me the moment you have something concrete."

"Will do. And Marcus?" Nicole finally looked up, her expression grave. "Be ready to run. If I'm right about what this thing is, activating it might announce our presence to something we really don't want to meet."

"Noted."

Marcus took the elevator up three decks, his mind already turning to the logistics of a fast exit if needed. The Valiant was well-equipped for a research vessel—a legacy of the corporate-funded expedition program that had commissioned her construction. The galaxy's major corporations had learned the hard way that scientific research in the Dead Sectors required defensive capabilities. Too many unarmed survey ships had vanished without trace.

The bridge was quiet when he arrived, just Aria at the sensor station and Kael at the helm, running routine diagnostics. The viewscreen showed the Ashford Belt stretching away into the distance, thousands of tumbling shapes silhouetted against the distant sun.

Marcus settled into the tactical station and pulled up the long-range sensor logs. Nothing had changed in the past hour—they were alone in this remote system, exactly as they'd intended when they'd chosen it for the artifact analysis. No traffic, no colonies, no corporate listening posts. Just the Valiant and the secrets they were trying to uncover.

The proximity alarms shattered the calm.

"Contact!" Aria's hands flew across her console. "Three ships dropping from fold-space. Range: two hundred thousand kilometers and closing."

Marcus felt his pulse quicken as threat indicators bloomed across the holographic display. Three heavy cruisers, each one bearing crimson and black markings he'd helped design five years ago. The lead vessel's designation flashed: OS Retribution.

"Obsidian Solutions," he said, keeping his voice level despite the cold settling in his gut. "They found us."

"How?" Kael twisted in his seat. "We went dark before entering the system. No transponder, no comm signals, nothing."

"Doesn't matter right now." Marcus pulled up the weapons grid, his hands moving through activation protocols he hadn't executed in three years. The muscle memory returned with unsettling ease—neural pathways reactivated by proximity to violence. "They're here, and they want the artifact. Kael, get us between them and the belt. We need the asteroids for cover."

"On it." Kael's fingers danced across the helm controls, and the Valiant's engines fired. Through the viewscreen, space wheeled as they came about.

Marcus opened a ship-wide channel. "All hands, this is Marcus. We have incoming hostiles. Secure stations and prepare for evasive maneuvers." He switched to Nicole's private channel. "Nicole, how long until that translation sequence is complete?"

Her voice came back tense. "Still ten minutes. Whatever you're going to do up there, buy me time."

"Trying." He closed the channel and studied the tactical display. Three heavy cruisers, the lead commanded by someone he knew too well. Commander Voss had been Marcus's training officer at Obsidian Solutions, back when Marcus still believed corporate security work meant protecting people rather than crushing them. He'd learned better after the Epsilon Station incident, when Obsidian had ordered him to fire on civilian protesters. He'd refused, resigned his commission, and spent the next three years trying to bury those memories in academic research.

Now his past had caught up with him, and Voss would show no mercy to a deserter who'd stolen corporate secrets when he left.

"Second wave," Aria called out. "Four more signatures—frigates and destroyers. They're spreading out, trying to box us in."

Seven ships. Marcus ran the calculations automatically, tactical training surfacing despite his years away from combat. The Valiant was fast and better armed than most research vessels, but against a full Obsidian task force, they were hopelessly outmatched. Their only advantage was terrain.

"Time to firing range?" he asked.

"Sixty seconds," Aria said.

Marcus activated the main cannon, watching the power flow stabilize. "Target the lead cruiser's sensor array. Fire when ready."

The Valiant's primary weapon spat coherent light across the void. Marcus watched the beam lance toward the Retribution, saw the enemy ship's shields flare and buckle. Not a killing blow, but enough to blind them temporarily.

"Nice shooting," Gregor's voice crackled through from engineering. "Though if we keep drawing this kind of power, I can't guarantee the fold-drive will be ready when we need it."

"Noted. Keep it charging." Marcus brought up the secondary weapons grid, his hands moving with practiced efficiency despite the three-year gap in his combat experience. It troubled him how easily the old skills returned, how smoothly he slipped back into the mindset that treated other ships as targets rather than vessels full of living crew. He'd left Obsidian specifically to avoid this—the casual acceptance of violence, the reduction of human lives to tactical calculations.

But if he hesitated now, his own crew would die. Sometimes the only choice was which compromise with your principles you could live with.

The bridge shuddered as the Obsidian ships returned fire.

"Shields at seventy percent," Aria reported. "They're coordinating their fire patterns. Professional work."

"It's Voss," Marcus said. "He always is."

The Ashford Belt loomed closer on the viewscreen—thousands of irregular shapes tumbling through space in chaotic orbits. Some were as large as small moons, ancient structures buried under accumulated ice and stone.

"Entering the belt in ten seconds," Kael warned. "Hold on."

The Valiant plunged into the asteroid field. Space around them became a maze of stone and ice, obstacles appearing and vanishing with dizzying speed. Kael threaded the ship between collisions with reflexes Marcus could only admire, spinning and diving through gaps that seemed impossibly narrow.

"They're following," Aria said. "The cruisers are holding back, but the lighter ships are in pursuit."

"Good." Marcus pulled up the aft cannon controls. "Give me manual targeting on weapons array three."

He watched the pursuing frigates on his display, waiting as they navigated the field with more caution than Kael's reckless weaving. The Obsidian pilots were competent and professional—they'd been trained the same way Marcus had. Follow protocol, minimize risk, complete the mission. That professionalism would be their weakness here.

A destroyer emerged from behind a massive ice asteroid, weapons already charging. Marcus fired first, a spread of kinetic rounds that tore through the ship's forward shields and punctured its hull. Atmosphere vented in a crystalline spray, and the destroyer tumbled away, engines sputtering.

Marcus felt his stomach tighten as he watched the wounded ship drift. How many crew? Thirty? Forty? Some of them might have been people he'd trained with, served alongside. He forced the thought down. Later. He could wrestle with the moral cost later, if they survived.

"One down," he said. "Six to go."

"Marcus." Nicole's voice carried an edge he rarely heard from her. "The artifact's responding to the combat. Energy signatures are spiking."

"Is that a problem?"

"I don't know yet. Just—try not to die before I figure it out."

Another frigate appeared on his sensors, flanking from above. Marcus designated it for Aria. "Can you handle that one?"

"Already on it." The Valiant's secondary cannons cracked, and precision fire carved through the frigate's engine housing. The ship went dark, drifting on momentum alone.

"Two down," Aria said. "But the cruisers are repositioning. They're setting up a crossfire pattern at the belt's far edge."

Marcus pulled up the tactical overview. She was right—Voss was using the asteroids to herd them, forcing them toward a killing field where the heavy cruisers could bring full firepower to bear. A classic encirclement maneuver.

"Kael, can you get us out of the belt before we reach that convergence point?"

"Not without exposing our stern to those cruisers. We'd be sitting targets."

Marcus studied the display, mind racing through options. They couldn't go forward into the trap, couldn't go back through the pursuing ships, couldn't exit the belt without presenting an easy target. Unless—

"There." He highlighted a massive asteroid near the center of the field, a tumbling mountain of nickel-iron ore three kilometers across. "Take us there. Put that rock between us and the cruisers."

"That's a dead end," Aria said. "We'll be trapped."

"Trust me."

Kael banked hard, engines screaming. The pursuing frigates adjusted course, closing in. Let them think the Valiant was cornered.

"Gregor," Marcus said, "I need you to vent plasma from the port coolant system. Make it look like we took a hit."

"That's going to make engineering very uncomfortable very quickly," Gregor replied. "But I can do it."

"Do it now."

The Valiant shuddered as superheated plasma erupted from the port side, a bright gout of energy that would read on sensors as catastrophic damage. Marcus brought the ship around the asteroid's bulk, using the rock as a shield.

"Cutting main engines," Kael said. "We're drifting."

Through the viewscreen, the three pursuing frigates approached cautiously. They'd seen the plasma vent, would be scanning for confirmation of the kill. Standard procedure—verify target destruction before reporting success.

Marcus waited, counting seconds, watching the frigates close to optimal range. His finger hovered over the weapons grid.

"Now," he said.

The Valiant's engines roared back to life. Every weapon on the ship fired simultaneously—cannons, missiles, kinetic impactors. The frigates, caught completely off-guard, took the full salvo. One exploded outright, reactor breaching in white fire. The other two tumbled away, hulls breached, systems failing.

Marcus's hands were steady on the controls, but his mind recoiled from what he'd just done. A sucker punch, executed with cold efficiency. Three ships disabled or destroyed, perhaps two hundred crew dead or dying. He'd killed before in his Obsidian years, but never so calculatedly, never with such ruthless precision.

The old Marcus—the one who'd resigned his commission over moral objections—would have hesitated. Would have searched for another way. But that Marcus would have gotten his crew killed in the past five minutes.

Chapter Forty-Nine

The Fall

Nicole's hands swept across the weapons console with practiced precision, each toggle and trajectory calculation a reflex honed through years of combat. The Valiant lurched as another plasma volley slammed against the failing shields, the impact shuddering through the deck plates beneath her boots.

"Starboard shield at twenty-eight percent," Aria's voice came tight with concentration. "Port side at fifteen and dropping."

Marcus stood at the center of the bridge, his silhouette dark against the tactical display's harsh glow. The three Obsidian Solutions cruisers circled them like predators around wounded prey, their movements coordinated with the precision only his old company could achieve.

Nicole armed another missile cluster, her finger hovering over the release trigger. "Targeting solution locked on the lead cruiser's engine array."

"Hold fire," Marcus said. "They're not shooting to kill yet. They want the ship intact."

The comm crackled to life, a voice Nicole recognized from countless briefing recordings. Commander Voss, Marcus's former second-in-command, his tone carrying the false warmth of an old friend extending an invitation.

"Marcus, this doesn't have to end badly. Surrender the Valiant and we'll discuss terms. You know I've always respected you."

Marcus's jaw tightened. Nicole watched his reflection in the tactical screen, saw the weight of recognition settle across his features. Years of shared combat, shared command decisions, shared victories—all rendered meaningless by whatever Obsidian Solutions had offered Voss.

"Voss," Marcus said, activating the comm. His voice was level, but Nicole caught the undercurrent of betrayal beneath it. "You're still following Kade's orders?"

A pause. When Voss responded, Nicole detected something almost like regret threading through his tone. "Following opportunity. The dead sectors are changing everything. You saw what was in Bastion. That kind of discovery doesn't belong in independent hands."

"So you'd take it by force?" Marcus's knuckles whitened against the console edge. "We built something better than this. Something that meant something."

"We built a company," Voss replied. "Nothing more. And companies adapt or die. I learned that from you, Marcus. You taught me to see the larger tactical picture, to understand when principles become luxuries we can't afford." Another plasma volley rocked the bridge, punctuating his point. "I'm giving you the same chance you gave me at Sigma Station—the opportunity to survive with honor intact. Don't make me take you down the hard way."

Marcus's expression hardened. "You want a lesson in tactical thinking? Here's one: some principles aren't luxuries. They're what separate us from pirates." He severed the channel with deliberate finality.

Voss's response came immediately, all false warmth stripped away. "So be it. Target their engines. Take them alive if possible."

Another impact rocked the bridge. Nicole seized the console edge, her display flickering as power fluctuated. The Valiant was tough, built for extended operations in hostile space, but three heavy cruisers represented firepower she couldn't match indefinitely.

"They're targeting our engine couplings," Aria called out. "Trying to disable, not destroy."

Nicole's tactical display painted the pattern clearly now. Precision strikes against propulsion and communications, the kind of surgical assault designed to leave a ship dead in space but salvageable. Obsidian Solutions wanted the Valiant's cargo hold intact, wanted the artifacts and data they'd recovered from Bastion's depths.

Marcus moved to her station, leaning close enough that only Nicole could hear. "Can you give me a window? Somewhere I can punch through their formation?"

She ran the calculations, her mind processing vectors and acceleration curves. The lead cruiser had overextended its position slightly, creating a narrow corridor between it and its nearest support vessel.

"There," she said, highlighting the gap on his command display. "But we'd take heavy fire threading that needle. Shields won't hold."

"They don't need to hold," Marcus said. "They just need to get us through."

He straightened, his voice carrying across the bridge with calm authority. "Kai, I need maximum acceleration on my mark. Aria, transfer all remaining shield power to forward arc. Nicole, clear that corridor."

Nicole's fingers flew across the weapons console. They had twenty-three missiles remaining in their depleted magazine—most of the supply expended during the running battle that had dragged them to this moment. She calculated spread patterns and detonation timing, building a firing solution that would force the cruisers to choose between maintaining formation or taking evasive action. Eighteen missiles to carve the corridor. Five held back for emergencies.

"Solution locked," she said.

"Fire."

The Valiant's missile ports bloomed with explosive light as the payload launched. Nicole tracked their trajectories, watched them streak toward the two nearest cruisers. The lead cruiser's point defense systems engaged immediately, creating a defensive curtain that detonated half the missiles before they could reach optimal range.

But the other half got through.

Explosions blossomed against the cruiser's hull, shields flaring brilliant white. The second cruiser veered off, breaking formation. The corridor widened.

"Now," Marcus said.

Kai slammed the engines to full thrust. The acceleration pressed Nicole back against her seat as the Valiant surged forward, diving through the gap. Plasma fire streaked past the viewscreen in brilliant arcs. One shot clipped their forward section—shields absorbing most of the impact but letting enough through to send sparks cascading from the navigation console. Aria ducked away as a panel exploded, smoke flooding the bridge.

"Forward shields at nine percent," Aria coughed, waving smoke away. "Port and starboard collapsing."

They were through the formation now, open space ahead. Nicole allowed herself one breath of relief before her tactical display showed the cruisers already wheeling to pursue.

"Engineering section," Marcus called over the internal comm. "Status on fold-drive?"

The response came back static-laced and grim. "Another ten minutes minimum to cycle the capacitors. We don't have the power distribution we need."

Nicole watched the pursuing cruisers close the distance. Ten minutes might as well be ten hours.

"They're charging weapons," she reported. "No more warning shots. They're done trying to disable us."

Marcus's hand gripped the back of her chair. Nicole saw him processing options, running through the limited choices they had left.

"Nicole," he said quietly. "That asteroid cluster at bearing two-seven-three. How dense?"

She pulled up the scan data, analyzing the field sprawled across their forward starboard quadrant. "Dense enough to be dangerous. Mixed composition, chaotic orbits. Not something I'd recommend threading at combat speed."

"But possible?"

She met his eyes and understood what he was asking. Not whether it was safe, but whether it gave them a chance. "Possible, yes."

"Kai," Marcus said, louder now. "Plot an intercept course for that asteroid field. Maximum safe speed."

"That's not actually safe," Kai protested, but his hands were already moving across the helm controls.

"Nothing about this is safe," Marcus said. "But it's better than staying in open space with three cruisers on our tail."

The ship banked hard to starboard, the artificial gravity compensating sluggishly enough that Nicole felt her stomach lurch. The asteroid cluster swelled on the viewscreen ahead—thousands of rocks ranging from pebble-size to chunks large enough to crush the Valiant completely.

They had perhaps three minutes before the cruisers closed to optimal firing range. Three minutes to reach the relative safety of the asteroid field or die trying.

"They're adjusting pursuit vectors," Nicole reported. "Splitting up to cut off our exit routes once we're in the field."

Smart, she thought. Voss had learned from Marcus's tactics, understood how to counter his preferred strategies. The cruisers spread out as they approached the cluster's perimeter, positioning themselves to bracket any escape vector the Valiant might attempt.

"Entering field in thirty seconds," Kai announced. "Everyone hold on to something."

Nicole gripped her console as the first asteroids flashed past the viewscreen. Kai's hands moved constantly, the ship twisting and banking through impossibly narrow gaps. Asteroids streaked close enough that she could see their pitted surfaces, ancient rock scarred by eons of collisions.

Behind them, the three cruisers followed.

"They're maintaining pursuit," Aria called. "Lead cruiser's matching our trajectory."

The Valiant dove deeper into the field. Nicole's weapons console showed minimal targeting solutions—the field was too dense, too chaotic for effective firing. They were committed to Kai's flying now.

A proximity alarm shrieked. Kai jerked the helm hard to port. An asteroid the size of a transport shuttle scraped past their starboard side, close enough that Nicole heard the grinding screech of rock against hull.

"Starboard hull breach," Aria reported, her voice tight. "Sections fourteen through sixteen. Emergency bulkheads holding."

Marcus moved to the tactical console beside Nicole, pulling up a three-dimensional map of the asteroid field. His fingers traced paths through the chaos.

"There," he said, pointing to a dense cluster of larger asteroids orbiting a central mass. "We use that gravitational pocket to slingshot around. It'll put us behind their formation."

Nicole ran the numbers. "We'd need to get close enough that the tidal forces would stress the hull beyond safe parameters."

"Safe's already behind us," Marcus said. "Kai, plot the course."

The Valiant dove deeper, the rocks growing larger and more densely packed. Kai adjusted their approach vector with minute corrections, setting up the gravitational slingshot that would whip them around the massive central asteroid's far side.

"Twenty seconds to insertion point," Kai called.

Nicole felt the gravitational pull before the sensors registered it. The Valiant's hull groaned under the stress. Warning klaxons wailed across every station.

The ship whipped around the asteroid's far side, the viewscreen showing ancient rock so close that Nicole could count individual craters. Then they were past, hurtling back through the field at a vector that would bring them up behind the nearest cruiser.

"Weapons range in fifteen seconds," Nicole reported, her fingers already warming up the forward plasma cannons. "Targeting their engine section."

The cruiser appeared on her targeting display, shields oriented forward to protect against asteroid impacts, its aft section exposed. A perfect shot. Nicole locked the solution, waited for the range indicator to shift green.

"Fire."

Twin plasma bolts lanced across space, punching through the cruiser's minimal rear shielding and boring into its main engine core. The ship's propulsion array erupted in secondary explosions. It began to tumble, attitude control thrusters insufficient to maintain stability in the dense asteroid field.

"First one's down," Nicole said, allowing herself a moment of satisfaction.

But the other two cruisers were already adjusting, coming around with weapons hot. Nicole checked her remaining ammunition stores—five missiles and degraded plasma cannon capacity. The numbers were grimly inadequate.

The Valiant burst clear of the asteroid field's far edge. Open space ahead but no cover remaining. The two cruisers emerged moments later, their formation tight and coordinated.

"Fold-drive status," Marcus called to engineering.

"Still charging. Five minutes."

Five minutes to hold off two heavy cruisers with depleted weapons and failing shields. Nicole didn't need to run the calculations to know it was impossible.

"Incoming fire," Aria warned.

The first plasma volley struck before Kai could evade. The Valiant's port side erupted with impacts, the remaining shields collapsing entirely. Nicole felt the ship shudder as hull breaches cascaded across multiple sections.

"Shields are gone," Aria reported, her voice cracking. "We're taking direct hits."

Marcus moved to the center of the bridge, his expression focused despite the chaos erupting around them. "All power to engines. We run until the fold-drive's ready."

Another volley hammered the ship. A console exploded behind Nicole, showering her with sparks. She heard Kai cry out as shrapnel caught his arm, but he kept his hands on the helm, kept them moving.

"Two minutes to fold-drive," engineering called.
