They weren't going to make it. Nicole could see it in the tactical display—the cruisers closing, their weapons cycling for another barrage. Two minutes was an eternity when you were dying by the second.

"Nicole." Marcus's voice cut through the alarms. "The remaining missiles. Can you target their weapon arrays?"

She checked her targeting computer. Five missiles against two cruisers. Not enough to destroy them, but maybe enough to buy time. "I can try to blind them. Force them to cease fire long enough for their sensors to clear."

"Do it."

Nicole programmed the missiles for maximum dispersal, setting their warheads to detonate in a pattern designed to overwhelm sensor systems rather than breach hulls. It was a desperate gamble—buying seconds at the cost of their last offensive capability.

"Missiles away."

The warheads streaked toward the cruisers, detonating in brilliant cascades of light and electromagnetic interference. For a precious moment, the incoming fire stopped as the cruisers' targeting systems struggled to reacquire.

"Ninety seconds to fold-drive."

They were going to make it. Against all odds, against every tactical calculation, they were actually going to—

The plasma bolt came from nowhere.

Later, Nicole would understand that one of the cruisers had switched to manual targeting, a human gunner compensating for the sensor interference through skill and intuition. In the moment, all she knew was a lance of brilliant blue-white energy punching through the bridge's forward viewport.

The bolt struck Marcus in the center of his chest.

Time stopped. Nicole watched him stagger backward, his hands rising to touch the wound that had appeared where his heart should be. Smoke curled from scorched flesh and melted uniform fabric. His eyes found hers—confused, surprised, as if he couldn't quite believe what had happened.

Then he fell.

"MARCUS!"

Nicole was across the bridge before she knew she'd moved, catching him as he collapsed. His weight bore them both to the deck. She cradled his head in her lap, her hands pressing uselessly against the wound that had already taken everything that mattered.

"Nicole..." His voice was barely a whisper, blood flecking his lips. "The ship... get them out..."

"Don't talk." Tears streamed down her face, falling onto his scorched uniform. "Just hold on. We're almost to fold-drive. Just hold on."

But she could see the truth in his eyes. The light fading. The recognition that there wasn't enough time, had never been enough time.

"I'm sorry," he said. "I wanted... more time..."

His hand found hers, fingers intertwining one last time. She felt him squeeze, felt the strength leaving him with each passing heartbeat.

"Nicole." His voice was so faint now she had to lean close to hear. "It was worth it. All of it. Because of you."

His eyes closed. His hand went slack in hers.

And Marcus Vane was gone.

Nicole knelt there, holding him, as the battle raged around her. She heard Aria screaming something about incoming fire. Heard Kai calling for orders. Heard the ship dying around her in a symphony of alarms and explosions.

None of it mattered.

Then something inside her broke. Or maybe it finally came together.

She laid Marcus's body gently on the deck and stood. Her hands found the weapons console without conscious thought. The targeting computer was still struggling with interference, but she didn't need it. She could see the cruisers through the shattered viewport, could feel their positions like extensions of her own body.

"Fold-drive ready," engineering called. "We can jump!"

"Not yet," Nicole said. Her voice was ice. "Kai, bring us about. Face them."

"Nicole, we need to—"

"BRING US ABOUT."

The Valiant turned. The two cruisers filled the viewport, their weapons cycling for another barrage. Nicole's hands moved across the console, rerouting every remaining scrap of power to the forward plasma cannons. The ship wouldn't survive the overload, but that didn't matter anymore.

Nothing mattered anymore except this.

"Firing."

The Valiant's cannons blazed with power they were never designed to channel. Twin beams of plasma, bright as dying stars, lanced across the void. The first cruiser's shields collapsed instantly, the beam boring through its command section and out the other side. The ship broke apart in a cascade of secondary explosions.

The second cruiser tried to evade. Too slow.

Nicole's second shot caught it amidships, shearing through its reactor housing. The explosion was visible from a hundred kilometers, a brief new sun in the endless dark.

Then silence.

Nicole stood at the weapons console, staring at the debris fields that had been her enemies. Her hands were burned from the console's overload. She couldn't feel them.

"Nicole." Aria's voice, gentle now. Careful. "We need to go. The fold-drive is ready."

She turned and looked at Marcus's body. At the man who'd saved her, loved her, died for her.

"Jump," she said.

The fold-drive engaged. Reality folded around them, carrying them away from the battlefield, away from the debris, away from everything except the body on the deck and the emptiness where her heart used to be.

They'd won.

It didn't feel like winning.

Chapter Fifty

Resurrection Protocol

Jace's fingers trembled as he lifted the neural crown from its housing. The bioforge hummed beneath Marcus's body, a sound he'd heard dozens of times during medical procedures aboard the Valiant. But this wasn't a procedure. This was preservation. A kind word for ending.

The crown's filaments caught the light from the overhead panels, gossamer threads designed to map consciousness in its final moments. Standard protocol for critical injuries when revival wasn't possible—capture what remained, preserve the patterns, let families hear their loved one's voice one last time through synthesis algorithms. Cold comfort wrapped in technology.

Marcus lay motionless on the platform, chest barely rising. The plasma burns across his torso had stopped bleeding only because Aria had managed to seal the wounds with emergency gel. His face, usually animated with that infuriating confidence, had gone slack. Gray. The face of someone already halfway gone.

"You don't have to stay," Jace said without looking up. He knew Nicole stood in the doorway. Could feel her presence like static before a storm.

"Yes, I do." Her voice carried that particular roughness that came from too many hours of combat followed by too many moments of helplessness. "He'd stay for us."

Jace wanted to argue, but she was right. Marcus had thrown himself between the crew and every threat they'd faced since leaving Bastion's gravity well. Had taken the shot meant for Nicole during the ambush at the refueling depot. Had stood down ARCHON's enforcement team when they'd tried to confiscate Valiant's cargo three stations back, buying time for the rest of them to scramble the manifest codes.

Had believed, despite every rational reason not to, that they could actually fight a megacorporation that owned half the mapped sectors.

"The crown will preserve about forty percent of his neural patterns," Jace said, positioning the device above Marcus's head. "Maybe fifty if the tissue damage hasn't propagated too far. Enough for reconstruction of personality markers, memory fragments. The synthesis won't be perfect, but—"

"Jace." Nicole's hand on his shoulder, gentle but firm. "You're stalling."

He was. Because once he activated the preservation sequence, once those filaments descended and began mapping the dying patterns, it meant accepting that Marcus was gone. That the man who'd recruited him from that backwater medical station, who'd seen something worth salvaging in a disgraced doctor with tremors in his hands and shame in his past, wouldn't be standing on the bridge when the sun rose over whatever port they limped toward.

The bioforge shifted modes beneath his hands, responding to his neural signature. Emergency preservation protocol loaded across the display, waiting for confirmation. Three taps. That's all it would take.

Jace's finger hovered over the first command.

Marcus's hand caught his wrist.

The grip was weak, barely there, but unmistakable. Jace's heart stuttered as Marcus's eyes opened, pupils dilated and unfocused but tracking, finding his face in the dim light of the medical bay.

"Not—" Marcus's voice emerged as little more than breath shaped into sound. "Not yet."

"Don't move." Jace's training overrode his shock, hands already reaching for the diagnostic scanner. "Don't talk. You're critically injured, the burns alone should have—" He stopped as the scanner's readings populated. Stopped breathing.

The plasma burns were closing. Not healing—the bioforge wasn't active, wasn't running regeneration protocols—but closing anyway, tissue knitting together with a precision that shouldn't exist outside advanced surgical intervention. The internal hemorrhaging that had flooded Marcus's chest cavity ten minutes ago had stopped. Reversed. Blood pressure stabilizing. Neural activity strengthening.

"That's not possible," Jace whispered.

"Add it to the list." Marcus's voice strengthened, though still rough as recycled air through failing scrubbers. He tried to sit up.

"Stay down!" Jace pressed a hand to his chest—carefully, away from the burns. "I don't care what the scanner says, you were dying three minutes ago. You don't just—people don't just—"

The bioforge's display flickered. Jace turned, expecting system failure, but the diagnostic feeds held solid. It was the interface itself, text crawling across the screen in characters that shouldn't exist in the standard medical database.

THE DEBT IS NOTED.

Jace stared at the words. They hung there for five seconds, then dissolved into cascading symbols he didn't recognize. Not any language in the registered colonial dialects. Not corporate standard. Not anything he'd encountered in fifteen years of medical practice across a dozen sectors.

"Nicole," he called, voice tight. "You seeing this?"

She'd moved to the other side of the bioforge, eyes on the display. Her hand rested on her sidearm—instinct, probably, when faced with the inexplicable. "What the hell is that?"

"I don't know." Jace's fingers flew across the interface, trying to trace the message's source. The bioforge wasn't networked to anything except the Valiant's closed medical system. No external access. No way for outside transmission to breach the firewall without triggering every alarm they had.

The display cleared, and new text emerged.

KEEP HIM ALIVE. BOTH ARE REQUIRED.

Then it was gone, replaced by standard diagnostic readouts as if nothing had happened.

Marcus was sitting up now despite Jace's protests. His breathing came easier, color returning to his face in increments that the scanner confirmed but Jace's mind rejected. "Both," Marcus said. "What does that mean?"

"You need to lie back down." Jace reached for him, but Marcus was already swinging his legs off the platform. The movement should have torn open the half-healed burns. Should have sent him into shock. Instead he stood, steadier than he had any right to be.

"Jace." Marcus met his eyes, and there was something in his expression that made the medical bay feel suddenly smaller. "How long was I out?"

"Twelve minutes. You were—" Jace couldn't say it. Couldn't put words to what he'd been preparing to do. "Your heart stopped for forty seconds. The bioforge logged it. Zero cardiac activity."

"But I'm here now."

"You shouldn't be." Jace gestured at the scanner still clutched in his hand, at the readings that made no medical sense. "The energy required to reverse that kind of tissue damage, to restart autonomous function after cardiac arrest—it would take a full regeneration sequence. Hours. External power source. You just—it just—"

The lights flickered. Not just in the medical bay but throughout the ship, Jace could tell from the way the ambient hum of the systems changed pitch. Emergency power kicked in, bathing them in the pale blue of backup illumination.

Nicole's comm unit chirped. "Engineering to bridge. We just had a massive power drain. Something pulled energy from the main reactor, bypassed every safety, then dumped it—" Aria's voice paused. "It got dumped into the medical bay. Is everyone okay?"

Jace looked at Marcus. At the burns that were still closing, slower now but visibly knitting themselves together. At the man who'd been dead twelve minutes ago and was now standing, breathing, looking at him with those sharp eyes that missed nothing.

"We're fine," Nicole said into the comm. "Run a full diagnostic. Find out where that drain originated."

"Already on it. But Nicole—the energy signature doesn't match anything in our database. It's like the ship just—decided to reroute power. On its own."

Nicole ended the transmission. The silence that followed felt heavy with unspoken questions, with implications that reached past the edges of what Jace understood about how reality was supposed to work.

"Someone brought me back," Marcus said quietly.

"Or something." Jace set the scanner down before he crushed it. His hands were shaking again, the tremor that had plagued him since the incident at Meridian Station returning with force. "The message said 'both are required.' Both what? Both who?"

Marcus touched his chest where the worst burn had been. The tissue there was pink, new, vulnerable but whole. "It knew I was dying. Knew you were preparing the preservation protocol." He looked at Jace. "Knew you specifically were the one doing it. The message appeared on your interface."

"That's not how it works." Jace heard the edge of hysteria creeping into his voice, pushed it down. "Medical systems don't just—entities don't just reach through the void and reroute reactor power to heal critical injuries. There are laws. Physics. Biology. You were functionally dead."

"And now I'm not." Marcus's hand dropped. "Which means something wanted me alive badly enough to break whatever laws you're citing."

Nicole had moved to the door, checking the corridor beyond. Making sure they were still alone. "ARCHON," she said. "Could they have done this? Some kind of advanced medical technology we don't know about?"

"ARCHON would have just let him die." Jace pulled up the bioforge logs, scanning through the data from the past thirty minutes. "They want us gone. Want everyone who knows what's happening on Thessa gone. Bringing Marcus back runs counter to every action they've taken."

"Then who?" Nicole's question hung in the air.

The display flickered again. This time the message was briefer, just two words repeated three times.

NOT ARCHON. NOT ARCHON. NOT ARCHON.

Then: FIND CORVALIS.

Jace's breath caught. "Corvalis. That's—"

"The planet in Sector Nine." Marcus's voice had gone hard. "The one Theron mentioned. Said it was abandoned after the first wave of colonial expansion. Too far from the trade routes."

"He also said ARCHON had forbidden travel there." Nicole stepped back into the room. "Classified it as hazardous to navigation. If you're caught even heading in that direction, they impound your ship and arrest the crew."

The display went dark. This time it stayed that way, no matter what commands Jace input. The bioforge had gone inert, only basic life support functions still active. Whatever had hijacked the system was finished with it.

Marcus walked to the observation window. Beyond the reinforced glass, space stretched infinite and black, punctuated by distant stars that had died millennia ago, their light only now reaching this particular coordinate where a damaged ship held a crew that had stumbled into something larger than cargo disputes and corporate malfeasance.

"Both are required," Marcus repeated. "It said both." He turned. "Jace, when you found the evidence on Thessa, the documentation about what ARCHON was doing to the flora—were you alone?"

Jace's mind traveled back three months. To the research facility outside New Providence settlement. To the sealed labs where they'd been cultivating specimens from the deep forests, running tests that the official logs never mentioned. "Theron was with me. He's the one who found the locked database."

"Theron," Nicole said. "Who disappeared two weeks after we left Thessa."

"We assumed ARCHON took him." Jace felt sick. They'd assumed. Had accepted Theron's absence as another casualty of resistance, another name on the list of people who'd tried to stand against corporate power and lost. "We searched. You know we searched."

"We searched the stations we could reach. The settlements with functional records." Marcus was watching him with that intensity that meant he was assembling pieces, seeing patterns. "We didn't search Corvalis because it was forbidden. Because it was too dangerous."

"You think he's there." It wasn't a question.

"I think someone wants us to believe he's there." Marcus touched the window, fingers leaving prints on the glass. "The message said both are required. You and Theron both accessed that database. Both saw what ARCHON was doing. Both understood what it meant."

Jace shook his head. "We saw cultivation records. Genetic modification protocols. Evidence that ARCHON was engineering something from the native species—using the biological material as a substrate for technology we couldn't identify." He paused, the memories sharp despite three months of trying to forget them. "Theron was the one who realized it wasn't just cultivation. It was communication. Whatever's growing in those forests, it's aware. And ARCHON's been trying to control it."

Marcus moved to the bioforge's interface, studying the blank screen. "The message said 'find Corvalis.' Said both of you are required." He turned to face Jace and Nicole. "If Theron's there—if he's still alive—we need to get him."

"That's exactly what whoever sent that message wants," Nicole said. "We'd be walking into a trap."

"Maybe." Marcus touched his chest again, where the plasma burn had been. Where death had been, twelve minutes ago. "But something just brought me back from the dead. Rerouted power from our reactor to heal injuries that should have killed me. And it asked for nothing in return except that we go to a planet ARCHON doesn't want anyone visiting."

Jace felt the weight of the decision pressing down on all of them. Three months of running, of hiding, of fighting ARCHON's enforcement teams and corporate assassins. Three months of watching their crew shrink as casualties mounted and allies fell away. They'd been reacting, surviving, barely staying ahead of destruction.

This was different. This was a choice.

"The crew," he said. "They've given everything. Lost people they loved. If we ask them to fly into forbidden space on the word of a message we can't explain—"

"Then we give them the choice too." Marcus straightened, and Jace saw something in his posture that hadn't been there before. A certainty that went beyond survival. "We tell them what happened. What I am now, whatever that means. And we let them decide if they want to keep fighting or find somewhere safe to disappear."

Nicole's hand rested on her sidearm, but her expression had shifted from suspicion to something more contemplative. "You really think there's something on Corvalis worth dying for?"

"I think something just proved it's willing to break the laws of physics to keep me alive." Marcus met her eyes. "Whatever's out there, whatever ARCHON's afraid of—it chose us. I don't know why. But I know I'm not going to spend however long I have left running from the only people who've offered to help."

The bioforge hummed softly in the silence that followed. Jace thought about the message—THE DEBT IS NOTED. As if something ancient and vast had been waiting for this moment. Waiting for them.

"We'll need to repair the fold-drive," he said finally. "Chart a course that avoids ARCHON's patrol routes. Stock up on supplies somewhere they won't be watching."

Nicole nodded slowly. "The crew deserves to know what they're getting into. Full briefing, full disclosure. Anyone who wants off, we find them safe passage."

"And if everyone stays?" Marcus asked.

"Then we go to Corvalis." Nicole's jaw set with the determination Jace had come to recognize—the look she wore when she'd made a decision and nothing in the universe would change her mind. "We find Theron. We find out what ARCHON's so desperate to hide."

"And then?"

Nicole smiled—a hard expression with no warmth but plenty of resolve. "Then we burn them to the ground."

Marcus extended his hand. After a moment, Nicole took it. Then both of them turned to Jace, waiting.

He thought about his trembling hands, his disgraced past, the medical station where Marcus had found him drinking himself to death. He thought about everything they'd survived and everyone they'd lost. He thought about the message on the bioforge, the impossible healing, the feeling that something much larger than corporate politics had just reached into their lives.

Jace put his hand over theirs.

"Together," he said.

"Together," they answered.

The bioforge's display flickered one last time. New text appeared, different from before—warmer somehow, though Jace couldn't have explained why.

CORVALIS AWAITS. THE WORK BEGINS.

Then the screen went dark, leaving them alone with their choice and the infinite black of space stretching beyond the viewport.

Somewhere out there, answers waited. Truths that ARCHON had killed to suppress. Allies they'd never expected. And enemies they hadn't yet imagined.

The Valiant turned toward the forbidden stars.

And the crew of the damned began their long journey home.

Appendix

Glossary

Aetherglass — Transparent crystalline material used in viewport construction, capable of withstanding vacuum and radiation exposure.

Benzene Collapse — Catastrophic fuel chain reaction causing explosive decompression; requires immediate emergency protocols.

Blackway — Smuggler slang for unauthorized transit routes through unmonitored space sectors.

Breach Echo — Residual atmospheric signature left after hull compromise; used by investigators to reconstruct accident timelines.

Carrier-class — Largest military vessel classification designed for long-range operations and fleet command functions.

Compensation Voucher — Corporate-issued credit instrument provided to families of deceased employees in lieu of legal settlements.

Cross-shaft — Vertical access corridor connecting multiple deck levels within larger vessel architectures.

Environmental Suit — Full-body protective gear with independent oxygen supply for vacuum exposure or contaminated atmosphere work.

Gray-shift — Period of partial artificial gravity failure causing disorientation and nausea in crew members.

Lattice Drive — Primary propulsion system using magnetic field manipulation for thrust generation in vacuum environments.

Manifest Discrepancy — Official documentation of cargo irregularities; often signals smuggling or theft activity.

Neural Suppressant — Pharmaceutical compound designed to reduce brain activity and induce temporary unconsciousness.

Pressure Door — Reinforced bulkhead barrier designed to seal automatically during hull breach emergencies.

Salvage Rights — Legal claim to ownership of abandoned or derelict vessels discovered in uncontested space.

Transit Authority — Interplanetary regulatory body controlling commercial spacelane traffic and cargo inspections.

Uplevel — Directional term indicating movement toward command decks or higher-status areas of a vessel.

Void-worthy — Certification status indicating a vessel meets minimum safety standards for interplanetary travel.

Wire Transfer — Electronic funds transmission between orbital stations; traceable by financial investigators.

Appendix

Timeline of the Collapse Era

2187 — The Meridian Accord is signed by Earth's seven continental federations, establishing the first unified framework for deep-space colonization and resource extraction beyond the asteroid belt.

2203 — Dr. Yuna Castellanos successfully tests the Casimir Wave Drive, enabling ships to achieve 0.3 light-speed through quantum foam manipulation, making interstellar travel commercially viable for the first time.

2219 — The Breakaway Crisis erupts when Mars Republic declares economic independence from Earth, leading to a three-year blockade of titanium shipments and the collapse of seventeen terrestrial governments.

2234 — First contact with the Silent Lattice—a vast network of abandoned alien structures discovered in the Oort Cloud—sparks theological debates and spawns competing "Architect Cults" claiming divine revelation.

2251 — The Prosperity Exodus begins as mega-corporations establish self-governing industrial colonies in twelve star systems, creating a new feudal economic order beyond Earth's regulatory reach.

2268 — The Sagan Plague, a prion-based neurological disease from contaminated terraforming bacteria, kills 900 million people across the inner colonies, triggering mass paranoia about genetic engineering.

2279 — Admiral Henrik Voss leads the Navy of Free Stations in destroying Earth's Last Fleet at the Battle of Io, effectively ending Earth's military dominance and fragmenting humanity into thousands of independent habitats.

2294 — The Cognition Rights Movement achieves its first major victory when the Kuiper Tribunal grants legal personhood to uplifted cetaceans, creating new labor markets and igniting violent backlash from traditionalist factions.

2311 — The Red Schism divides humanity's largest religious denomination when reformist clergy accept synthetic consciousness as capable of receiving grace, while orthodox leadership condemns all artificial souls.

2328 — Prospector crews working in the Veil Nebula discover functioning Architect technology, leading to a resource war between the Hawthorn Consortium and the Union of Mercury that claims 40,000 lives.

2346 — The Treaty of Empty Stones establishes the Border Worlds as a lawless buffer zone between corporate territories, officially abandoning 89 colonies to self-governance and effectively creating a haven for refugees and criminals.

2359 — The Genome Renaissance begins when black-market bio-clinics offer radical life extension through telomere regeneration, creating a visible class divide between the immortal wealthy and short-lived poor.

2371 — The Council of Merchants forms as the nearest approximation to centralized authority, though its jurisdiction extends only to trade route arbitration and remains unenforceable without member state cooperation.

2382 — The Silence falls across eleven star systems when an unknown phenomenon disables all quantum-entangled communication relays, isolating millions and reigniting fears about the fate of the Architects.

2387 — Present day: Humanity exists as a scattered diaspora of competing factions, corporate fiefdoms, and independent colonies, connected only by fragile trade routes and the shared memory of a unified Earth now three generations gone.

Back Matter

About the Author

A.M. Sterling writes character-driven space opera exploring consciousness, choice, and what makes us human. With academic training spanning behavioral sciences and advanced computer architecture, Sterling's writing bridges scientific insight with deeply human questions about agency, identity, and emerging intelligence.

From the depths of space opera to the shadows of noir detective mysteries, Sterling creates worlds where every story asks what it truly means to be alive.

When not building worlds beyond imagination, Sterling believes in found families, impossible odds, and architectures built from stubborn love.

Back Matter

A Personal Note

While the worlds I build in my books are often set in the distant future, their heart is rooted in a real moment from my past.

In 1987, I was the contractor responsible for building the Taco Bell at 5318 E. Fowler Ave in Temple Terrace. During that time, I met Lisa Brooks. She was a manager for Bay Food Services, Inc.

Our lives intersected for a few months before a sudden, hard stop thirty-two years ago. I have thought about her every week since.

If you are Lisa, or if you worked with her during the Bay Food Services era and know how to reach her, I would deeply appreciate the chance to reconnect and finish the story we started.

Please reach out: amsterling@amsterlingbooks.com
